Higher-rank graphs and the graded K K -theory of Kumjian-Pask Algebras
Roozbeh Hazrat, Promit Mukherjee, David Pask, and Sujit Kumar Sardar

Abstract
. This paper lays out the foundations of graded K K -theory for Leavitt algebras associated with higher-rank graphs, also known as Kumjian–Pask algebras, establishing it as a potential tool for their classification. For a row-finite k k -graph Λ \Lambda without sources, we show that there exists a ℤ ⁡ [ ℤ k ] \mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}^{k}] -module isomorphism between the graded zeroth (integral) homology H 0 gr ​ ( 𝒢 Λ ) H_{0}^{\operatorname{gr}}(\mathcal{G}_{\Lambda}) of the infinite path groupoid 𝒢 Λ \mathcal{G}_{\Lambda} and the graded Grothendieck group K 0 gr ​ ( KP 𝗄 ⁡ ( Λ ) ) K_{0}^{\operatorname{gr}}(\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Lambda)) of the Kumjian-Pask algebra KP 𝗄 ⁡ ( Λ ) \operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Lambda) , which respects the positive cones (i.e., the talented monoids). We demonstrate that the k k -graph moves of in-splitting and sink deletion defined by Eckhardt et al. (Canad. J. Math. 2022) preserve the graded K K -theory of associated Kumjian-Pask algebras and produce algebras which are graded Morita equivalent, thus providing evidence that graded K K -theory may be an effective invariant for classifying certain Kumjian-Pask algebras. We also determine a natural sufficient condition regarding the fullness of the graded Grothendieck group functor. More precisely, for two row-finite k k -graphs Λ \Lambda and Ω \Omega without sources and with finite object sets, we obtain a sufficient criterion for lifting a pointed order-preserving ℤ ⁡ [ ℤ k ] \mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}^{k}] -module homomorphism between K 0 gr ​ ( KP 𝗄 ⁡ ( Λ ) ) K_{0}^{\operatorname{gr}}(\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Lambda)) and K 0 gr ​ ( KP 𝗄 ⁡ ( Ω ) ) K_{0}^{\operatorname{gr}}(\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Omega)) to a unital graded ring homomorphism between KP 𝗄 ⁡ ( Λ ) \operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Lambda) and KP 𝗄 ⁡ ( Ω ) \operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Omega) . For this we adapt, in the setting of k k -graphs, the bridging bimodule technique recently introduced by Abrams, Ruiz and Tomforde (Algebr. Represent. Theory 2024).



2020 Mathematics Subject Classification
16W50, 19A49, 19D55, 22A22, 37B10

1.
Introduction

The triangle of symbolic dynamics, operator algebras, and noncommutative algebras has seen a notable surge of activity in recent years. The classical dynamical notions such as conjugacy, shift equivalence and flow equivalence can be captured by both analytical and discrete combinatorial algebras (here, graph
C^{*}
-algebras
[
35
]
and Leavitt path algebras
[
1
,
7
]
) arising from underlying dynamics. On the other hand, conjecturally, these algebras can be classified by certain variants of
K
-theory, thus enabling one to interpret the dynamical behaviour in algebraic terms, and vice versa
[
19
,
23
]
.

To be precise, for a finite graph
E
with no sink, the graded Grothendieck group
K_{0}^{\operatorname{gr}}
of the Leavitt path algebra
L_{\mathsf{k}}(E)
, coincides with the equivariant
K
-theory
K_{0}^{\mathbb{T}}
of the graph
C^{*}
-algebra
C^{*}(E)
and they both coincide with Krieger’s dimension group of the shift of finite type associated to the adjacency matrix of the graph
E

[
28
,
37
]
. The long-standing Graded Classification Conjecture states that the pointed pre-ordered
\mathbb{Z}[x,x^{-1}]
-module
K_{0}^{\operatorname{gr}}
classifies the class of Leavitt path algebras (and similarly the graph
C^{*}
-algebras). A consequence of the conjecture is that, two shifts of finite type are eventually conjugate or, shift equivalent if and only if the associated Leavitt path algebras are graded Morita equivalent.

In an effort to settle the conjecture, it has been shown that the pointed
K_{0}^{\operatorname{gr}}
is a full functor for the category of Leavitt path algebras; namely, a pointed order-preserving
\mathbb{Z}[x,x^{-1}]
-module homomorphism
\phi:K_{0}^{\operatorname{gr}}(L_{\mathsf{k}}(E))\rightarrow K_{0}^{\operatorname{gr}}(L_{\mathsf{k}}(F))
lifts to a unital graded homomorphism
L_{\mathsf{k}}(E)\rightarrow L_{\mathsf{k}}(F)

[
9
,
46
]
. And that this functor is invariant under out- and in-splitting graph moves, which gives the graded Morita equivalent algebras
[
18
]
. We refer the readers to
[
2
,
6
,
9
,
10
,
11
,
12
,
13
,
17
,
22
,
46
,
47
]
for recent works on the Graded Classification Conjecture and
[
19
]
for a comprehensive survey.

This paper aims to initiate a study of graded
K
-theory in the context of a generalised class of Leavitt path algebras, namely those associated with higher-rank graphs (or,
k
-graphs); commonly referred to as Kumjian–Pask algebras
[
8
]
. Higher-rank graphs (see Definition
2.1
below) were introduced by Kumjian and the third named author in
[
34
]
to provide a combinatorial framework to higher-rank Cuntz-Krieger algebras
[
45
]
. Soon it appeared that the
C^{*}
-algebras of higher-rank graphs cover a broader class of
C^{*}
-algebras, including the well-known graph
C^{*}
-algebras. Moreover, the
C^{*}
-algebras and Kumjian-Pask algebras associated to higher-rank graphs include many examples which are not graph
C^{*}
-algebras and Leavitt path algebras (see
[
41
,
8
]
); also it is observed recently in
[
32
]
that there exists a finitely generated commutative refinement monoid which can not be realised as a graph monoid but can be realised as the monoid of a row-finite higher-rank graph. Higher-rank graphs, being naturally equipped with delicate categorical structure, often demands innovative and subtle ideas to establish results related to their algebras.

For a higher-rank
k
-graph
\Lambda
, and its associated Kumjian-Pask algebra
\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Lambda)
with coefficient in a field
\mathsf{k}
, one can describe the graded Grothendieck group
K_{0}^{\operatorname{gr}}(\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Lambda))
directly from the underlying
k
-graph
\Lambda
. The positive cone of this group coincides with the so-called
talented monoid
of
\Lambda
, studied in
[
32
]
.
Parallel to the theory of Leavitt path algebras, it is natural to ask whether this invariant captures similar structural or categorical properties in the context of Kumjian–Pask algebras.

As illustrated by Example
7.1
, the
2
-graphs arising from two different factorisation of the following bicoloured skeleton

.
v
e_{1}
e_{2}
f

give rise to two non-graded isomorphic Kumjian-Pask algebras:
L_{\mathsf{k}}(1,2)[t^{-1},t]
, the Laurent polynomial algebra with coefficient from the Leavitt path algebra
L_{\mathsf{k}}(1,2)
of two petal rose
R_{2}
, where
t
commutes with
L_{\mathsf{k}}(1,2)
and
L_{\mathsf{k}}(1,2)[t^{-1},t;\varphi]
, the skew Laurent polynomial algebra where
\varphi:L_{\mathsf{k}}(1,2)\rightarrow L_{\mathsf{k}}(1,2)
is the ring automorphism switching the petals. However, one can observe that these algebras have the same graded Grothendieck group
\mathbb{Z}[1/2]
.

This observation naturally suggests the following problem.

Problem 1.1
.

Characterise the class of
k
-graphs for which the existence of (pointed) order-preserving
\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}^{k}]
-module isomorphism between the graded Grothendieck groups imply that the respective Kumjian-Pask algebras are graded (isomorphic) Morita equivalent.

One should note that, for
1
-graphs, Problem
1.1
, is precisely the Graded Classification Conjecture for Leavitt path algebras. Although the above problem is yet unsolved, it appears, at least from the discrepancy mentioned above, that the
K
-theoretic classification of Kumjian-Pask algebras of higher-rank graphs might be much more involved than that of Leavitt path algebras. This motivates us to investigate how far the graded Grothendieck group can capture the substantial information about this algebra. In this direction, we here accomplish three objectives, each of which contributes to our quest in spite of being independent in their treatment.

We start by showing that the newly developed moves on
k
-graphs
[
21
]
, namely, in-splitting and sink deletion produce Kumjian-Pask algebras that are graded Morita equivalent (see Propositions
3.1
&
3.3
). We further directly establish in Theorems
3.2
and
3.4
that the graded
K
-theories of these algebras remain isomorphic under the said moves. As a continuation of
[
32
]
, this gives further evidence that talented monoid may be an effective invariant for classifying certain Kumjian-Pask algebras up to graded Morita equivalence. It is interesting to note that the moves of in- and out-splitting appear extensively in the study of dynamical systems, especially shifts of finite type. It is known that two-dimensional shifts of finite type are best modelled by using textile systems (see
[
33
]
) introduced by Nasu
[
40
]
. However, finite
2
-graphs can also be used to form certain shifts of finite type. The connections between
2
-graphs in-splitting (
[
21
]
) and textile in-splitting (introduced by Johnson and Madden
[
33
]
) have been explored very recently in
[
14
]
, where it is shown that every
2
-graph in-splitting can be broken down into multiple textile in-splittings and inversions; consequently
2
-graph in-splitting gives conjugacy of the associated one-sided two-dimensional shifts of finite type. In one-dimension, conjugacy (eventual conjugacy) of edge shifts (essentially shifts of finite type) are characterized by strong shift equivalence (shift equivalence) of the corresponding adjacency matrices. In Remark
5.5
we observe that the vertex matrices of a row-finite
k
-graph
\Lambda
and its in-split
\Lambda_{I}
are naturally connected via a certain matrix relation which looks like a higher-dimensional analogue of shift equivalence in the class of non-negative integral square matrices. This together with the preceding discussion gives some hope to derive in future, some possible matrix conditions describing conjugacy of two-dimensional shifts of finite type, as desired at the end of
[
33
]
.

Our second objective is to realise the graded
K
-theory of Kumjian-Pask algebra as the graded homology
[
30
]
of the infinite path groupoid (see §
2.2
below) associated with the underlying
k
-graph. The seminal work of Renault
[
44
]
showcased topological groupoid as a potential tool to model operator algebras, especially
C^{*}
-algebras. In the past two decades, through several works (
[
3
,
4
,
5
,
15
,
16
,
30
,
34
,
38
,
39
]
and many more) it becomes prominent that groupoids play the role of a crucial junction point connecting combinatorial dynamical systems, their
C^{*}
-algebras and their algebraic counterparts. The homology theory of étale groupoids, introduced by Crainic and Moerdijk
[
20
]
, is significant in studying groupoid
C^{*}
-algebras
[
38
,
39
]
. In this context, Matui proved that for any étale groupoid
\mathcal{G}
arising from a one-sided, one-dimensional shift of finite type,
H_{0}(\mathcal{G})\cong K_{0}(C_{r}^{*}(\mathcal{G}))
(see
[
38
, Theorem 4.14]
). As the majority of groupoids can be naturally graded, an exciting program is to relate graded combinatorial invariants for graded groupoids (e.g., graded homology) to their Steinberg and
C^{*}
-algebraic counterparts (here graded
K
-theory). In line with this program, we here prove, in two different ways (see Theorem
4.6
and Remark
4.7
), that there is an order-preserving
\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}^{k}]
-module isomorphism between the graded zeroth homology
H_{0}^{\operatorname{gr}}(\mathcal{G}_{\Lambda})
of the infinite path groupoid
\mathcal{G}_{\Lambda}
of a row-finite
k
-graph
\Lambda
without sources and the graded
K
-theory of the corresponding Kumjian-Pask algebra
\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Lambda)
. This extends
[
30
, Theorem 6.6]
to higher dimensions. Moreover, Theorem
4.6
together with Remark
5.2
and
[
34
, Corollary 3.5
(i)
]
enables us to obtain the following relationships:

H_{0}(\mathcal{G}_{\Lambda\times_{d}\mathbb{Z}^{k}})\cong H_{0}(\mathcal{G}_{\Lambda}\times_{\tilde{d}}\mathbb{Z}^{k})=H_{0}^{\operatorname{gr}}(\mathcal{G}_{\Lambda})\cong K_{0}^{\operatorname{gr}}(\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Lambda))\cong K_{0}(C^{*}(\Lambda\times_{d}\mathbb{Z}^{k}))\cong K_{0}(C_{r}^{*}(\mathcal{G}_{\Lambda\times_{d}\mathbb{Z}^{k}})),

extending above-mentioned Matui’s result to skew-product
k
-graph groupoids which possibly do not arise from shifts of finite type.

The third and final goal of this paper is to seek an answer to the following question:

Question 1.2
.

Given two row-finite
k
-graphs
\Lambda
and
\Omega
without sources and with finite vertex sets, when one can lift a pointed order-preserving
\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}^{k}]
-module homomorphism between
K_{0}^{\operatorname{gr}}(\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Lambda))
and
K_{0}^{\operatorname{gr}}(\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Omega))
to a unital graded ring homomorphism between
\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Lambda)
and
\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Omega)
?

The above question is framed in categorical terms in Question
6.1
and is indeed related to the fullness of the graded Grothendieck group functor. The analogue of the above problem on the level of Leavitt path algebras has been solved independently by several authors viz., Vaš
[
46
]
, Arnone
[
9
]
and Brix et al.
[
13
]
. The answer is that we can always lift a homomorphism between graded
K
-theories of Leavitt path algebras to a graded algebra homomorphism between the underlying algebras. The authors in
[
13
]
used the idea of
bridging bimodule
recently invented by Abrams, Ruiz and Tomforde
[
2
]
, to prove the fullness of the functor
K_{0}^{\operatorname{gr}}
. Motivated by this approach, in order to find an answer to the lifting question, we develop machinery to adopt the bridging bimodule technique in our higher-rank setting. However, we face some technical hurdles. In a higher-rank graph, a path can be made of edges having different degrees and this simple fact can cause a serious compatibility issue in the process of forming a well-defined conjugacy between two
k
-graphs
\Lambda
and
\Omega
especially since any such conjugacy should respect the internal factorization rules of
\Lambda
and
\Omega
at the time of intertwining the
\mathsf{k}\Lambda^{0}-\mathsf{k}\Lambda^{0}
-bimodule
\mathsf{k}\Lambda^{\mathbf{n}}
with the
\mathsf{k}\Omega^{0}-\mathsf{k}\Omega^{0}
-bimodule
\mathsf{k}\Omega^{\mathbf{n}}
(see diagram (
11
) of Lemma
6.9
). To overcome this, we work in the level of polymorphisms and introduce a certain condition on matrices
R
in
\mathbb{M}_{\Lambda^{0}\times\Omega^{0}}(\mathbb{N})
satisfying
A_{\mathbf{e}_{i}}R=RB_{\mathbf{e}_{i}}
for all
i=1,2,...,k
, which is actually a coherence condition taking care of compatibility between the mutual commutativity of the polymorphism of
R
with the coordinate polymorphisms of
\Lambda
,
\Omega
and the individual factorization rules of
\Lambda
and
\Omega
. In Definition
6.5
, we call a matrix
R
satisfying such coherence condition, a
bridging
matrix. We also give enough justification, in Remarks
6.6
, about the significance of the bridging criterion and also describe its similarity with the associativity condition required to form a
k
-graph out of a
k
-coloured skeleton (see
[
26
, Theorem 4.4]
). Though the bridging criterion is vacuously satisfied in
1
-dimension, it is not at all obvious while moving to higher-dimensions; Examples
6.7
and
6.8
justify this for
k=2
.

Coming back to the lifting question, in Lemma
5.3

(ii)
, we show that any pointed order-preserving
\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}^{k}]
-module homomorphism
\mathfrak{h}:K_{0}^{\operatorname{gr}}(\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Lambda))\longrightarrow K_{0}^{\operatorname{gr}}(\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Omega))
gives rise to a matrix
R\in\mathbb{M}_{\Lambda^{0}\times\Omega^{0}}(\mathbb{N})
whose polymorphism
E_{R}
makes the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Lambda^{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Lambda^{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E_{\mathbf{e}_{i}}^{\Omega}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E_{R}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E_{R}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E_{\mathbf{e}_{i}}^{\Lambda}}

commute in the category of polymorphisms for each
i=1,2,...,k
, where
E_{\mathbf{e}_{i}}^{\Lambda}
,
E_{\mathbf{e}_{i}}^{\Omega}
are the
i^{\text{th}}
-coordinate graphs of
\Lambda
and
\Omega
respectively (see §
6
for more details). If, in addition,
R
is a bridging matrix then by a step-by-step construction of a bridging bimodule
M(R)
(a suitable graded
\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Lambda)-\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Omega)
-bimodule), we finally show in Theorem
6.12
that there exists a graded homomorphism
\psi:\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Lambda)\longrightarrow\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Omega)
such that
K_{0}^{\operatorname{gr}}(\psi)=\mathfrak{h}
. In this way, we establish the bridging criterion as a sufficient condition for lifting homomorphism in the level of graded
K
-theories to the level of Kumjian-Pask algebras.

Throughout this paper, all our
k
-graphs are row-finite without sources.

The paper is structured as follows. After this introductory section, we recall in §
2
, the very basic concepts related to higher-rank graphs and Kumjian-Pask algebras. This includes fundamental examples of
k
-graphs (Examples
2.2
,
2.3
,
2.4
), the description of the infinite path groupoid and most importantly a brief understanding about the graded
K
-groups of Kumjian-Pask algebras. The preliminary section is followed by §
3
–
6
, where we collect the main findings of the paper. In §
3
, we describe the
k
-graph moves of in-splitting and sink-deletion separately in two subsections (see §
3.1
and §
3.2
). We obtain Theorems
3.2
and
3.4
which states that the talented monoid of a
k
-graph remains
\mathbb{Z}^{k}
-isomorphic under each of these moves. §
4
starts with a brief introduction to the homology theory of étale groupoids as well as the graded homology of a
\Gamma
-graded groupoid,
\Gamma
being a discrete abelian group. In the rest of this section, we focus on the path groupoid of a
k
-graph
\Lambda
and prove necessary results (Lemma
4.4
, Proposition
4.5
) leading to Theorem
4.6
which exhibits a desired isomorphism between
H_{0}^{\operatorname{gr}}(\mathcal{G}_{\Lambda})
and
K_{0}^{\operatorname{gr}}(\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Lambda))
. We also provide some interesting connections between (graded) homology of
\mathcal{G}_{\Lambda}
and that of
\mathcal{G}_{f^{*}(\Lambda)}
where
f^{*}(\Lambda)
is the pullback of
\Lambda
with respect to a surjective monoid homomorphism
f:\mathbb{N}^{\ell}\longrightarrow\mathbb{N}^{k}
. §
5
is dedicated to the study of the dimension group associated with a
k
-graph
\Lambda
which is actually another realisation of the graded
K
-theory. The main result of this section is Theorem
5.4
which describes the existence of an isomorphism between graded Grothendieck groups of Kumjian-Pask algebras via a specific matrix condition connecting the vertex matrices of the underlying
k
-graphs. In §
6
, we take up the lifting question (Question
6.1
) and through the introduction of bridging matrix, determine a natural sufficient condition in Theorem
6.12
, under which the lifting can be done. We conclude the paper in §
7
discussing a few problems which have not been explored here and might be interesting for further research.

2.
Basic concepts

2.1.
Higher-rank graphs and Kumjian-Pask algebras

For any positive integer
k
,
\mathbb{N}^{k}
stands for the commutative monoid of
k
-tuples of non-negative integers under coordinatewise addition. It is simultaneously viewed as a small category with one object.

Definition 2.1
.

A
higher-rank graph
(also called a
k
-graph
) is a countable small category
\Lambda
together with a functor
d:\Lambda\longrightarrow\mathbb{N}^{k}
(called the
degree functor
) which satisfies the
unique factorization property
: if
\lambda\in\Lambda
and
d(\lambda)=\mathbf{n}+\mathbf{m}\in\mathbb{N}^{k}
then there exist unique
\alpha,\beta\in\Lambda
such that
d(\lambda)=\mathbf{n},d(\mu)=\mathbf{m}
and
\lambda=\alpha\beta
.

The
range
and
source
of any morphism
\lambda\in\Lambda
are usually denoted as
r(\lambda)
and
s(\lambda)
. For any
\mathbf{n}\in\mathbb{N}^{k}
and
u,v\in\operatorname{Obj}(\Lambda)
we denote
\Lambda^{\mathbf{n}}:=d^{-1}(\mathbf{n})
,
u\Lambda^{\mathbf{n}}:=r^{-1}(u)\cap\Lambda^{\mathbf{n}}
,
\Lambda^{\mathbf{n}}v:=\Lambda^{\mathbf{n}}\cap s^{-1}(v)
and
u\Lambda^{\mathbf{n}}v:=u\Lambda^{\mathbf{n}}\cap\Lambda^{\mathbf{n}}v
. Also, we write
\Lambda^{\mathbf{1}}=\displaystyle{\bigcup_{i=1}^{k}}~\Lambda^{\mathbf{e}_{i}}
and
v\Lambda^{\mathbf{1}}=\displaystyle{\bigcup_{i=1}^{k}}~v\Lambda^{\mathbf{e}_{i}}
, where
\{\mathbf{e}_{i}~|~i=1,\ldots,k\}
is the canonical basis for
\mathbb{N}^{k}
. Using the factorization property, one can show that
\operatorname{Obj}(\Lambda)=\Lambda^{0}
. In this paper, we confine ourselves to
row-finite
k
-graphs without sources
, i.e.,
k
-graphs
\Lambda
such that
0<|v\Lambda^{\mathbf{n}}|<\infty
for all
v\in\Lambda^{0}
and
\mathbf{n}\in\mathbb{N}^{k}
. Let
\Lambda
be any
k
-graph and
\lambda\in\Lambda
. Suppose
\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n}\in\mathbb{N}^{k}
are such that
0\leq\mathbf{m}\leq\mathbf{n}\leq d(\lambda)
. By applying the unique factorization, we have unique paths
\mu_{1},\mu_{2},\mu_{3}\in\Lambda
such that

d(\mu_{1})=\mathbf{m},d(\mu_{2})=\mathbf{n}-\mathbf{m},d(\mu_{3})=d(\lambda)-\mathbf{n}
and
\lambda=\mu_{1}\mu_{2}\mu_{3}
.

We denote the paths
\mu_{1},\mu_{2}
and
\mu_{3}
by
\lambda(0,\mathbf{m}),\lambda(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})
and
\lambda(\mathbf{n},d(\lambda))
respectively. Let
\lambda,\mu\in\Lambda
. A path
\tau\in\Lambda
is called a
minimal common extension
of
\lambda
and
\mu
if

d(\tau)=d(\lambda)\vee d(\mu),

\tau(0,d(\lambda))=\lambda
and
\tau(0,d(\mu))=\mu
.

Define

\operatorname{MCE}(\lambda,\mu):=\{\tau~|~\tau
is a minimal common extension of
\lambda,\mu\}
.

To each
k
-graph
\Lambda
, we can associate a (edge) coloured directed graph
G_{\Lambda}
called the
1
-skeleton
of
\Lambda
. Define the set of vertices
G_{\Lambda}^{0}:=\Lambda^{0}
and set of edges
G_{\Lambda}^{\mathbf{1}}:=\Lambda^{\mathbf{1}}
. Range and source maps are the restrictions of
r
and
s
to
\Lambda^{\mathbf{1}}
. Fix
k
-different colours, each of which corresponds to a generator of
\mathbb{N}^{k}
. If
\lambda\in G_{\Lambda}^{\mathbf{1}}
has degree
\mathbf{e}_{i}
and
c_{i}
is the colour corresponding to
\mathbf{e}_{i}
then we colour the edge
\lambda
by
c_{i}
. On the other hand, given a
k
-coloured directed graph
G
one can obtain a
k
-graph by defining, on the path category
G^{*}
, an equivalence relation
\sim
satisfying some specific properties (see
[
26
, Theorems 4.4 and 4.5]
) which encode the factorisation property. This correspondence between
k
-graphs and
k
-coloured directed graphs with certain equivalence relations gives a general mechanism to perform constructions on
k
-graphs and to establish results by focusing on the
1
-skeleton.

Example 2.2
.

(
The
k
-dimensional grid graph
) Let
k
be any positive integer and
\mathbf{n}\in\mathbb{N}^{k}
. Denote by
\mathbf{n}^{\downarrow}
, the principal down-set of
\mathbf{n}
in the poset
\mathbb{N}^{k}
(with respect to coordinatewise ordering). Then
\mathbf{n}^{\downarrow}
is also a poset. Let
\Omega_{k,\mathbf{n}}
be the corresponding poset category i.e.,

\Omega_{k,\mathbf{n}}:=\{(\mathbf{p},\mathbf{q})\in\mathbf{n}^{\downarrow}\times\mathbf{n}^{\downarrow}~|~\mathbf{p}\leq\mathbf{q}\},

with
r((\mathbf{p},\mathbf{q}))=\mathbf{n}
,
s((\mathbf{p},\mathbf{q}))=\mathbf{q}
and the composition is defined as

(\mathbf{p},\mathbf{q})(\mathbf{q},\mathbf{t}):=(\mathbf{p},\mathbf{t}).

Then
\Omega_{k,\mathbf{n}}
can be viewed as a
k
-graph with respect to the degree functor
d_{\mathbf{n}}:\Omega_{k,\mathbf{n}}\longrightarrow\mathbb{N}^{k}
;
d_{\mathbf{n}}((\mathbf{p},\mathbf{q})):=\mathbf{q}-\mathbf{p}
. Instead of choosing a principal down-set, if we consider the poset
\mathbb{N}^{k}
as a whole, then the corresponding poset category, denoted as
\Omega_{k}
, is also a
k
-graph with the degree functor
d:\Omega_{k}\longrightarrow\mathbb{N}^{k}
defined by
d((\mathbf{p},\mathbf{q})):=\mathbf{q}-\mathbf{p}
for all
\mathbf{p},\mathbf{q}\in\mathbb{N}^{k}
with
\mathbf{p}\leq\mathbf{q}
.

Below we show the
1
-skeleton of
\Omega_{3,(2,2,2)}
. For the three basic
3
-tuples
\mathbf{e}_{1},\mathbf{e}_{2}
and
\mathbf{e}_{3}
we use the colours blue (solid), green (dotted) and red (dashed) respectively.

(0,0,0)
(2,2,2)

Example 2.3
.

(
Pullback of a
k
-graph
) Suppose we have a
k
-graph
\Lambda
with degree functor
d
. Let
\ell
be any positive integer. A common way to produce an
\ell
-graph out of
\Lambda
is to consider a monoid homomorphism
f:\mathbb{N}^{\ell}\longrightarrow\mathbb{N}^{k}
. Set

f^{*}(\Lambda):=\{(\lambda,\mathbf{n})~|~d(\lambda)=f(\mathbf{n})\},

s((\lambda,\mathbf{n})):=(s(\lambda),0),~~r((\lambda,\mathbf{n})):=(r(\lambda),0),~~d^{*}((\lambda,\mathbf{n})):=\mathbf{n}.

Define a composition as

(\lambda,\mathbf{n})(\mu,\mathbf{m}):=(\lambda\mu,\mathbf{n}+\mathbf{m}).

Then
(f^{*}(\Lambda),d^{*})
is an
\ell
-graph, which is often called the
pullback of
\Lambda
along
f
(see
[
34
, Definition 1.9]
).

Pulling back is a standard procedure to transit between higher-rank graphs of different dimensions. Below we provide two simple instances; in each case we only draw the skeletons of the
k
-graphs and mention the relevant monoid homomorphism, along which the pullback is performed.

(i)

1
-graph
\rightsquigarrow

2
-graph:

.
.
R_{1}
f^{*}(R_{1})
f:\mathbb{N}^{2}\longrightarrow\mathbb{N}
f((x,y)):=x+y

(ii)

2
-graph
\rightsquigarrow

1
-graphs:

.
h_{1}
h_{2}
f
.
.
\iota_{2}:\mathbb{N}\longrightarrow\mathbb{N}^{2}
\iota_{2}(x):=(0,x)
\iota_{1}:\mathbb{N}\longrightarrow\mathbb{N}^{2}
\iota_{1}(x):=(x,0)
\Lambda
\iota_{2}^{*}(\Lambda)
\iota_{1}^{*}(\Lambda)

The edges
h_{1},h_{2}
in
\Lambda
have degree
(0,1)
and the edge
f
has degree
(1,0)
. The factorizations of paths of degree
(1,1)
are given as
h_{1}f=fh_{1}
,
h_{2}f=fh_{2}
.

In Example
2.3

(ii)
, the two directed graphs
\iota_{1}^{*}(\Lambda)
and
\iota_{2}^{*}(\Lambda)
are known as the coordinate graphs of the
2
-graph
\Lambda
. More generally, given a
k
-graph
\Lambda
, the
i^{\text{th}}
coordinate graph
associated with
\Lambda
is a directed graph
E_{\mathbf{e}_{i}}^{\Lambda}
with set of vertices
\Lambda^{0}
, set of edges
\Lambda^{\mathbf{e}_{i}}
, the range and source maps are the restrictions of
r,s
to
\Lambda^{\mathbf{e}_{i}}
.

Example 2.4
.

(
Skew-product
k
-graph
) Let
\Gamma
be a discrete abelian group and
\Lambda
a
k
-graph. Given a functor
c:\Lambda\to\Gamma
, we may form the
skew-product
k
-graph

\Lambda\times_{c}\Gamma
which is the set
\Lambda\times\Gamma
endowed with structure maps given by
r((\lambda,\gamma)):=(r(\lambda),\gamma)
,
s((\lambda,\gamma)):=(s(\lambda),c(\lambda)+\gamma)
, product
(\lambda,\gamma)(\mu,c(\lambda)+\gamma):=(\lambda\mu,\gamma)
, and
d(\lambda,\gamma):=d(\lambda)
(see
[
34
, Definition 5.1]
). There is a natural
\Gamma
-action
\alpha
on
\Lambda\times_{c}\Gamma
given by
\alpha_{\kappa}(\lambda,\gamma):=(\lambda,\kappa+\gamma)
. Note that the degree functor
d:\Lambda\longrightarrow\mathbb{N}^{k}
can be regarded as a functor from
\Lambda
to the abelian group
\mathbb{Z}^{k}
. We often denote the corresponding skew-product
k
-graph
\Lambda\times_{d}\mathbb{Z}^{k}
by
\overline{\Lambda}
. For example, if
\Lambda
is the
2
-graph
f^{*}(R_{1})
of Example
2.3

(i)
, then
\overline{\Lambda}
is isomorphic to the infinite
2
-graph
\Delta_{2}
(see
[
32
, Example 2.2(iv)]
) whose definition is similar to that of the
2
-dimensional grid graph
\Omega_{2}
in Example
2.2
.

Let us now recall
Kumjian-Pask algebras
associated with row-finite
k
-graphs with no sources.

Definition 2.5
.

Following
[
8
]
, let
\Lambda^{\neq 0}:=\{\lambda\in\Lambda~|~d(\lambda)\neq 0\}=\Lambda\setminus\Lambda^{0}
and set
G(\Lambda^{\neq 0}):=\{\lambda^{*}~|~\lambda\in\Lambda^{\neq 0}\}
. Let
\mathsf{k}
be any field. The
Kumjian-Pask
algebra of
\Lambda
over
\mathsf{k}
is denoted by
\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Lambda)
and is defined to be the universal associative
\mathsf{k}
-algebra generated by symbols
p_{v},s_{\lambda},s_{\lambda^{*}}
;
v\in\Lambda^{0},\lambda\in\Lambda
subject to the relations:

(KP1)

p_{u}p_{v}=\delta_{u,v}p_{u}
for all
u,v\in\Lambda^{0}
;

(KP2)

s_{\lambda}s_{\mu}=s_{\lambda\mu},

s_{\mu^{*}}s_{\lambda^{*}}=s_{(\lambda\mu)^{*}}
for all
\lambda,\mu\in\Lambda^{\neq 0}
with
s(\lambda)=r(\mu)
and

p_{r(\lambda)}s_{\lambda}=s_{\lambda}=s_{\lambda}p_{s(\lambda)},

p_{s(\lambda)}s_{\lambda^{*}}=s_{\lambda^{*}}=s_{\lambda^{*}}p_{r(\lambda)}
for all
\lambda\in\Lambda^{\neq 0}
;

(KP3)

s_{\lambda^{*}}s_{\mu}=\delta_{\lambda,\mu}p_{s(\lambda)}
for all
\lambda,\mu\in\Lambda^{\neq 0}
with
d(\lambda)=d(\mu)
;

(KP4)

p_{v}=\displaystyle{\sum_{\lambda\in v\Lambda^{\mathbf{n}}}}s_{\lambda}s_{\lambda^{*}}
for all
v\in\Lambda^{0}
and for all
\mathbf{n}\in\mathbb{N}^{k}\setminus\{0\}
.

Kumjian-Pask algebra is the higher-rank analogue of Leavitt path algebra: Let
E
be a directed graph, then
E^{*}
is a
1
-graph where the roles of the range and source maps are reversed; hence
L_{\mathsf{k}}(E)=\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(E^{*})
.

\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Lambda)
is canonically
\mathbb{Z}^{k}
-graded, where the
n^{\text{th}}
homogeneous component is defined as:

\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Lambda)_{\mathbf{n}}:=\operatorname{span}_{\mathsf{k}}\{s_{\alpha}s_{\beta^{*}}~|~\alpha,\beta\in\Lambda,s(\alpha)=s(\beta)~\text{and}~d(\alpha)-d(\beta)=\mathbf{n}\}.

2.2.
The infinite path groupoid

We assume that the readers are well familiar with the notion of groupoids. Briefly, a
groupoid

\mathcal{G}
is a small category consisting of isomorphisms only. The
range
and
source
of a morphism
x\in\mathcal{G}
are respectively denoted as
r(x)
and
s(x)
. The set of objects is denoted by
\mathcal{G}^{(0)}
, usually called the
unit space
and the set of
composable
pairs is denoted by
\mathcal{G}^{(2)}
i.e.,

\mathcal{G}^{(2)}:=\{(x,y)\in\mathcal{G}\times\mathcal{G}~|~s(x)=r(y)\}.

As we shall be concerned with the graded homology of a certain étale groupoid in Section
4
, we recall the necessary terminologies related to topological groupoids.

A groupoid
\mathcal{G}
is called a
topological groupoid
if it is endowed with a topology such that the partial multiplication and the inversion are continuous. By an
étale
groupoid, here we mean a locally compact Hausdorff topological groupoid
\mathcal{G}
such that the range map
r
(equivalently, the source map
s
) is a local homeomorphism i.e., for each
x\in\mathcal{G}
there is an open neighbourhood
U
of
x
such that
r|_{U}:U\longrightarrow r(U)
is a homeomorphism. A subset
B
of a groupoid
\mathcal{G}
is called a
bisection
if the restrictions
r|_{B}
and
s|_{B}
are injective. An
ample
groupoid is an étale groupoid
\mathcal{G}
whose topology has a basis consisting of compact open bisections of
\mathcal{G}
. In an ample groupoid
\mathcal{G}
, the set of all compact open bisections is denoted by
\mathcal{G}^{a}
and it forms an inverse semigroup with respect to the product

AB:=\{xy~|~(x,y)\in(A\times B)\cap\mathcal{G}^{(2)}\},

and inversion

A^{-1}:=\{x^{-1}~|~x\in A\}.

For any subset
X
of the unit space
\mathcal{G}^{(0)}
the
restriction
of
\mathcal{G}
to
X
is denoted by
\mathcal{G}|_{X}
and is defined as
\mathcal{G}|_{X}:=\{x\in\mathcal{G}~|~s(x),r(x)\in X\}
. The subset
X
is called
\mathcal{G}
-full
if for any
u\in\mathcal{G}^{(0)}
there exists
x\in\mathcal{G}
such that
r(x)=u
and
s(x)\in X
.

Let
\Gamma
be a group with discrete topology. A topological groupoid
\mathcal{G}
is said to be
\Gamma
-
graded
if there is a continuous functor
c:\mathcal{G}\longrightarrow\Gamma
; which is usually referred to as a
cocycle
on
\mathcal{G}
. A subset
X
of
\mathcal{G}
is called
homogeneous
if
X\subseteq c^{-1}(\gamma)
for some
\gamma\in\Gamma
. The set of all homogeneous compact open bisections of
\mathcal{G}
is denoted by
\mathcal{G}_{h}^{a}
.

The étale groupoid, we are concerned with here, is the infinite path groupoid associated with a
k
-graph introduced in
[
34
]
. We now recall its construction. Let
\Lambda
be a row-finite
k
-graph with no sources. An infinite path of
\Lambda
is a degree preserving functor
x:\Omega_{k}\longrightarrow\Lambda
. The set of infinite paths is denoted by
\Lambda^{\infty}
. For any
x\in\Lambda^{\infty}
, the
range
of
x
is defined as
r(x):=x(0)
. For
\mathbf{p}\in\mathbb{N}^{k}
and
x\in\Lambda^{\infty}
, the
\mathbf{p}
-shifted infinite path
\sigma^{\mathbf{p}}(x)
is defined as:

\sigma^{\mathbf{p}}(x)((\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})):=x((\mathbf{m}+\mathbf{p},\mathbf{n}+\mathbf{p}))
for all
(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n})\in\Omega_{k}
.

Define

\mathcal{G}_{\Lambda}:=\{(x,\mathbf{n}-\mathbf{m},y)\in\Lambda^{\infty}\times\mathbb{Z}^{k}\times\Lambda^{\infty}~|~\mathbf{n},\mathbf{m}\in\mathbb{N}^{k}
and
\sigma^{\mathbf{n}}(x)=\sigma^{\mathbf{m}}(y)\}
,

and
\mathcal{G}_{\Lambda}^{(0)}:=\Lambda^{\infty}
; the range and source maps are defined as
r((x,\mathbf{n},y)):=x
and
s((x,\mathbf{n},y)):=y
. Two arrows
(x,\mathbf{n},y)
and
(z,\mathbf{m},w)
are composable if and only if
y=z
and in that case

(x,\mathbf{n},y)(y,\mathbf{m},w):=(x,\mathbf{n}+\mathbf{m},w)
.

Define
(x,\mathbf{n},y)^{-1}:=(y,-\mathbf{n},x)
. Then
\mathcal{G}_{\Lambda}
is a groupoid which is known as the
infinite path groupoid
of
\Lambda
.

For
\lambda,\mu\in\Lambda
with
s(\lambda)=s(\mu)
define

Z(\lambda,\mu):=\{(\lambda z,d(\lambda)-d(\mu),\mu z)~|~z\in s(\lambda)\Lambda^{\infty}\}
.

Then, from
[
34
, Proposition 2.8]
,
\mathcal{B}:=\{Z(\lambda,\mu)~|~\lambda,\mu\in\Lambda,s(\lambda)=s(\mu)\}
forms a basis for an étale topology on
\mathcal{G}_{\Lambda}
. Moreover, each
Z(\lambda,\mu)
is a compact open bisection which makes
\mathcal{G}_{\Lambda}
an ample groupoid. If we identify
x\in\Lambda^{\infty}
with the arrow
(x,0,x)
then the sets
Z(\lambda):=Z(\lambda,\lambda)
form a basis of compact open sets for the subspace topology on
\mathcal{G}_{\Lambda}^{(0)}
. The groupoid
\mathcal{G}_{\Lambda}
is very useful as it provides groupoid models for the
C^{*}
-algebra and the Kumjian-Pask algebra of
\Lambda

[
34
,
16
]
.

2.3.
Graded
K
-theory of Kumjian-Pask algebra

Let
A
be a
\Gamma
​-graded ring, where
\Gamma
is an abelian group. The category of finitely generated

\Gamma
​-graded projective right
A
-modules is denoted by
{\operatorname{{Pgr}^{\Gamma}\!-}}A
. This
is an exact category with the usual notion of a (split) short exact
sequence. Thus one can apply Quillen’s
Q
-construction
[
43
]
to obtain
K
-groups

K_{i}({\operatorname{{Pgr}^{\Gamma}\!-}}A),

for
i\geq 0
, which we denote by
K_{i}^{\operatorname{gr}}(A)
.
The group
\Gamma
acts on the category

{\operatorname{{Pgr}^{\Gamma}\!-}}A
from the right via
(P,\alpha)\mapsto P(\alpha)
, where
\alpha\in\Gamma
. By the functoriality of
K
-groups, this gives
K_{i}^{\operatorname{gr}}(A)
the structure of a right
\mathbb{Z}[\Gamma]
-module
[
29
]
. For a trivial group
\Gamma
, one retrieves the
K
-theory groups
K_{i}(A)
.

For
i=0
,
K^{\operatorname{gr}}_{0}(A)
is called the graded Grothendieck group of
A
, which coincides with the group completion of the commutative monoid
\mathcal{V}^{\operatorname{gr}}(A)
, the monoid of isomorphism classes of finitely generated
\Gamma
-graded projective (right)
A
-modules. Again, for a trivial group, the group completion of
\mathcal{V}(A)
gives the Grothendieck group
K_{0}(A)
.

In the setting of Leavitt path algebras, for a graph
E
, one can realise
L_{\mathsf{k}}(E)
as a Bergman algebra, which in turn allows one to describe
\mathcal{V}(L_{\mathsf{k}}(E))
and consequently,
K_{0}(L_{\mathsf{k}}(E))
from the underlying graph
E

[
7
]
.

The class of Kumjian–Pask algebras that can be realised as Bergman algebras remains unknown.
Therefore, there is not yet a systematic way to describe the Grothendieck group
K_{0}(\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Lambda))
for a
k
-graph
\Lambda
(see Problem
7.3
). However, one can describe the commutative
\Gamma
-monoid
\mathcal{V}^{\operatorname{gr}}(\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Lambda))
and the graded Grothendieck group
K^{\operatorname{gr}}_{0}(\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Lambda))
. This is possible as we have the following equivalence of the categories:

\operatorname{\textbf{Gr}}\!-\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Lambda)\sim\operatorname{\textbf{Mod}}\!-\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\overline{\Lambda}),

where
\overline{\Lambda}=\Lambda\times_{d}\mathbb{Z}^{k}
, is the skew-product higher-rank graph (see Example
2.4
). One then can show that
\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\overline{\Lambda})
is an ultramatricial algebra, which in turn allows computing the lower
K
-theory (see
[
5
]
). In fact,
\mathcal{V}^{\operatorname{gr}}(\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Lambda))
can be realised effectively from the geometry of
\Lambda
. Recall from
[
32
]
that the
talented monoid

T_{\Lambda}
is the
k
-graph monoid of the skew-product
k
-graph
\overline{\Lambda}:=\Lambda\times_{d}\mathbb{Z}^{k}
. More formally,
T_{\Lambda}
can be defined as the commutative monoid freely generated by the symbols
v(\mathbf{n})
,
v\in\Lambda^{0},\mathbf{n}\in\mathbb{Z}^{k}
subject to the following relations:

v(\mathbf{n})=\displaystyle{\sum_{\alpha\in v\Lambda^{\mathbf{e}_{i}}}}s(\alpha)(\mathbf{n}+\mathbf{e}_{i})
,

for all
i=1,2,\ldots,k
(see
[
32
, Definition 3.9]
). As a joint consequence of
[
5
, Corollary 6.7]
and
[
32
, Proposition 3.14
(i)
]
, it follows that
\mathcal{V}^{\operatorname{gr}}(\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Lambda))\cong T_{\Lambda}
as
\mathbb{Z}^{k}
-monoids and therefore the Grothendieck group completion of
T_{\Lambda}
is
K_{0}^{\operatorname{gr}}(\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Lambda))
, the graded
K
-theory of
\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Lambda)
. In view of this and due to the simpler description of
T_{\Lambda}
over
\mathcal{V}^{\operatorname{gr}}(\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Lambda))
, it is quite helpful to deal with
T_{\Lambda}
if one wants to study the graded
K
-theory of
\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Lambda)
. This motivates our program of classifying Kumjian-Pask algebras by
K
-theoretic invariants.

3.
Invariance of graded
K
-theory under some
k
-graph moves

In
[
32
]
, some Morita invariant properties of Kumjian-Pask algebra e.g., being simple, semisimple and the graded Morita invariant property of being graded ideal simple are characterised via properties of the talented monoid that are preserved under
\mathbb{Z}^{k}
-monoid isomorphisms. It suggests that talented monoid may be a possible classifying invariant for certain Kumjian-Pask algebras up to graded Morita equivalence. In this section we gather further evidence in support of this by directly showing that the talented monoid remains isomorphic under certain
moves
on
k
-graphs preserving graded Morita equivalence of Kumjian-Pask algebras.

Among the four moves introduced in
[
21
]
, we only focus on
in-splitting
and
sink deletion
. The other two moves namely,
delay
and
reduction
(see
[
21
]
to know how these works), in general, do not preserve the talented monoid. For example, if we consider
2
-graphs
\Sigma
,
\Lambda
and
\Gamma
with the following
1
-skeletons:

.
u
Reduction
Delay
u
v
u
w
f
f^{1}
f^{2}
\Sigma
\Lambda
\Gamma

then
\Lambda
is the reduction of
\Sigma
at the vertex
w
and
\Gamma
is obtained from
\Lambda
by delaying the edge
f
. One can easily show that
T_{\Lambda}\cong\mathbb{N}
whereas both
T_{\Sigma}
and
T_{\Gamma}
are isomorphic to
\mathbb{N}\oplus\mathbb{N}
. Since graded Morita equivalent rings have order isomorphic graded
K
-groups and hence isomorphic graded
\mathcal{V}
-monoids, the above example shows that the moves of reduction and delay may fail to preserve graded Morita equivalence of Kumjian-Pask algebras.

3.1.
In-splitting at a single vertex

In this subsection, we review the move of
in-splitting
a
k
-graph
\Lambda
introduced by Eckhardt et al.
[
21
, Section 3]
as the analogue of out-splitting of directed graphs.

Let
v\in\Lambda^{0}
. The main idea of in-splitting
\Lambda
at
v
is to partition
v\Lambda^{\mathbf{1}}
into two nonempty subsets
\mathcal{E}_{1},\mathcal{E}_{2}
by keeping in mind the
pairing condition
: if
\lambda,\mu\in v\Lambda^{\mathbf{1}}
are such that
\lambda\alpha=\mu\beta
for some
\alpha,\beta\in\Lambda^{\mathbf{1}}
then
\lambda
and
\mu
should be in the same partition set. If
\lambda\neq\mu
and
d(\lambda)=d(\mu)
then
\operatorname{MCE}(\lambda,\mu)=\emptyset
and so the condition put no restriction on
\lambda,\mu
; they may or may not be kept in the same partition set.

Suppose
v\Lambda^{\mathbf{1}}=\mathcal{E}_{1}\cup\mathcal{E}_{2}
is a partition satisfying the pairing condition. The
in-split
of
\Lambda
at
v
is a
k
-graph
\Lambda_{I}
where the set of vertices

\Lambda_{I}^{0}:=(\Lambda^{0}\setminus\{v\})\cup\{v^{1},v^{2}\},

and the set of edges

\Lambda_{I}^{\mathbf{1}}:=(\Lambda^{\mathbf{1}}\setminus\Lambda^{\mathbf{1}}v)\cup\{f^{1},f^{2}~|~f\in\Lambda^{\mathbf{1}}v\}.

The range and source maps are defined on edges as follows:

r_{I}(\alpha):=\left\{\begin{array}[]{llll}r(\alpha)&\mbox{if }\alpha\in\Lambda^{\mathbf{1}}\setminus(\Lambda^{\mathbf{1}}v\cup v\Lambda^{\mathbf{1}}),\\
v^{i}&\mbox{if }\alpha\in v\Lambda^{\mathbf{1}}\setminus\Lambda^{\mathbf{1}}v~\mbox{and}~\alpha\in\mathcal{E}_{i};~i=1,2,\\
r(f)&\mbox{if }\alpha=f^{i}~\mbox{and}~r(f)\neq v,\\
v^{j}&\mbox{if }\alpha=f^{i}~\mbox{and}~f\in\mathcal{E}_{j};~j=1,2,\end{array}\right.

s_{I}(\alpha):=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}s(\alpha)&\mbox{if }\alpha\in\Lambda^{\mathbf{1}}\setminus\Lambda^{\mathbf{1}}v,\\
v^{i}&\mbox{if }\alpha=f^{i};~i=1,2,\end{array}\right.

and then extended to all paths. Similarly the degree map
d_{I}
is defined on edges as

d_{I}(\alpha):=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}d(\alpha)&\mbox{if }\alpha\in\Lambda^{\mathbf{1}}\setminus\Lambda^{\mathbf{1}}v,\\
d(f)&\mbox{if }\alpha=f^{i};~i=1,2,\end{array}\right.

and then additively extended to any path. That
(\Lambda_{I},d_{I})
is indeed a
k
-graph is established in
[
21
, Theorem 3.8]
. To keep track of the new vertices and edges originated in the in-splitting process, it is helpful to assign a
parent
to each member of
\Lambda_{I}
. This is done by defining
par(v^{i})=v
,
par(f^{i})=f
for
i=1,2
and
par(x)=x
if
x
is neither
v^{i}
nor
f^{i}
. Also, if
\lambda=\lambda_{1}\lambda_{2}\cdots\lambda_{n}
then
par(\lambda)=par(\lambda_{1})par(\lambda_{2})\cdots par(\lambda_{n})
. The factorization rule for paths in
\Lambda_{I}
is then determined by the factorization of their parent in
\Lambda
.

In
[
21
, Theorem 3.9]
, it was shown that there is a gauge-action preserving isomorphism between the
C^{*}
-algebra of a
k
-graph
\Lambda
and the
C^{*}
-algebra of its in-split
\Lambda_{I}
at a vertex
v
. The following can be seen as a purely algebraic analogue of this result.

Proposition 3.1
.

Let
\Lambda
be a row-finite
k
-graph with no sources and
\Lambda_{I}
, the in-split of
\Lambda
at a vertex
v
with respect to the partition
v\Lambda^{\mathbf{1}}=\mathcal{E}_{1}\cup\mathcal{E}_{2}
. Let
\mathsf{k}
be any field. Then there is a graded isomorphism

\pi:\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Lambda)\longrightarrow\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Lambda_{I}).

Consequently,
\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Lambda)
and
\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Lambda_{I})
are graded Morita equivalent.

Proof.

Suppose
(q,t)
and
(p,s)
denote the Kumjian-Pask families generating
\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Lambda)
and
\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Lambda_{I})
respectively. Define

P_{u}:=\displaystyle{\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}w\in\Lambda_{I}^{0}\\
par(w)=u\end{subarray}}}p_{w},~S_{\lambda}:=\displaystyle{\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\alpha\in\Lambda_{I}\\
par(\alpha)=\lambda\end{subarray}}}s_{\alpha},~\text{and}~S_{\lambda^{*}}:=\displaystyle{\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\alpha\in\Lambda_{I}\\
par(\alpha)=\lambda\end{subarray}}}s_{\alpha^{*}}

for all
u\in\Lambda^{0}
and
\lambda\in\Lambda^{\neq 0}
. We now prove that
\{P_{u},S_{\lambda},S_{\lambda^{*}}\}
forms a Kumjian-Pask
\Lambda
-family in the
\mathsf{k}
-algebra
\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Lambda_{I})
. Since each
P_{u}
is a sum of mutually orthogonal idempotents and distinct vertices in
\Lambda
can not have a common offspring, so
P_{u}
’s are also mutually orthogonal idempotents in
\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Lambda_{I})
which shows that
(P,S)
satisfies
(KP1)
. Let
\lambda,\mu\in\Lambda^{\neq 0}
be such that
s(\lambda)=r(\mu)
. If
r(\mu)\neq v
then for all
\alpha,\beta\in\Lambda_{I}
with
par(\alpha)=\lambda
,
par(\beta)=\mu
we have
r_{I}(\beta)=r(\mu)=s(\lambda)=s_{I}(\alpha)
and so

\displaystyle S_{\lambda}S_{\mu}=\left(\displaystyle{\sum_{par(\alpha)=\lambda}}s_{\alpha}\right)\left(\displaystyle{\sum_{par(\beta)=\mu}}s_{\beta}\right)

\displaystyle=\displaystyle{\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}par(\alpha)=\lambda\\
par(\beta)=\mu\end{subarray}}}s_{\alpha}s_{\beta}

\displaystyle=\displaystyle{\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}par(\alpha)=\lambda\\
par(\beta)=\mu\end{subarray}}}s_{\alpha\beta}

\displaystyle=\displaystyle{\sum_{par(\gamma)=\lambda\mu}}s_{\gamma}=S_{\lambda\mu}.

The second last equality follows since there is a bijection between the sets

\{\alpha\in\Lambda_{I}~|~par(\alpha)=\lambda\}\times\{\beta\in\Lambda_{I}~|~par(\beta)=\mu\}

and

\{\gamma\in\Lambda_{I}~|~par(\gamma)=\lambda\mu\}

defined by
(\alpha,\beta)\longmapsto\alpha\beta
. If
r(\mu)=v
then
\mu=g\eta
for some
g\in v\Lambda^{\mathbf{1}}
. Without loss of generality, assume that
g\in\mathcal{E}_{1}
. Then for any
\beta\in\Lambda_{I}
with
par(\beta)=\mu
,
r_{I}(\beta)=v^{1}
. In this case,

S_{\lambda}S_{\mu}=\left(\displaystyle{\sum_{par(\alpha)=\lambda}}s_{\alpha}\right)\left(\displaystyle{\sum_{par(\beta)=\mu}}s_{\beta}\right)=\displaystyle{\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}par(\alpha)=\lambda,s_{I}(\alpha)=v^{1}\\
par(\beta)=\mu\end{subarray}}}s_{\alpha\beta}=\displaystyle{\sum_{par(\gamma)=\lambda\mu}}s_{\gamma}=S_{\lambda\mu}.

Similarly, one can show that
S_{\lambda^{*}}S_{\mu^{*}}=S_{(\lambda\mu)^{*}}
for all
\lambda,\mu\in\Lambda
with
r(\mu)=s(\lambda)
. The other relations in
(KP2)
are straightforward to verify. For
(KP3)
choose
\lambda,\mu\in\Lambda^{\neq 0}
such that
\lambda\neq\mu
and
d(\lambda)=d(\mu)
. Then
\alpha\neq\beta
for all
\alpha,\beta
with
par(\alpha)=\lambda
and
par(\beta)=\mu
. Since
d_{I}(\alpha)=d(\lambda)=d(\mu)=d_{I}(\beta)
, so we have

S_{\lambda^{*}}S_{\mu}=\left(\displaystyle{\sum_{par(\alpha)=\lambda}}s_{\alpha^{*}}\right)\left(\displaystyle{\sum_{par(\beta)=\mu}}s_{\beta}\right)=\displaystyle{\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}par(\alpha)=\lambda\\
par(\beta)=\mu\end{subarray}}}s_{\alpha^{*}}s_{\beta}=0.

On the other hand,

S_{\lambda^{*}}S_{\lambda}=\left(\displaystyle{\sum_{par(\alpha)=\lambda}}s_{\alpha^{*}}\right)\left(\displaystyle{\sum_{par(\beta)=\alpha}}s_{\beta}\right)=\displaystyle{\sum_{par(\alpha)=\lambda}}s_{\alpha^{*}}s_{\alpha}.

If
s(\lambda)\neq v
then for all
\alpha\in\Lambda_{I}
with
par(\alpha)=\lambda
,
s_{I}(\alpha)=s(\lambda)
. By
[
21
, Remark 3.7]
, there is exactly one such
\alpha
and hence
S_{\lambda^{*}}S_{\lambda}=s_{\alpha^{*}}s_{\alpha}=p_{s_{I}(\alpha)}=P_{s(\lambda)}
. If
s(\lambda)=v
then we can write
\lambda=\delta f
where
f\in\Lambda^{\mathbf{1}}v
. We observe that in this case, there are exactly two offspring of
\lambda
in
\Lambda_{I}
. Indeed, if
par(\alpha)=\lambda
then
\alpha=\xi h
for some
\xi\in\Lambda_{I}
,
h\in\Lambda_{I}^{1}
such that
par(\xi)=\delta
and
par(h)=f
. Clearly
h
is either
f^{1}
or
f^{2}
. In both cases

s_{I}(\xi):=\left\{\begin{array}[]{lll}r(f)&\mbox{if }r(f)\neq v,\\
v^{1}&\mbox{if }f\in\mathcal{E}_{1},\\
v^{2}&\mbox{if }f\in\mathcal{E}_{2}.\end{array}\right.

Again in view of
[
21
, Remark 3.7]
, there is a unique
\xi
with the above properties, and we have exactly two offspring of
\lambda
namely
\alpha_{1}:=\xi f^{1}
and
\alpha_{2}:=\xi f^{2}
. Now,

S_{\lambda^{*}}S_{\lambda}=\displaystyle{\sum_{par(\alpha)=\lambda}}s_{\alpha^{*}}s_{\alpha}=s_{\alpha_{1}^{*}}s_{\alpha_{1}}+s_{\alpha_{2}^{*}}s_{\alpha_{2}}=p_{v^{1}}+p_{v^{2}}=P_{s(\lambda)}.

Hence
(KP3)
is verified. Finally, for
(KP4)
, take
u\in\Lambda^{0}
and
\mathbf{n}\in\mathbb{N}^{k}\setminus\{0\}
. If
\alpha\neq\beta
and
par(\alpha)=par(\beta)
then
s_{I}(\alpha)\neq s_{I}(\beta)
and consequently
s_{\alpha}s_{\beta^{*}}=0
. Therefore,

\displaystyle{\sum_{\lambda\in u\Lambda^{\mathbf{n}}}}S_{\lambda}S_{\lambda^{*}}=\displaystyle{\sum_{\lambda\in u\Lambda^{\mathbf{n}}}}\left(\displaystyle{\sum_{par(\alpha)=\lambda}}s_{\alpha}s_{\alpha^{*}}\right).

If
u\neq v
then
u\Lambda_{I}^{\mathbf{n}}=\displaystyle{\bigsqcup_{\lambda\in u\Lambda^{\mathbf{n}}}}\{\alpha\in\Lambda_{I}~|~par(\alpha)=\lambda\}
and so

\displaystyle{\sum_{\lambda\in u\Lambda^{\mathbf{n}}}}S_{\lambda}S_{\lambda^{*}}=\displaystyle{\sum_{\alpha\in u\Lambda_{I}^{\mathbf{n}}}}s_{\alpha}s_{\alpha^{*}}=p_{u}=P_{u}.

Again

\displaystyle\displaystyle{\sum_{\lambda\in v\Lambda^{\mathbf{n}}}}S_{\lambda}S_{\lambda^{*}}

\displaystyle=\displaystyle{\sum_{\lambda\in v\Lambda^{\mathbf{n}}}}\left(\displaystyle{\sum_{par(\alpha)=\lambda}}s_{\alpha}s_{\alpha^{*}}\right)

\displaystyle=\displaystyle{\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lambda\in v\Lambda^{\mathbf{n}}\\
\lambda(0,\mathbf{e}_{i})\in\mathcal{E}_{1}\end{subarray}}}\left(\displaystyle{\sum_{par(\alpha)=\lambda}}s_{\alpha}s_{\alpha^{*}}\right)+\displaystyle{\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lambda\in v\Lambda^{\mathbf{n}}\\
\lambda(0,\mathbf{e}_{i})\in\mathcal{E}_{2}\end{subarray}}}\left(\displaystyle{\sum_{par(\beta)=\lambda}}s_{\beta}s_{\beta^{*}}\right)~(\text{since}~\mathbf{n}\neq 0,\mathbf{n}\geq\mathbf{e}_{i}~\text{for some}~i=1,2,...,k)

\displaystyle=\displaystyle{\sum_{\alpha\in v^{1}\Lambda_{I}^{\mathbf{n}}}}s_{\alpha}s_{\alpha^{*}}+\displaystyle{\sum_{\beta\in v^{2}\Lambda_{I}^{\mathbf{n}}}}s_{\beta}s_{\beta^{*}}

\displaystyle=p_{v^{1}}+p_{v^{2}}=P_{v}.

Thus
(P,S)
satisfies
(KP4)
and becomes a Kumjian-Pask
\Lambda
-family in
\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Lambda_{I})
. By
[
8
, Theorem 3.4]
, we have a unique
k
-algebra homomorphism
\pi:\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Lambda)\longrightarrow\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Lambda_{I})
such that

\pi(q_{w})=P_{w},~\pi(t_{\lambda})=S_{\lambda}~\text{and}~\pi(t_{\lambda^{*}})=S_{\lambda^{*}},

for all
w\in\lambda^{0}
and
\lambda\in\Lambda^{\neq 0}
. Since
d_{I}(\alpha)=d(par(\alpha))
for all
\alpha\in\Lambda_{I}
,
\pi
preserves the degrees of generators of
\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Lambda)
and thus a graded homomorphism. Note that for all
w\in\Lambda^{0}
,
\pi(q_{w})
being a sum of vertex idempotents in
\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Lambda_{I})
is never
0
and so by invoking the graded uniqueness theorem
[
8
, Theorem 4.1]
, we can conclude that
\pi
is injective. For any
w\in\Lambda^{0}\setminus\{v\}
,
p_{w}=P_{w}=\pi(q_{w})
. Choose any
j\in\{1,2,...,k\}
. Then, for
i=1,2
,

p_{v^{i}}=\displaystyle{\sum_{\alpha\in v^{i}\Lambda_{I}^{\mathbf{e}_{j}}}}s_{\alpha}s_{\alpha^{*}}=\displaystyle{\sum_{\lambda\in v\Lambda^{\mathbf{e}_{j}}\cap\mathcal{E}_{i}}}\left(\displaystyle{\sum_{par(\alpha)=\lambda}}s_{\alpha}s_{\alpha^{*}}\right)=\displaystyle{\sum_{\lambda\in v\Lambda^{\mathbf{e}_{j}}\cap\mathcal{E}_{i}}}S_{\lambda}S_{\lambda^{*}}=\pi\left(\displaystyle{\sum_{\lambda\in v\Lambda^{\mathbf{e}_{j}}\cap\mathcal{E}_{i}}}t_{\lambda}t_{\lambda^{*}}\right).

Let
\alpha\in\Lambda_{I}^{\neq 0}
. If
s(par(\alpha))\neq v
, then
s_{\alpha}=S_{par(\alpha)}=\pi(t_{par(\alpha)})
and
s_{\alpha^{*}}=\pi(t_{par(\alpha)^{*}})
. If
s(par(\alpha))=v
then
\alpha=\mu f^{i}
for some
\mu\in\Lambda_{I},f\in\Lambda^{\mathbf{1}}v
and
i\in\{1,2\}
. Subsequently,
s_{\alpha}=S_{par(\alpha)}p_{v^{i}}=\pi(t_{par(\alpha)})p_{v^{i}}\in\operatorname{Im}(\pi)
since
p_{v^{i}}\in\operatorname{Im}(\pi)
. Similarly
s_{\alpha^{*}}\in\operatorname{Im}(\pi)
. Thus all the generators of
\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Lambda_{I})
are in
\operatorname{Im}(\pi)
which proves that
\pi
is surjective. Therefore,
\pi
is a graded isomorphism of
\mathbb{Z}^{k}
-graded
\mathsf{k}
-algebras.
∎

As an immediate consequence of the above Proposition, it follows that whenever we in-split a
k
-graph
\Lambda
at a vertex
v
, then there is an isomorphism between the categories
\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Lambda)-\operatorname{\textbf{Gr}}
and
\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Lambda_{I})-\operatorname{\textbf{Gr}}
(as any graded left
\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Lambda_{I})
-module can be realised canonically as a graded left
\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Lambda)
-module via the graded isomorphism
\pi
of Proposition
3.1
). This category isomorphism commutes with the respective shift functors
\tau_{\mathbf{n}}
for every
\mathbf{n}\in\mathbb{Z}^{k}
and hence induces a
\mathbb{Z}^{k}
-monoid isomorphism from
\mathcal{V}^{\operatorname{gr}}(\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Lambda))
to
\mathcal{V}^{\operatorname{gr}}(\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Lambda_{I}))
; the
\mathbb{Z}^{k}
-action on
\mathcal{V}^{\operatorname{gr}}(\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Lambda))
being given as
~{}^{\mathbf{n}}[P]:=[P(\mathbf{n})]
. Therefore, in-splitting results in
\mathbb{Z}^{k}
-monoid isomorphic talented monoids. However, we can prove this independently without referring to the Kumjian-Pask algebras which justifies that talented monoid directly captures those geometries of a
k
-graph which are linked with the graded Morita invariant properties of the corresponding Kumjian-Pask algebra.

The directed graph version of the following theorem was obtained in
[
18
, Theorem 4.7]
.

Theorem 3.2
.

Let
\Lambda
be a row-finite
k
-graph with no sources and
\Lambda_{I}
the in-split of
\Lambda
at a vertex
v
with respect to the partition
v\Lambda^{\mathbf{1}}=\mathcal{E}_{1}\cup\mathcal{E}_{2}
. The map
\phi_{I}:T_{\Lambda}\longrightarrow T_{\Lambda_{I}}
defined on generators as:

\phi_{I}(w(\mathbf{n})):=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}w(\mathbf{n})&\mbox{if }w\neq v,\\
v^{1}(\mathbf{n})+v^{2}(\mathbf{n})&\mbox{if }w=v\end{array}\right.

for all
w\in\Lambda^{0}
and
\mathbf{n}\in\mathbb{Z}^{k}
, is a
\mathbb{Z}^{k}
-monoid isomorphism.

Proof.

We first show that
\phi_{I}
is well-defined. For this we need to show that
\phi_{I}
preserves the relation:

u(\mathbf{n})=\displaystyle{\sum_{\lambda\in u\Lambda^{\mathbf{e}_{i}}}}s(\lambda)(\mathbf{n}+\mathbf{e}_{i}),

for all
u\in\Lambda^{0}
,
\mathbf{n}\in\mathbb{Z}^{k}
and
i=1,2,...,k
. Note that

\phi_{I}\left(\displaystyle{\sum_{\lambda\in u\Lambda^{\mathbf{e}_{i}}}}s(\lambda)(\mathbf{n}+\mathbf{e}_{i})\right)=\displaystyle{\sum_{\lambda\in u\Lambda^{\mathbf{e}_{i}}}}\phi_{I}(s(\lambda)(\mathbf{n}+\mathbf{e}_{i}))=\displaystyle{\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lambda\in u\Lambda^{\mathbf{e}_{i}}\\
s(\lambda)\neq v\end{subarray}}}s(\lambda)(\mathbf{n}+\mathbf{e}_{i})+\displaystyle{\sum_{\lambda\in u\Lambda^{\mathbf{e}_{i}}v}}(v^{1}(\mathbf{n}+\mathbf{e}_{i})+v^{2}(\mathbf{n}+\mathbf{e}_{i})).

If
u\neq v
, it implies

\displaystyle\phi_{I}\left(\displaystyle{\sum_{\lambda\in u\Lambda^{\mathbf{e}_{i}}}}s(\lambda)(\mathbf{n}+\mathbf{e}_{i})\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\displaystyle{\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\alpha\in u\Lambda_{I}^{\mathbf{e}_{i}}\\
s(par(\alpha))\neq v\end{subarray}}}s_{I}(\alpha)(\mathbf{n}+\mathbf{e}_{i})+\displaystyle{\sum_{\beta\in u\Lambda_{I}^{\mathbf{e}_{i}}v^{1}}}s_{I}(\beta)(\mathbf{n}+\mathbf{e}_{i})+\displaystyle{\sum_{\gamma\in u\Lambda_{I}^{\mathbf{e}_{i}}v^{2}}}s_{I}(\gamma)(\mathbf{n}+\mathbf{e}_{i})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\displaystyle{\sum_{h\in u\Lambda_{I}^{\mathbf{e}_{i}}}}s_{I}(h)(\mathbf{n}+\mathbf{e}_{i})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle~u(\mathbf{n})=\phi_{I}(u(\mathbf{n})),

whereas

\displaystyle\phi_{I}\left(\displaystyle{\sum_{\lambda\in v\Lambda^{\mathbf{e}_{i}}}}s(\lambda)(\mathbf{n}+\mathbf{e}_{i})\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\displaystyle{\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lambda\in v\Lambda^{\mathbf{e}_{i}}\cap\mathcal{E}_{1}\\
s(\lambda)\neq v\end{subarray}}}s(\lambda)(\mathbf{n}+\mathbf{e}_{i})+\displaystyle{\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lambda\in v\Lambda^{\mathbf{e}_{i}}\cap\mathcal{E}_{2}\\
s(\lambda)\neq v\end{subarray}}}s(\lambda)(\mathbf{n}+\mathbf{e}_{i})+\displaystyle{\sum_{\lambda\in v\Lambda^{\mathbf{e}_{i}}v\cap\mathcal{E}_{1}}}(v^{1}(\mathbf{n}+\mathbf{e}_{i})+v^{2}(\mathbf{n}+\mathbf{e}_{i}))

\displaystyle+\displaystyle{\sum_{\lambda\in v\Lambda^{\mathbf{e}_{i}}v\cap\mathcal{E}_{2}}}(v^{1}(\mathbf{n}+\mathbf{e}_{i})+v^{2}(\mathbf{n}+\mathbf{e}_{i}))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(\displaystyle{\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lambda\in v\Lambda^{\mathbf{e}_{i}}\cap\mathcal{E}_{1}\\
s(\lambda)\neq v\end{subarray}}}s(\lambda)(\mathbf{n}+\mathbf{e}_{i})+\displaystyle{\sum_{\lambda\in v\Lambda^{\mathbf{e}_{i}}v\cap\mathcal{E}_{1}}}v^{1}(\mathbf{n}+\mathbf{e}_{i})+\displaystyle{\sum_{\lambda\in v\Lambda^{\mathbf{e}_{i}}v\cap\mathcal{E}_{1}}}v^{2}(\mathbf{n}+\mathbf{e}_{i})\right)

\displaystyle+\left(\displaystyle{\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lambda\in v\Lambda^{\mathbf{e}_{i}}\cap\mathcal{E}_{2}\\
s(\lambda)\neq v\end{subarray}}}s(\lambda)(\mathbf{n}+\mathbf{e}_{i})+\displaystyle{\sum_{\lambda\in v\Lambda^{\mathbf{e}_{i}}v\cap\mathcal{E}_{2}}}v^{1}(\mathbf{n}+\mathbf{e}_{i})+\displaystyle{\sum_{\lambda\in v\Lambda^{\mathbf{e}_{i}}v\cap\mathcal{E}_{2}}}v^{2}(\mathbf{n}+\mathbf{e}_{i})\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(\displaystyle{\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\alpha\in v^{1}\Lambda_{I}^{\mathbf{e}_{i}}\\
s(par(\alpha))\neq v\end{subarray}}}s_{I}(\alpha)(\mathbf{n}+\mathbf{e}_{i})+\displaystyle{\sum_{\beta\in v^{1}\Lambda_{I}^{\mathbf{e}_{i}}v^{1}}}s_{I}(\beta)(\mathbf{n}+\mathbf{e}_{i})+\displaystyle{\sum_{\gamma\in v^{1}\Lambda_{I}^{\mathbf{e}_{i}}v^{2}}}s_{I}(\gamma)(\mathbf{n}+\mathbf{e}_{i})\right)

\displaystyle+\left(\displaystyle{\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\alpha^{\prime}\in v^{2}\Lambda_{I}^{\mathbf{e}_{i}}\\
s(par(\alpha^{\prime}))\neq v\end{subarray}}}s_{I}(\alpha^{\prime})(\mathbf{n}+\mathbf{e}_{i})+\displaystyle{\sum_{\beta^{\prime}\in v^{2}\Lambda_{I}^{\mathbf{e}_{i}}v^{1}}}s_{I}(\beta^{\prime})(\mathbf{n}+\mathbf{e}_{i})+\displaystyle{\sum_{\gamma^{\prime}\in v^{2}\Lambda_{I}^{\mathbf{e}_{i}}v^{2}}}s_{I}(\gamma^{\prime})(\mathbf{n}+\mathbf{e}_{i})\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\displaystyle{\sum_{g\in v^{1}\Lambda_{I}^{\mathbf{e}_{i}}}}s_{I}(g)(\mathbf{n}+\mathbf{e}_{i})+\displaystyle{\sum_{h\in v^{2}\Lambda_{I}^{\mathbf{e}_{i}}}}s_{I}(h)(\mathbf{n}+\mathbf{e}_{i})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle~v^{1}(\mathbf{n})+v^{2}(\mathbf{n})=\phi_{I}(v(\mathbf{n})).

Thus
\phi_{I}
is a well-defined monoid homomorphism. We now define an inverse for
\phi_{I}
. For this we fix
j\in\{1,2,...,k\}
and define a map
\psi:T_{\Lambda_{I}}\longrightarrow T_{\Lambda}
on generators by

\psi(u(\mathbf{n})):=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\hskip 35.56593ptu(\mathbf{n})&\mbox{if }u\neq v^{1},v^{2},\\
\displaystyle{\sum_{f\in\mathcal{E}_{i}\cap\Lambda^{\mathbf{e}_{j}}}}s(f)(\mathbf{n}+\mathbf{e}_{j})&\mbox{if }u=v^{i}~\mbox{for}~i=1,2.\end{array}\right.

Again we first need to check that
\psi
is well-defined. If
u\neq v^{1},v^{2}
then for each
i=1,2,...,k
and
\mathbf{n}\in\mathbb{Z}^{k}
, we have

\displaystyle\psi\left(\displaystyle{\sum_{\alpha\in u\Lambda_{I}^{\mathbf{e}_{i}}}}s_{I}(\alpha)(\mathbf{n}+\mathbf{e}_{i})\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\displaystyle{\sum_{\alpha\in u\Lambda_{I}^{\mathbf{e}_{i}}}}\psi(s_{I}(\alpha)(\mathbf{n}+\mathbf{e}_{i}))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\displaystyle{\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\alpha\in u\Lambda_{I}^{\mathbf{e}_{i}}\\
s_{I}(\alpha)\neq v^{1},v^{2}\end{subarray}}}s_{I}(\alpha)(\mathbf{n}+\mathbf{e}_{i})+\displaystyle{\sum_{\alpha\in u\Lambda_{I}^{\mathbf{e}_{i}}v^{1}}}\left(\displaystyle{\sum_{f\in\mathcal{E}_{1}\cap\Lambda^{\mathbf{e}_{j}}}}s(f)(\mathbf{n}+\mathbf{e}_{i}+\mathbf{e}_{j})\right)+\displaystyle{\sum_{\alpha\in u\Lambda_{I}^{\mathbf{e}_{i}}v^{2}}}\left(\displaystyle{\sum_{f\in\mathcal{E}_{2}\cap\Lambda^{\mathbf{e}_{j}}}}s(f)(\mathbf{n}+\mathbf{e}_{i}+\mathbf{e}_{j})\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\displaystyle{\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\alpha\in u\Lambda_{I}^{\mathbf{e}_{i}}\\
par(\alpha)\neq v\end{subarray}}}s(par(\alpha))(\mathbf{n}+\mathbf{e}_{i})+\displaystyle{\sum_{\lambda\in u\Lambda^{\mathbf{e}_{i}}v}}\left(\displaystyle{\sum_{f\in\mathcal{E}_{1}\cap\Lambda^{\mathbf{e}_{j}}}}s(f)(\mathbf{n}+\mathbf{e}_{i}+\mathbf{e}_{j})+\displaystyle{\sum_{f\in\mathcal{E}_{2}\cap\Lambda^{\mathbf{e}_{j}}}}s(f)(\mathbf{n}+\mathbf{e}_{i}+\mathbf{e}_{j})\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\displaystyle{\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lambda\in u\Lambda^{\mathbf{e}_{i}}\\
s(\lambda)\neq v\end{subarray}}}s(\lambda)(\mathbf{n}+\mathbf{e}_{i})+\displaystyle{\sum_{\lambda\in u\Lambda^{\mathbf{e}_{i}}v}}\left(\displaystyle{\sum_{f\in v\Lambda^{\mathbf{e}_{j}}}}s(f)(\mathbf{n}+\mathbf{e}_{i}+\mathbf{e}_{j})\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\displaystyle{\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lambda\in u\Lambda^{\mathbf{e}_{i}}\\
s(\lambda)\neq v\end{subarray}}}s(\lambda)(\mathbf{n}+\mathbf{e}_{i})+\displaystyle{\sum_{\lambda\in u\Lambda^{\mathbf{e}_{i}}v}}v(\mathbf{n}+\mathbf{e}_{i})~~(\text{using relations of}~T_{\Lambda})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\displaystyle{\sum_{\lambda\in u\Lambda^{\mathbf{e}_{i}}}}s(\lambda)(\mathbf{n}+\mathbf{e}_{i})=u(\mathbf{n})=\psi(u(\mathbf{n})).

For
t=1,2

\displaystyle\psi\left(\displaystyle{\sum_{\alpha\in v^{t}\Lambda_{I}^{\mathbf{e}_{i}}}}s_{I}(\alpha)(\mathbf{n}+\mathbf{e}_{i})\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\displaystyle{\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\alpha\in v^{t}\Lambda_{I}^{\mathbf{e}_{i}}\\
s_{I}(\alpha)\neq v^{1},v^{2}\end{subarray}}}s_{I}(\alpha)(\mathbf{n}+\mathbf{e}_{i})+\displaystyle{\sum_{\alpha\in v^{t}\Lambda_{I}^{\mathbf{e}_{i}}v^{1}}}\left(\displaystyle{\sum_{f\in\mathcal{E}_{1}\cap\Lambda^{\mathbf{e}_{j}}}}s(f)(\mathbf{n}+\mathbf{e}_{i}+\mathbf{e}_{j})\right)+\displaystyle{\sum_{\alpha\in v^{t}\Lambda_{I}^{\mathbf{e}_{i}}v^{2}}}\left(\displaystyle{\sum_{f\in\mathcal{E}_{2}\cap\Lambda^{\mathbf{e}_{j}}}}s(f)(\mathbf{n}+\mathbf{e}_{i}+\mathbf{e}_{j})\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\displaystyle{\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\alpha\in v^{t}\Lambda_{I}^{\mathbf{e}_{i}}\\
par(\alpha)\neq v\end{subarray}}}s(par(\alpha))(\mathbf{n}+\mathbf{e}_{i})+\displaystyle{\sum_{\lambda\in v\Lambda^{\mathbf{e}_{i}}v\cap\mathcal{E}_{t}}}\left(\displaystyle{\sum_{f\in\mathcal{E}_{1}\cap\Lambda^{\mathbf{e}_{j}}}}s(f)(\mathbf{n}+\mathbf{e}_{i}+\mathbf{e}_{j})+\displaystyle{\sum_{f\in\mathcal{E}_{2}\cap\Lambda^{\mathbf{e}_{j}}}}s(f)(\mathbf{n}+\mathbf{e}_{i}+\mathbf{e}_{j})\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\displaystyle{\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lambda\in v\Lambda^{\mathbf{e}_{i}}\cap\mathcal{E}_{t}\\
s(\lambda)\neq v\end{subarray}}}s(\lambda)(\mathbf{n}+\mathbf{e}_{i})+\displaystyle{\sum_{\lambda\in v\Lambda^{\mathbf{e}_{i}}v\cap\mathcal{E}_{t}}}\left(\displaystyle{\sum_{f\in v\Lambda^{\mathbf{e}_{j}}}}s(f)(\mathbf{n}+\mathbf{e}_{i}+\mathbf{e}_{j})\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\displaystyle{\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lambda\in v\Lambda^{\mathbf{e}_{i}}\cap\mathcal{E}_{t}\\
s(\lambda)\neq v\end{subarray}}}s(\lambda)(\mathbf{n}+\mathbf{e}_{i})+\displaystyle{\sum_{\lambda\in v\Lambda^{\mathbf{e}_{i}}v\cap\mathcal{E}_{t}}}v(\mathbf{n}+\mathbf{e}_{i})~~(\text{using relations of}~T_{\Lambda})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\displaystyle{\sum_{\lambda\in\mathcal{E}_{t}\cap\Lambda^{\mathbf{e}_{i}}}}s(\lambda)(\mathbf{n}+\mathbf{e}_{i}).

The second equality above follows since the parent of any
\alpha\in v^{t}\Lambda_{I}^{\mathbf{e}_{i}}v^{1}
(or
v^{t}\Lambda_{I}^{\mathbf{e}_{i}}v^{2}
) is a loop at
v
of degree
\mathbf{e}_{i}
, contained in the partition set
\mathcal{E}_{t}
; and hence both
v^{t}\Lambda_{I}^{\mathbf{e}_{i}}v^{1}
and
v^{t}\Lambda_{I}^{\mathbf{e}_{i}}v^{2}
are in bijection with
v\Lambda^{\mathbf{e}_{i}}v\cap\mathcal{E}_{t}
. The crucial part which remains to prove in order to complete the verification is that the equality

(1)

\displaystyle{\sum_{\lambda\in\mathcal{E}_{t}\cap\Lambda^{\mathbf{e}_{i}}}}s(\lambda)(\mathbf{n}+\mathbf{e}_{i})=\displaystyle{\sum_{\mu\in\mathcal{E}_{t}\cap\Lambda^{\mathbf{e}_{j}}}}s(\mu)(\mathbf{n}+\mathbf{e}_{j})

holds in
T_{\Lambda}
for each
i=1,2,...,k
. There is nothing to show if
i=j
. So assume that
i\neq j
. Then in
T_{\Lambda}
,

\displaystyle\displaystyle{\sum_{\lambda\in\mathcal{E}_{t}\cap\Lambda^{\mathbf{e}_{i}}}}s(\lambda)(\mathbf{n}+\mathbf{e}_{i})

\displaystyle=\displaystyle{\sum_{\lambda\in\mathcal{E}_{t}\cap\Lambda^{\mathbf{e}_{i}}}}\left(\displaystyle{\sum_{\alpha\in s(\lambda)\Lambda^{\mathbf{e}_{j}}}}s(\alpha)(\mathbf{n}+\mathbf{e}_{i}+\mathbf{e}_{j})\right)

\displaystyle=\displaystyle{\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\gamma\in v\Lambda^{\mathbf{e}_{i}+\mathbf{e}_{j}}\\
\gamma(0,\mathbf{e}_{i})\in\mathcal{E}_{t}\end{subarray}}}s(\gamma)(\mathbf{n}+\mathbf{e}_{i}+\mathbf{e}_{j})

\displaystyle=\displaystyle{\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\gamma\in v\Lambda^{\mathbf{e}_{i}+\mathbf{e}_{j}}\\
\gamma(0,\mathbf{e}_{j})\in\mathcal{E}_{t}\end{subarray}}}s(\gamma)(\mathbf{n}+\mathbf{e}_{i}+\mathbf{e}_{j})

\displaystyle=\displaystyle{\sum_{\mu\in\mathcal{E}_{t}\cap\Lambda^{\mathbf{e}_{j}}}}\left(\displaystyle{\sum_{\beta\in s(\mu)\Lambda^{\mathbf{e}_{i}}}}s(\beta)(\mathbf{n}+\mathbf{e}_{j}+\mathbf{e}_{i})\right)=\displaystyle{\sum_{\mu\in\mathcal{E}_{t}\cap\Lambda^{\mathbf{e}_{j}}}}s(\mu)(\mathbf{n}+\mathbf{e}_{j}).

The second and fourth equality are self-explanatory. For the third one, note that whenever
\gamma=\lambda\alpha\in v\Lambda^{\mathbf{e}_{i}+\mathbf{e}_{j}}
and
\lambda\in\mathcal{E}_{t}\cap\Lambda^{\mathbf{e}_{i}}
, by the factorization property of
\Lambda
, there exist unique
\mu\in v\Lambda^{\mathbf{e}_{j}}
and
\beta\in s(\mu)\Lambda^{\mathbf{e}_{i}}
such that
\gamma=\mu\beta
. Then by the pairing condition,
\lambda
and
\mu
are in the same partition set, whence
\mu\in\mathcal{E}_{t}
. Thus
\gamma(0,\mathbf{e}_{i})\in\mathcal{E}_{t}
if and only if
\gamma(0,\mathbf{e}_{j})\in\mathcal{E}_{t}
.

Now using
(\ref{welldefined})
we have

\psi\left(\displaystyle{\sum_{\alpha\in v^{t}\Lambda_{I}^{\mathbf{e}_{i}}}}s_{I}(\alpha)(\mathbf{n}+\mathbf{e}_{i})\right)=\displaystyle{\sum_{\lambda\in\mathcal{E}_{t}\cap\Lambda^{\mathbf{e}_{i}}}}s(\lambda)(\mathbf{n}+\mathbf{e}_{i})=\displaystyle{\sum_{\mu\in\mathcal{E}_{t}\cap\Lambda^{\mathbf{e}_{j}}}}s(\mu)(\mathbf{n}+\mathbf{e}_{j})=\psi(v^{t}(\mathbf{n})),

showing that
\psi
is well-defined. To complete the proof, we observe that
\phi_{I}
and
\psi
are mutually inverse homomorphisms. If
w\neq v
then
\psi(\phi_{I}(w(\mathbf{n})))=\psi(w(\mathbf{n}))=w(\mathbf{n})
. Again

\psi(\phi_{I}(v(\mathbf{n})))=\psi(v^{1}(\mathbf{n})+v^{2}(\mathbf{n}))=\displaystyle{\sum_{f\in\mathcal{E}_{1}\cap\Lambda^{\mathbf{e}_{j}}}}s(f)(\mathbf{n}+\mathbf{e}_{j})+\displaystyle{\sum_{g\in\mathcal{E}_{2}\cap\Lambda^{\mathbf{e}_{j}}}}s(g)(\mathbf{n}+\mathbf{e}_{j})=\displaystyle{\sum_{h\in v\Lambda^{\mathbf{e}_{j}}}}s(h)(\mathbf{n}+\mathbf{e}_{j})=v(\mathbf{n}).

On the other hand for
u\neq v^{1},v^{2}
,
\phi_{I}(\psi(u(\mathbf{n})))=\phi_{I}(u(\mathbf{n}))=u(\mathbf{n})
and for
t=1,2

\displaystyle\phi_{I}(\psi(v^{t}(\mathbf{n})))

\displaystyle=\phi_{I}\left(\displaystyle{\sum_{f\in\mathcal{E}_{t}\cap\Lambda^{\mathbf{e}_{j}}}}s(f)(\mathbf{n}+\mathbf{e}_{j})\right)

\displaystyle=\displaystyle{\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}f\in\mathcal{E}_{t}\cap\Lambda^{\mathbf{e}_{j}}\\
s(f)\neq v\end{subarray}}}s(f)(\mathbf{n}+\mathbf{e}_{j})+\displaystyle{\sum_{f\in\mathcal{E}_{t}\cap\Lambda^{\mathbf{e}_{j}}v}}(v^{1}(\mathbf{n}+\mathbf{e}_{j})+v^{2}(\mathbf{n}+\mathbf{e}_{j}))

\displaystyle=\displaystyle{\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\alpha\in v^{t}\Lambda_{I}^{\mathbf{e}_{j}}\\
s_{I}(\alpha)\neq v^{1},v^{2}\end{subarray}}}s_{I}(\alpha)(\mathbf{n}+\mathbf{e}_{j})+\displaystyle{\sum_{\alpha\in v^{t}\Lambda_{I}^{\mathbf{e}_{j}}v^{1}}}s_{I}(\alpha)(\mathbf{n}+\mathbf{e}_{j})+\displaystyle{\sum_{\alpha\in v^{t}\Lambda_{I}^{\mathbf{e}_{j}}v^{2}}}s_{I}(\alpha)(\mathbf{n}+\mathbf{e}_{j})

\displaystyle=\displaystyle{\sum_{\alpha\in v^{t}\Lambda_{I}^{\mathbf{e}_{j}}}}s_{I}(\alpha)(\mathbf{n}+\mathbf{e}_{j})

\displaystyle=v^{t}(\mathbf{n})~~(\text{using relations of}~T_{\Lambda_{I}}).

∎

3.2.
Sink deletion

If a directed graph
E
has a source
v
which is also a regular vertex, then deleting
v
and all the edges emanating from
v
, one obtains the
source elimination graph

E_{\setminus v}
. Similar to in-splitting and out-splitting, the move of source elimination also does not change the graded Morita equivalent class of the corresponding Leavitt path algebra (see
[
28
, Proposition 13]
). The directions of paths are reversed in the transition from directed graphs to path categories and so at the level of
k
-graphs, the analogue of the source elimination process is
sink deletion
. We briefly review the process as described in
[
21
, Section 5]
.

A vertex
v\in\Lambda^{0}
is called an
\mathbf{e}_{i}
-sink
(where
1\leq i\leq k
) if
\Lambda^{\mathbf{e}_{i}}v=\emptyset
. Notice that an
\mathbf{e}_{i}
-sink
v
may emit edges of other degrees and if that happens then
v
is not the only
\mathbf{e}_{i}
-sink present in the
k
-graph. It is not hard to observe that in this case, any
w\in\Lambda^{0}
such that
w\Lambda v\neq\emptyset
is also an
\mathbf{e}_{i}
-sink (see
[
21
, Lemma 5.3]
). Keeping this in mind, the move of sink deletion at
v
not only removes
v
but also removes all its out-neighbours. More precisely, the
sink deletion
k
-graph
of
\Lambda
is defined as

\Lambda_{S}:=\{\lambda\in\Lambda~|~r(\lambda)\Lambda v=\emptyset\}.

Clearly
\Lambda_{S}^{0}=\{w\in\Lambda^{0}~|~w\Lambda v=\emptyset\}
. The source, range and degree maps are denoted by
s_{S}
,
r_{S}
and
d_{S}
respectively, and they are the restrictions of
s
,
r
and
d
to
\Lambda_{S}
. By
[
21
, Theorem 5.4]

(\Lambda_{S},d_{S})
is also a row-finite
k
-graph having no sources.

The following proposition shows that sink deletion leaves invariant the graded Morita equivalence class of Kumjian-Pask algebras. For the
C^{*}
-algebra analogue, see
[
21
, Theorem 5.5]
.

Proposition 3.3
.

Let
\Lambda
be a row-finite
k
-graph without sources,
v
an
\mathbf{e}_{i}
-sink in
\Lambda
and
\Lambda_{S}
the sink deletion
k
-graph obtained by deleting
v
. Suppose
|\Lambda^{0}|<\infty
. Then for any field
\mathsf{k}
, the Kumjian-Pask algebras
\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Lambda)
and
\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Lambda_{S})
are graded Morita equivalent.

Proof.

Suppose
(p,s)
and
(q,t)
are the respective Kumjian-Pask families in
\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Lambda)
and
\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Lambda_{S})
. Let
H:=\Lambda_{S}^{0}
. Let
\lambda\in\Lambda
be such that
r(\lambda)\in H
. So
r(\Lambda)\Lambda v=\emptyset
. This implies
s(\lambda)\Lambda v=\emptyset
i.e.,
s(\lambda)\in H
. Therefore,
H
is a hereditary subset of
\Lambda^{0}
. Notice that
\Lambda_{S}=\Lambda_{H}
where
\Lambda_{H}
is the
k
-graph defined as:

\Lambda_{H}:=\{\lambda\in\Lambda~|~r(\lambda)\in H\}.

Then the assignment

q_{v}\longmapsto p_{v},~~~t_{\lambda}\longmapsto s_{\lambda},~~~t_{\lambda^{*}}\longmapsto s_{\lambda^{*}},

extends to a
\mathbb{Z}^{k}
-graded injective homomorphism
\varphi
from
\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Lambda_{S})
to
\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Lambda)
and
\operatorname{Im}(\varphi)=p\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Lambda)p
where
p=\displaystyle{\sum_{w\in\Lambda_{S}^{0}}}p_{w}
. We are not going to prove it here, as it will be exactly the same as the Leavitt path algebra case given in
[
1
, Proposition 2.2.22
(i)
and
(ii)
]
. Hence,
\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Lambda_{S})\cong p\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Lambda)p
as
\mathbb{Z}^{k}
-graded rings. Clearly,
p
is a homogeneous idempotent of
\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Lambda)
. We claim that
p
is a full idempotent. First, observe that
\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Lambda)p\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Lambda)=I_{H}
, the ideal of
\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Lambda)
generated by
\{p_{u}~|~u\in H\}
. This is because for every
u\in H
,
p_{u}=p_{u}pp_{u}
and so
I_{H}\subseteq\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Lambda)p\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Lambda)
, the other inclusion is trivial as
p\in I_{H}
. Again, notice that the saturated closure of
H
is
\Lambda^{0}
. To confirm this, choose any
u\in\Lambda^{0}\setminus\Lambda_{S}^{0}
and
\alpha\in u\Lambda^{\mathbf{e}_{i}}
. Then
s(\alpha)\Lambda v=\emptyset
otherwise
s(\alpha)
turns out to be an
\mathbf{e}_{i}
-sink, which is a contradiction. Thus
s(u\Lambda^{\mathbf{e}_{i}})\subseteq H
and consequently
u\in\overline{H}
. Putting these together, we have

\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Lambda)p\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Lambda)=I_{H}=I_{\Lambda^{0}}=\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Lambda).

So our claim is proved. Therefore, the corner
p\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Lambda)p
and
\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Lambda)
are graded Morita equivalent and the result follows by applying
[
28
, Theorem 3]
.
∎

We now show that the talented monoid of
\Lambda
remains invariant under the move of sink deletion. The following theorem should be viewed as the
k
-graph analogue of
[
18
, Proposition 4.2]
.

Theorem 3.4
.

Let
\Lambda
be a row-finite
k
-graph without sources,
v
an
\mathbf{e}_{i}
-sink and
\Lambda_{S}
the sink deletion
k
-graph. Then there is a
\mathbb{Z}^{k}
-monoid isomorphism
\phi_{S}:T_{\Lambda_{S}}\longrightarrow T_{\Lambda}
such that
\phi_{S}(w(\mathbf{n}))=w(\mathbf{n})
for all
w\in\Lambda_{S}^{0}
and
\mathbf{n}\in\mathbb{Z}^{k}
.

Proof.

The assignment
w(\mathbf{n})\longmapsto w(\mathbf{n})
when extended linearly to all of
T_{\Lambda_{S}}
gives a well-defined
\mathbb{Z}^{k}
-monoid homomorphism
\phi_{S}:T_{\Lambda_{S}}\longrightarrow T_{\Lambda}
. This is easy to prove since for any
w\in\Lambda_{S}^{0}
,
\mathbf{n}\in\mathbb{Z}^{k}
and
\mathbf{m}\in\mathbb{N}^{k}
we have

\phi_{S}(w(\mathbf{n}))=w(\mathbf{n})=\displaystyle{\sum_{\lambda\in w\Lambda^{\mathbf{m}}}}s(\lambda)(\mathbf{n}+\mathbf{m})=\displaystyle{\sum_{\lambda\in w\Lambda_{S}^{\mathbf{m}}}}s(\lambda)(\mathbf{n}+\mathbf{m})=\phi_{S}\left(\displaystyle{\sum_{\lambda\in w\Lambda_{S}^{\mathbf{m}}}}s(\lambda)(\mathbf{n}+\mathbf{m})\right).

Suppose
x,y\in T_{\Lambda_{S}}
are such that
\phi_{S}(x)=\phi_{S}(y)
. Let
x=\displaystyle{\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}}u_{i}(\mathbf{p}_{i})
and
y=\displaystyle{\sum_{j=1}^{t}}w_{j}(\mathbf{q}_{j})
.
Then in
T_{\Lambda}
we have

\displaystyle{\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}}u_{i}(\mathbf{p}_{i})=\displaystyle{\sum_{j=1}^{t}}w_{j}(\mathbf{q}_{j}).

Passing this equality to
M_{\overline{\Lambda}}
(where
\overline{\Lambda}
is the skew product
k
-graph) and using the confluence lemma (see
[
32
, Lemma 3.5]
),
we have
\gamma\in\mathbb{F}_{\overline{\Lambda}}\setminus\{0\}
such that

\displaystyle{\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}}(u_{i},\mathbf{p}_{i})\longrightarrow\gamma~\text{and}~\displaystyle{\sum_{j=1}^{t}}(w_{j},\mathbf{q}_{j})\longrightarrow\gamma.

Since
u_{i},w_{j}\in\Lambda_{S}^{0}
for all
i=1,2,...,\ell
,
j=1,2,...,t
and
\Lambda_{S}^{0}
is hereditary, so every vertex in the support of
\gamma
is in fact a vertex of
\overline{\Lambda_{S}}
. Therefore, by applying the confluence lemma again, we have

\displaystyle{\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}}(u_{i},\mathbf{p}_{i})=\displaystyle{\sum_{j=1}^{t}}(w_{j},\mathbf{q}_{j})

in
M_{\overline{\Lambda_{S}}}
and so
x=y
in
T_{\Lambda_{S}}
. This proves that
\phi_{S}
is injective. For surjectivity, it suffices to show that
u(\mathbf{n})\in\operatorname{Im}(\phi_{S})
for all
u\in\Lambda^{0}\setminus\Lambda_{S}^{0}
and
n\in\mathbb{Z}^{k}
. For each such
u
and
\mathbf{n}
we can write

u(\mathbf{n})=\displaystyle{\sum_{\alpha\in u\Lambda^{\mathbf{e}_{i}}}}s(\alpha)(\mathbf{n}+\mathbf{e}_{i})

in
T_{\Lambda}
. The sum is nonempty since
\Lambda
has no sources. If
\alpha\in u\Lambda^{\mathbf{e}_{i}}
then
s(\alpha)\Lambda v=\emptyset
otherwise
s(\alpha)
becomes an
\mathbf{e}_{i}
-sink by
[
21
, Lemma 5.3]
, which is obviously not the case. This implies
s(\alpha)\in\Lambda_{S}^{0}
for all
\alpha\in u\Lambda^{\mathbf{e}_{i}}
and so

u(\mathbf{n})=\displaystyle{\sum_{\alpha\in u\Lambda^{\mathbf{e}_{i}}}}s(\alpha)(\mathbf{n}+\mathbf{e}_{i})=\phi_{S}\left(\displaystyle{\sum_{\alpha\in u\Lambda^{\mathbf{e}_{i}}}}s(\alpha)(\mathbf{n}+\mathbf{e}_{i})\right)\in\operatorname{Im}(\phi_{S}).

∎

We conclude this section by giving an example which illustrate our results on the invariance of talented monoid under the two
k
-graph moves we have discussed.

Example 3.5
.

Suppose the following is the
1
-skeleton of a
2
-graph
\Lambda
:

.
u
v
w
f
f_{1}
f_{2}
g
e
e^{\prime}
h_{1}
h_{2}
h

with the factorization rules given as follows:

(i)

u-u
bicoloured paths:
ef=fe
,
e^{\prime}f=fe^{\prime}
;

(ii)

u-w
bicoloured paths:
hf_{1}=ge
,
hf_{2}=ge^{\prime}
;

(iii)

u-v
bicoloured paths:
h_{1}f_{1}=f_{2}e
,
h_{1}f_{2}=f_{2}e^{\prime}
,
h_{2}f_{1}=f_{1}e
,
h_{2}f_{2}=f_{1}e^{\prime}
.

We now in-split
\Lambda
at the vertex
v
. Notice that there is only one possible partition (up to indexing) of
v\Lambda^{\mathbf{1}}
which satisfies the pairing condition, namely
\mathcal{E}_{1}=\{h_{1},f_{2}\}
and
\mathcal{E}_{2}=\{h_{2},f_{1}\}
. The in-split
2
-graph
\Lambda_{I}
has the following
1
-skeleton:

.
u
w
v^{1}
v^{2}
f
g
h_{1}^{2}
h_{2}^{1}
f_{2}
f_{1}
e
e^{\prime}
h_{1}^{1}
h_{2}^{2}
h^{1}
h^{2}

Again,
v
is an
(0,1)
-sink (red edges are of degree
(0,1)
) in
\Lambda
. Carrying out the sink deletion process, we obtain the
2
-graph
\Lambda_{S}
with the following
1
-skeleton:

.
u
f
e
e^{\prime}

By Theorem
3.2
and Theorem
3.4
,
T_{\Lambda}\cong T_{\Lambda_{I}}\cong T_{\Lambda_{S}}
. It is rather easy to compute
T_{\Lambda_{S}}
. In
T_{\Lambda_{S}}
we have
u((i,j))=u((i,0))
and
u((i,j))=2u((i+1,j))
for each
i,j\in\mathbb{Z}
. One can easily deduce that
T_{\Lambda_{S}}
is
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
-isomorphic to the commutative monoid

\mathbb{N}\left[\frac{1}{2}\right]:=\left\{\frac{m}{2^{t}}~:~m\in\mathbb{N},t\in\mathbb{Z}\right\}

where the
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
-action is given as:
{}^{(i,j)}\frac{m}{2^{t}}:=\frac{m}{2^{t+i}}
for all
(i,j)\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}
.

4.
Relationship between graded
K
-theory and graded homology

In this section, we investigate the relationship between the graded
K
-theory of a Kumjian-Pask algebra and the graded homology theory of the infinite path groupoid associated with the underlying
k
-graph. Before going to the details, let us briefly recall homology of étale groupoids, first introduced by Crainic and Moerdijk
[
20
]
. The readers may go through
[
38
,
39
]
for more insights on the homology theory of groupoids.

4.1.
Graded homology of a graded étale groupoid

Let
\mathcal{G}
be an étale groupoid. For each positive integer
n
, write

\mathcal{G}^{(n)}:=\{(g_{1},g_{2},...,g_{n})\in\mathcal{G}^{n}~|~s(g_{i})=r(g_{i+1})~\text{for all}~i=1,2,...,n-1\},

and define
n+1
maps
d_{i}^{(n)}:\mathcal{G}^{(n)}\longrightarrow\mathcal{G}^{(n-1)}
,
i=0,1,2,...,n
by

d_{i}^{(n)}((g_{1},g_{2},...,g_{n})):=\left\{\begin{array}[]{lll}(g_{2},g_{3},...,g_{n})&\mbox{if }i=0,\\
(g_{1},g_{2},...,g_{i}g_{i+1},...,g_{n})&\mbox{if }1\leq i\leq n-1,\\
(g_{1},g_{2},...,g_{n-1})&\mbox{if }i=n.\end{array}\right.

where
d_{0}^{(1)},d_{1}^{(1)}:\mathcal{G}^{(1)}\longrightarrow\mathcal{G}^{(0)}
are defined to be the
source
and
range
maps, respectively. For any topological abelian group
A
, suppose
C_{c}(\mathcal{G}^{(n)},A)
is the set of all continuous functions
f:\mathcal{G}^{(n)}\longrightarrow A
with compact support. Clearly, each
C_{c}(\mathcal{G}^{(n)},A)
is an abelian group. Define the
n^{\text{th}}

boundary map
to be the group homomorphism
\partial_{n}:C_{c}(\mathcal{G}^{(n)},A)\longrightarrow C_{c}(\mathcal{G}^{(n-1)},A)
by

\partial_{n}:=\displaystyle{\sum_{i=1}^{n}}(-1)^{i}d_{i*}^{(n)},

where
