d_{i*}^{(n)}:C_{c}(\mathcal{G}^{(n)},A)\longrightarrow C_{c}(\mathcal{G}^{(n-1)},A)
is the map given by
f\longmapsto d_{i*}^{(n)}(f)
where

(d_{i*}^{(n)}(f))(y):=\displaystyle{\sum_{d_{i}^{(n)}(x)=y}}f(x),

for all
y\in\mathcal{G}^{(n-1)}
. Then

0\overset{\partial_{0}}{\longleftarrow}C_{c}(\mathcal{G}^{(0)},A)\overset{\partial_{1}}{\longleftarrow}C_{c}(\mathcal{G}^{(1)},A)\overset{\partial_{2}}{\longleftarrow}C_{c}(\mathcal{G}^{(2)},A)\overset{\partial_{3}}{\longleftarrow}\cdots

is a chain complex, called the
Moore complex
of the simplicial abelian group
(C_{c}(\mathcal{G}^{(n)},A))_{n}
. The
n^{\text{th}}
homology group
of
\mathcal{G}
with constant coefficients
A
is defined as:

H_{n}(\mathcal{G},A):=\ker\partial_{n}/\operatorname{Im}\partial_{n+1}.

If we take
A=\mathbb{Z}
, the group of integers with discrete topology, then we simply write the
n^{\text{th}}
homology
H_{n}(\mathcal{G},\mathbb{Z})
as
H_{n}(\mathcal{G})
and call it the
n^{\text{th}}

integral homology
of
\mathcal{G}
. In particular,
H_{0}(\mathcal{G})=C_{c}(\mathcal{G}^{(0)},\mathbb{Z})/\operatorname{Im}\partial_{1}
. Also define

H_{0}(\mathcal{G})^{+}:=\{[f]\in H_{0}(\mathcal{G})~|~f(u)\geq 0~\text{for all}~u\in\mathcal{G}^{(0)}\},

where
[f]=f+\operatorname{Im}\partial_{1}
. Then
H_{0}(\mathcal{G})^{+}
is a commutative cancellative monoid and
(H_{0}(\mathcal{G}),H_{0}(\mathcal{G})^{+})
is a pre-ordered abelian group.

If
\Gamma
is a group with discrete topology acting continuously from left on the groupoid
\mathcal{G}
i.e., there is a group homomorphism
\phi:\Gamma\longrightarrow\operatorname{Aut}(\mathcal{G})
, then each
C_{c}(\mathcal{G}^{(n)},A)
is a
\Gamma
-module with respect to the following
\Gamma
-action:

\gamma\cdot f:=f\circ\phi_{\gamma^{-1}}.

Also
\partial_{n}
’s are
\Gamma
-module homomorphisms and consequently
H_{n}(\mathcal{G},A)
is a
\Gamma
-module.

We now present the definition of graded homology of an étale groupoid.

Definition 4.1
.

(
[
30
, Definition 5.3]
) Let
\mathcal{G}
be a
\Gamma
-graded étale groupoid graded by the continuous cocycle
c:\mathcal{G}\longrightarrow\Gamma
. For each
n\geq 0
, the
n^{\text{th}}
graded (integral) homology
of
\mathcal{G}
is defined as:

H_{n}^{\operatorname{gr}}(\mathcal{G}):=H_{n}(\mathcal{G}\times_{c}\Gamma).

Also, define

H_{0}^{\operatorname{gr}}(\mathcal{G})^{+}:=H_{0}(\mathcal{G}\times_{c}\Gamma)^{+}.

Here
\mathcal{G}\times_{c}\Gamma
is the skew-product of
\mathcal{G}
with
\Gamma
(see
[
44
, Definition 1.6]
). Since the skew-product groupoid
\mathcal{G}\times_{c}\Gamma
is equipped with a natural
\Gamma
-action:
~{}^{\gamma}(g,\alpha):=(g,\gamma\alpha)
, by the discussion above,
H_{n}^{\operatorname{gr}}(\mathcal{G})
is a left
\Gamma
-module.

4.2.
Graded zeroth homology of the infinite path groupoid

Given a
k
-graph
\Lambda
with degree functor
d
, the associated infinite path groupoid
\mathcal{G}_{\Lambda}
is canonically
\mathbb{Z}^{k}
-graded via the continuous cocycle

\displaystyle\tilde{d}:\mathcal{G}_{\Lambda}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\mathbb{Z}^{k}

\displaystyle(x,\mathbf{n},y)

\displaystyle\longmapsto\mathbf{n}

Therefore
H_{n}^{\operatorname{gr}}(\mathcal{G}_{\Lambda})=H_{n}(\mathcal{G}_{\Lambda}\times_{\tilde{d}}\mathbb{Z}^{k})
.

When
\mathcal{G}
is a strongly
\Gamma
-graded groupoid, one can relate the graded homology of
\mathcal{G}
to the usual (non-graded) homology of
\mathcal{G}_{\epsilon}
by
[
30
, Theorem 5.4]
, where
\epsilon
is the identity of
\Gamma
and
\mathcal{G}_{\epsilon}
is the
\epsilon^{\text{th}}
homogeneous component of
\mathcal{G}
. In the following proposition, we specialise this to the case of the infinite path groupoid of a
k
-graph
\Lambda
.

Proposition 4.2
.

For any row-finite
k
-graph
\Lambda
without sources, the following are equivalent.

(i)

\Lambda
satisfies Condition
(Y)
of
[
15
, Definition 4.10]
.

(ii)

\mathcal{G}_{\Lambda}
is a strongly
\mathbb{Z}^{k}
-graded groupoid.

(iii)

Y:=\mathcal{G}_{\Lambda}^{(0)}\times\{0\}
is a
(\mathcal{G}_{\Lambda}\times_{\tilde{d}}\mathbb{Z}^{k})
-full clopen subset of
(\mathcal{G}_{\Lambda}\times_{\tilde{d}}\mathbb{Z}^{k})^{(0)}
.

If any of the above holds, then
H_{n}^{\operatorname{gr}}(\mathcal{G}_{\Lambda})\cong H_{n}((\mathcal{G}_{\Lambda})_{0})
for any
n\geq 0
and
H_{0}^{\operatorname{gr}}(\mathcal{G}_{\Lambda})^{+}\cong H_{0}((\mathcal{G}_{\Lambda})_{0})^{+}
.

Proof.

(i)\Longrightarrow(ii)
by
[
15
, Theorem 4.12]
. Suppose
(ii)
holds.
Y
is clearly a clopen subset of
(\mathcal{G}_{\Lambda}\times_{\tilde{d}}\mathbb{Z}^{k})^{(0)}
. To show the fullness of
Y
, take any
((x,0,x),\mathbf{m})\in(\mathcal{G}_{\Lambda}\times_{\tilde{d}}\mathbb{Z}^{k})^{(0)}
. We can write
(x,0,x)=(x,-\mathbf{m},y)(y,\mathbf{m},x)
for some
y\in\Lambda^{\infty}
since
\mathcal{G}_{\Lambda}
is strongly
\mathbb{Z}^{k}
-graded. Then
((x,-\mathbf{m},y),\mathbf{m})\in\mathcal{G}_{\Lambda}\times_{\tilde{d}}\mathbb{Z}^{k}
,
r(((x,-\mathbf{m},y),\mathbf{m}))=((x,0,x),\mathbf{m})
and
s(((x,-\mathbf{m},y),\mathbf{m}))=((y,0,y),0)\in Y
. Hence,
Y
is
(\mathcal{G}_{\Lambda}\times_{\tilde{d}}\mathbb{Z}^{k})
-full and
(iii)
follows.

Suppose
(iii)
holds. Take any
\mathbf{m}\in\mathbb{N}^{k}
and
x\in\Lambda^{\infty}
. Then
((x,0,x),\mathbf{m})\in(\mathcal{G}_{\Lambda}\times_{\tilde{d}}\mathbb{Z}^{k})^{(0)}
. By fullness of
Y
, there exists
((x,\mathbf{n}-\mathbf{q},y),\mathbf{m})\in\mathcal{G}_{\Lambda}\times_{\tilde{d}}\mathbb{Z}^{k}
such that
s(((x,\mathbf{n}-\mathbf{q},y),\mathbf{m}))\in Y
. It follows that
\sigma^{\mathbf{n}}(x)=\sigma^{\mathbf{q}}(y)
and
\mathbf{n}-\mathbf{q}+\mathbf{m}=0
. Let
\beta=y(0,\mathbf{q})
. Then
s(\beta)=y(\mathbf{q})=\sigma^{\mathbf{q}}(y)(0)=\sigma^{\mathbf{n}}(x)(0)=x(\mathbf{n})
and
d(\beta)-\mathbf{n}=\mathbf{q}-\mathbf{n}=\mathbf{m}
. So
\Lambda
satisfies Condition
(Y)
.

For the remaining part, note that the unit space
(\mathcal{G}_{\Lambda}\times_{\tilde{d}}\mathbb{Z}^{k})^{(0)}=\mathcal{G}_{\Lambda}^{(0)}\times\mathbb{Z}^{k}
is
\sigma
-compact since
\Lambda^{0}
and
\mathbb{Z}^{k}
are countable,
Z(v)
’s are compact and

\mathcal{G}_{\Lambda}^{(0)}\times\mathbb{Z}^{k}=\displaystyle{\bigcup_{v\in\Lambda^{0},\mathbf{n}\in\mathbb{Z}^{k}}}Z(v)\times\{\mathbf{n}\}.

Also since
\mathcal{G}_{\Lambda}\times_{\tilde{d}}\mathbb{Z}^{k}
is ample, the unit space is totally disconnected. Thus, by
[
38
, Theorem 3.6 (2)]
,
\mathcal{G}_{\Lambda}\times_{\tilde{d}}\mathbb{Z}^{k}
is homologically similar to
(\mathcal{G}_{\Lambda}\times_{\tilde{d}}\mathbb{Z}^{k})|_{Y}
. Again
(\mathcal{G}_{\Lambda}\times_{\tilde{d}}\mathbb{Z}^{k})|_{Y}\cong(\mathcal{G}_{\Lambda})_{0}
via the canonical isomorphism which maps
((x,0,y),0)
to
(x,0,y)
. Therefore,
\mathcal{G}_{\Lambda}\times_{\tilde{d}}\mathbb{Z}^{k}
is homologically similar to
(\mathcal{G}_{\Lambda})_{0}
and we are done by
[
38
, Theorem 3.5 (2)]
.
∎

To relate the graded homology of the graph groupoid
\mathcal{G}_{E}
of a directed graph
E
with the graded
K
-theory of the corresponding Leavitt path algebra
L(E)
, the first named author and Li used
\mathcal{D}_{\mathbb{Z}}(E)
, the
diagonal subalgebra
of
L_{\mathbb{Z}}(E)
(the Leavitt path algebra of
E
with integral coefficients). They realised
\mathcal{D}_{\mathbb{Z}}(E)
as a certain quotient of the free abelian group generated by the elements of the form
\alpha\alpha^{*}
,
\alpha\in E^{*}
(see
[
30
, Lemma 6.4]
). The proof uses the fact that Leavitt path algebra of any directed graph has a specific basis
[
30
, Lemma 6.2]
. Unfortunately, we do not have a prescribed basis for the Kumjian-Pask algebra of a row-finite
k
-graph. We instead follow a different approach. Below we define the
path group
associated with a
k
-graph
\Lambda
which will be used in the sequel as a bridge to set up our desired relationship between the graded homology of
\mathcal{G}_{\Lambda}
and
K_{0}^{\operatorname{gr}}(\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Lambda))
.

Definition 4.3
.

Let
\Lambda
be a row-finite
k
-graph without sources. The
path group
of
\Lambda
is denoted by
\mathcal{P}(\Lambda)
and is defined as the free abelian group generated by
\Lambda
subject to the relations:

(2)

\lambda=\displaystyle{\sum_{\alpha\in s(\lambda)\Lambda^{\mathbf{n}}}}\lambda\alpha

for all
\lambda\in\Lambda
and
\mathbf{n}\in\mathbb{N}^{k}
.

We will show that as abelian groups
\mathcal{P}(\Lambda)\cong C_{c}(\mathcal{G}_{\Lambda}^{(0)},\mathbb{Z})
, where
\mathcal{G}_{\Lambda}
is the path groupoid associated to
\Lambda
. For this we need the following lemma.

Lemma 4.4
.

Let
\lambda\in\Lambda
be such that
Z(\lambda)=\displaystyle{\bigsqcup_{i=1}^{t}}~Z(\mu_{i})
for some
t\geq 1
and
\mu_{i}\in\Lambda
for each
i=1,2,...,t
. Then
\lambda=\displaystyle{\sum_{i=1}^{t}}\mu_{i}
in
\mathcal{P}(\Lambda)
.

Proof.

For each
i=1,2,...,t
we have

Z(\mu_{i})=Z(\mu_{i})\cap Z(\lambda)=\displaystyle{\bigsqcup_{\beta\in\operatorname{Ext}(\mu_{i},\{\lambda\})}}Z(\mu_{i}\beta)=\displaystyle{\bigsqcup_{\alpha\in\operatorname{Ext}(\lambda,\{\mu_{i}\})}}Z(\lambda\alpha).

The second equality follows from
[
32
, Lemma 3.17
(i)
]
and the last equality follows since for each
\alpha\in\operatorname{Ext}(\lambda,\{\mu_{i}\})
there is a unique
\beta\in\operatorname{Ext}(\mu_{i},\{\lambda\})
such that
\lambda\alpha=\mu_{i}\beta
and conversely. Thus

\displaystyle{\bigsqcup_{\nu\in s(\mu_{i})\Lambda^{(d(\mu_{i})\vee d(\lambda))-d(\mu_{i})}}}Z(\mu_{i}\nu)=\displaystyle{\bigsqcup_{\beta\in\operatorname{Ext}(\mu_{i},\{\lambda\})}}Z(\mu_{i}\beta).

Clearly
\operatorname{Ext}(\mu_{i},\{\lambda\})\subseteq s(\mu_{i})\Lambda^{(d(\mu_{i})\vee d(\lambda))-d(\mu_{i})}
. From the above equality, it follows that the reverse inclusion also holds. Indeed, if we take
\nu\in s(\mu_{i})\Lambda^{(d(\mu_{i})\vee d(\lambda))-d(\mu_{i})}
and any
x\in s(\nu)\Lambda^{\infty}
, then there is a unique
\beta\in\operatorname{Ext}(\mu_{i},\{\lambda\})
such that
\mu_{i}\nu x\in Z(\mu_{i}\beta)
which implies
\mu_{i}\nu x=\mu_{i}\beta y
for some
y\in s(\beta)\Lambda^{\infty}
. But since
d(\nu)=d(\beta)
, it must happen that
\nu=\beta
. So
\operatorname{Ext}(\mu_{i},\{\lambda\})=s(\mu_{i})\Lambda^{(d(\mu_{i})\vee d(\lambda))-d(\mu_{i})}
. Let

\mathbf{m}:=\displaystyle{\bigvee_{i=1}^{t}}\{(d(\lambda)\vee d(\mu_{i}))-d(\lambda)\}=\displaystyle{\bigvee_{i=1}^{t}}(d(\lambda)\vee d(\mu_{i}))-d(\lambda).

Now in
\mathcal{P}(\Lambda)
we have

\displaystyle\displaystyle{\sum_{i=1}^{t}}\mu_{i}

\displaystyle=\displaystyle{\sum_{i=1}^{t}}\left(\displaystyle{\sum_{\beta\in s(\mu_{i})\Lambda^{(d(\mu_{i})\vee d(\lambda))-d(\mu_{i})}}}\mu_{i}\beta\right)~(\text{by defining relations (\ref{pathrel})})

\displaystyle=\displaystyle{\sum_{i=1}^{t}}\left(\displaystyle{\sum_{\beta\in\operatorname{Ext}(\mu_{i},\{\lambda\})}}\mu_{i}\beta\right)

\displaystyle=\displaystyle{\sum_{i=1}^{t}}\left(\displaystyle{\sum_{\alpha\in\operatorname{Ext}(\lambda,\{\mu_{i}\})}}\lambda\alpha\right)

\displaystyle=\displaystyle{\sum_{i=1}^{t}}\left(\displaystyle{\sum_{\alpha\in\operatorname{Ext}(\lambda,\{\mu_{i}\})}}\left(\displaystyle{\sum_{\gamma\in s(\alpha)\Lambda^{\mathbf{m}-d(\alpha)}}}\lambda\alpha\gamma\right)\right)~(\text{by defining relations (\ref{pathrel})})

\displaystyle=\displaystyle{\sum_{\delta\in s(\lambda)\Lambda^{\mathbf{m}}}}\lambda\delta=\lambda~(\text{by defining relations (\ref{pathrel})})

It remains to explain how the last but one equality holds. If
i\neq j
then it is clear that
\operatorname{Ext}(\lambda,\{\mu_{i}\})\cap\operatorname{Ext}(\lambda,\{\mu_{j}\})=\emptyset
otherwise
Z(\mu_{i})\cap Z(\mu_{j})\neq\emptyset
. Again, for the same reason, it can’t happen that
\alpha\gamma=\alpha^{\prime}\gamma^{\prime}
for
\alpha\in\operatorname{Ext}(\lambda,\{\mu_{i}\})
,
\alpha^{\prime}\in\operatorname{Ext}(\lambda,\{\mu_{j}\})
,
\gamma\in s(\alpha)\Lambda^{\mathbf{m}-d(\alpha)}
and
\gamma^{\prime}\in s(\alpha^{\prime})\Lambda^{\mathbf{m}-d(\alpha^{\prime})}
. Thus all the summand in line
4
above are distinct. Also for each
i=1,2,\ldots,t
,
\alpha\in\operatorname{Ext}(\lambda,\{\mu_{i}\})
and
\gamma\in s(\alpha)\Lambda^{\mathbf{m}-d(\alpha)}
,
d(\alpha\gamma)=\mathbf{m}
which implies
\alpha\gamma\in s(\lambda)\Lambda^{\mathbf{m}}
. On the other hand, if
\delta\in s(\lambda)\Lambda^{\mathbf{m}}
then since
d(\mu_{i})\leq d(\lambda\delta)
and
Z(\lambda\delta)\subseteq Z(\lambda)=\displaystyle{\bigsqcup_{i=1}^{t}}~Z(\mu_{i})
, it follows that there is a unique
i
such that
(\lambda\delta)(0,d(\mu_{i}))=\mu_{i}
. So
\lambda\delta=\mu_{i}\beta
for some
\beta\in\Lambda
. Since
d(\lambda\delta)\geq d(\lambda)\vee d(\mu_{i})
, there should exist
\alpha,\beta^{\prime}\in\Lambda
such that
(\lambda\delta)(0,d(\lambda)\vee d(\mu_{i}))=\lambda\alpha
and
(\mu_{i}\beta)(0,d(\lambda)\vee d(\mu_{i}))=\mu_{i}\beta^{\prime}
whence
\lambda\alpha=\mu_{i}\beta^{\prime}
and
\alpha\in\operatorname{Ext}(\lambda,\{\mu_{i}\})
. Then
\delta=\alpha\gamma
for some
\gamma\in s(\alpha)\Lambda^{\mathbf{m}-d(\alpha)}
which proves that each
\delta\in s(\lambda)\Lambda^{\mathbf{m}}
is in the list of summand in line
4
.
∎

Proposition 4.5
.

Let
\Lambda
be any row-finite
k
-graph without sources and
\mathbb{Z}
, the group of integers equipped with discrete topology. Then the map
1_{Z(\lambda)}\mapsto\lambda
extends to an isomorphism
\phi:C_{c}(\mathcal{G}_{\Lambda}^{(0)},\mathbb{Z})\to\mathcal{P}(\Lambda)
.

Proof.

First, note that any
f\in C_{c}(\mathcal{G}_{\Lambda}^{(0)},\mathbb{Z})
can be written as
f=\displaystyle{\sum_{U\in\mathcal{F}}}a_{U}1_{U}
, where
\mathcal{F}
is a finite collection (empty if
f=0
) of mutually disjoint compact open subsets of
\mathcal{G}_{\Lambda}^{(0)}
and
a_{U}\in\mathbb{Z}\setminus\{0\}
for all
U\in\mathcal{F}
(precisely,
U=f^{-1}(a_{U})
). The set
\mathcal{B}:=\{Z(\lambda):\lambda\in\Lambda\}
forms a basis of compact sets for the topology of
\mathcal{G}_{\Lambda}^{(0)}
. Using this fact together with
[
32
, Lemma 3.17]
, we can decompose each
U\in\mathcal{F}
as a disjoint union
U=\displaystyle{\bigsqcup_{i=1}^{\ell_{U}}}~Z(\lambda_{\nu_{i}})
. So
f
can eventually be expressed as

f=\displaystyle{\sum_{U\in\mathcal{F}}}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{\ell_{U}}a_{U}1_{Z(\lambda_{\nu_{i}})}\right).

This shows that each
f\in C_{c}(\mathcal{G}_{\Lambda}^{(0)},\mathbb{Z})
can be written as a
\mathbb{Z}
-linear combination of characteristic functions of mutually disjoint basic open subsets. Define a map
\phi:C_{c}(\mathcal{G}_{\Lambda}^{(0)},\mathbb{Z})\longrightarrow\mathcal{P}(\Lambda)
by extending the rule

1_{Z(\lambda)}\longmapsto\lambda,

linearly to all
\mathbb{Z}
-linear combinations of characteristic functions of mutually disjoint basic open sets. We need to check that this gives a well-defined group homomorphism. We first observe that if
Z(\lambda)=\displaystyle{\bigsqcup_{i=1}^{m}}~U_{i}
where
U_{i}
’s are mutually disjoint compact open subsets of
\mathcal{G}_{\Lambda}^{(0)}
then
\phi(1_{Z(\lambda)})=\displaystyle{\sum_{i=1}^{m}}\phi(1_{U_{i}})
. Write
U_{i}=\displaystyle{\bigsqcup_{j=1}^{n_{i}}}~Z(\lambda_{i_{j}})
for each
i=1,2,...,m
. Then, by our definition
\phi(1_{U_{i}})=\displaystyle{\sum_{j=1}^{n_{i}}}\phi(1_{Z(\lambda_{i_{j}})})=\displaystyle{\sum_{j=1}^{n_{i}}}\lambda_{i_{j}}
. Again,
Z(\lambda)=\displaystyle{\bigsqcup_{i=1}^{m}}\left(\displaystyle{\bigsqcup_{j=1}^{n_{i}}}~Z(\lambda_{i_{j}})\right)
and so by applying Lemma
4.4
, we have

\phi(1_{Z(\lambda)})=\lambda=\displaystyle{\sum_{i=1}^{m}}\left(\displaystyle{\sum_{j=1}^{n_{i}}}\lambda_{i_{j}}\right)=\displaystyle{\sum_{i=1}^{m}}\phi(1_{U_{i}})

in
\mathcal{P}(\Lambda)
. Now assume
\displaystyle{\sum_{i=1}^{m}}a_{i}1_{Z(\lambda_{i})}=\displaystyle{\sum_{j=1}^{n}}b_{j}1_{Z(\mu_{j})}
, where
Z(\lambda_{i})
’s are mutually disjoint,
Z(\mu_{j})
’s are mutually disjoint and
a_{i},b_{j}\in\mathbb{Z}\setminus\{0\}
for all
i=1,2,\ldots,m
and
j=1,2,\ldots,n
. For each
i,j
set
W_{ij}:=Z(\lambda_{i})\cap Z(\mu_{j})
and
\mathcal{K}:=\{W_{ij}~|~W_{ij}\neq\emptyset\}
. Note that

Z(\lambda_{i})=\displaystyle{\bigsqcup_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq j\leq n\\
W_{ij}\neq\emptyset\end{subarray}}}~W_{ij}=\displaystyle{\bigsqcup_{W\in\mathcal{K},W\subseteq Z(\lambda_{i})}}~W

and similarly

Z(\mu_{j})=\displaystyle{\bigsqcup_{W\in\mathcal{K},W\subseteq Z(\mu_{j})}}~W.

By our earlier observation, it follows that

\phi\left(\displaystyle{\sum_{i=1}^{m}}a_{i}1_{Z(\lambda_{i})}\right)=\displaystyle{\sum_{i=1}^{m}}a_{i}\left(\displaystyle{\sum_{W\in\mathcal{K},W\subseteq Z(\lambda_{i})}}\phi(1_{W})\right)=\displaystyle{\sum_{W\in\mathcal{K}}}\left(\displaystyle{\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq i\leq m\\
W\subseteq Z(\lambda_{i})\end{subarray}}}a_{i}\right)\phi(1_{W}),

and similarly

\phi\left(\displaystyle{\sum_{j=1}^{n}}b_{j}1_{Z(\mu_{j})}\right)=\displaystyle{\sum_{W\in\mathcal{K}}}\left(\displaystyle{\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq j\leq n\\
W\subseteq Z(\mu_{j})\end{subarray}}}b_{j}\right)\phi(1_{W}).

We remark that there is a single summand in each summation inside the parenthesis on the right-hand side of both the equations above. Take any
W\in\mathcal{K}
and
u\in W
. Clearly
u\in Z(\lambda_{i})
if and only if
W\subseteq Z(\lambda_{i})
and
u\in Z(\mu_{j})
if and only if
W\subseteq Z(\mu_{j})
. Therefore

\displaystyle{\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq i\leq m\\
W\subseteq Z(\lambda_{i})\end{subarray}}}a_{i}=\displaystyle{\sum_{i=1}^{m}}a_{i}1_{Z(\lambda_{i})}(u)=\displaystyle{\sum_{j=1}^{n}}b_{j}1_{Z(\mu_{j})}(u)=\displaystyle{\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq j\leq n\\
W\subseteq Z(\mu_{j})\end{subarray}}}b_{j},

and consequently
\phi\left(\displaystyle{\sum_{i=1}^{m}}a_{i}1_{Z(\lambda_{i})}\right)=\phi\left(\displaystyle{\sum_{j=1}^{n}}b_{j}1_{Z(\mu_{j})}\right)
. This shows that
\phi
is well-defined. It is indeed a group homomorphism. For this we show that
\phi(a1_{Z(\lambda)}+b1_{Z(\mu)})=a\phi(1_{Z(\lambda)})+b\phi(1_{Z(\mu)})
and then one can extend the idea to show that
\phi(f+g)=\phi(f)+\phi(g)
for any two
f,g\in C_{c}(\mathcal{G}_{\Lambda}^{(0)},\mathbb{Z})
. Note that,
Z(\lambda)=(Z(\lambda)\setminus Z(\mu))\sqcup(Z(\lambda)\cap Z(\mu))
and
Z(\mu)=(Z(\mu)\setminus Z(\lambda))\sqcup(Z(\mu)\cap Z(\lambda))
. Since
\mathcal{G}_{\Lambda}^{(0)}
is Hausdorff,
Z(\lambda)\setminus Z(\mu)
,
Z(\lambda)\cap Z(\mu)
and
Z(\mu)\setminus Z(\lambda)
are mutually disjoint compact open subsets. Thus

\displaystyle~a\phi(1_{Z(\lambda)})+b\phi(1_{Z(\mu)})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle~a\left(\phi(1_{Z(\lambda)\setminus Z(\mu)})+\phi(1_{Z(\lambda)\cap Z(\mu)})\right)+b\left(\phi(1_{Z(\mu)\setminus Z(\lambda)})+\phi(1_{Z(\mu)\cap Z(\lambda)})\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle~a\phi(1_{Z(\lambda)\setminus Z(\mu)})+(a+b)\phi(1_{Z(\lambda)\cap Z(\mu)})+b\phi(1_{Z(\mu)\setminus Z(\lambda)})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle~\phi\left(a1_{Z(\lambda)\setminus Z(\mu)}+(a+b)1_{Z(\lambda)\cap Z(\mu)}+b1_{Z(\mu)\setminus Z(\lambda)}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle~\phi(a1_{Z(\lambda)}+b1_{Z(\mu)}),

where the first equality follows by our earlier observation. Now consider a map
\psi:\mathcal{P}(\Lambda)\longrightarrow C_{c}(\mathcal{G}_{\Lambda}^{(0)},\mathbb{Z})
defined on the generators by

\lambda\longmapsto 1_{Z(\lambda)},

and then extended linearly. Note that for any
\mathbf{n}\in\mathbb{N}^{k}
,

\psi\left(\displaystyle{\sum_{\alpha\in s(\lambda)\Lambda^{\mathbf{n}}}}\lambda\alpha\right)=\displaystyle{\sum_{\alpha\in s(\lambda)\Lambda^{\mathbf{n}}}}1_{Z(\lambda\alpha)}=1_{Z(\lambda)}

since
Z(\lambda)=\displaystyle{\bigsqcup_{\alpha\in s(\lambda)\Lambda^{\mathbf{n}}}}Z(\lambda\alpha)
, and hence
\psi
is compatible with the defining relations of
\mathcal{P}(\Lambda)
. Evidently,
\psi(\phi(1_{Z(\lambda)}))=\psi(\lambda)=1_{Z(\lambda)}
and
\phi(\psi(\lambda))=\phi(1_{Z(\lambda)})=\lambda
which assures that
\phi
,
\psi
are mutually inverse group homomorphisms and that completes the proof.
∎

Consider the skew-product
k
-graph
\overline{\Lambda}:=\Lambda\times_{d}\mathbb{Z}^{k}
(see Example
2.4
). Applying Proposition
4.5
on
\overline{\Lambda}
we obtain
C_{c}(\mathcal{G}_{\overline{\Lambda}}^{(0)},\mathbb{Z})\cong\mathcal{P}(\overline{\Lambda})
. Next, we observe that
\mathcal{P}(\overline{\Lambda})
is a
\mathbb{Z}^{k}
-module with respect to a certain action of
\mathbb{Z}^{k}
.

Note that there is a groupoid isomorphism (see
[
34
, Theorem 5.2]
)

\displaystyle\varphi:\mathcal{G}_{\Lambda}\times_{\tilde{d}}\mathbb{Z}^{k}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\mathcal{G}_{\overline{\Lambda}}

\displaystyle((x,\mathbf{n},y),\mathbf{m})

\displaystyle\longmapsto((x,\mathbf{m}),\mathbf{n},(y,\mathbf{m}+\mathbf{n})).

The readers should note that the set of infinite paths in
\overline{\Lambda}
i.e.,
\overline{\Lambda}^{\infty}
is identified with
\Lambda^{\infty}\times\mathbb{Z}^{k}
as follows: for any
\chi\in\overline{\Lambda}^{\infty}
,
\chi\longmapsto(\pi_{1}\circ\chi,\pi_{2}(\chi(0)))\in\Lambda^{\infty}\times\mathbb{Z}^{k}
, where
\pi_{1}:\Lambda\times\mathbb{Z}^{k}\longrightarrow\Lambda
and
\pi_{2}:\Lambda\times\mathbb{Z}^{k}\longrightarrow\mathbb{Z}^{k}
are the usual projections; on the other hand any
(x,\mathbf{n})\in\Lambda^{\infty}\times\mathbb{Z}^{k}
is realised as the infinite path
(x,\mathbf{n}):\Omega_{k}\longrightarrow\overline{\Lambda}
defined by
(x,\mathbf{n})(\mathbf{p},\mathbf{q}):=(x(\mathbf{p},\mathbf{q}),\mathbf{n}+\mathbf{p})
.

There is a canonical left
\mathbb{Z}^{k}
-action on the skew-product groupoid
\mathcal{G}_{\Lambda}\times_{\tilde{d}}\mathbb{Z}^{k}
defined as:

q\cdot((x,\mathbf{n},y),\mathbf{m}):=((x,\mathbf{n},y),\mathbf{q}+\mathbf{m}),

which, in view of the above isomorphism
\varphi
, induces a
\mathbb{Z}^{k}
-action on
\mathcal{G}_{\overline{\Lambda}}
as follows:

q\cdot((x,\mathbf{m}),\mathbf{n},(y,\mathbf{m}+\mathbf{n})):=((x,\mathbf{q}+\mathbf{m}),\mathbf{n},(y,\mathbf{p}+\mathbf{m}+\mathbf{n})).

Viewing this action as a group homomorphism
\eta:\mathbb{Z}^{k}\longrightarrow\operatorname{Aut}(\mathcal{G}_{\overline{\Lambda}})
we have a left
\mathbb{Z}^{k}
-action on the abelian group
C_{c}(\mathcal{G}_{\overline{\Lambda}}^{(0)},\mathbb{Z})
defined by

\mathbf{q}\cdot f:=f\circ\eta_{-\mathbf{q}}.

Finally define a left
\mathbb{Z}^{k}
-action on the generators of the path group
\mathcal{P}(\overline{\Lambda})
by

\mathbf{q}\cdot(\lambda,\mathbf{n}):=\phi(\mathbf{q}\cdot 1_{Z((\lambda,\mathbf{n}))}),

where
\phi
is the isomorphism between
C_{c}(\mathcal{G}_{\overline{\Lambda}}^{(0)},\mathbb{Z})
and
\mathcal{P}(\overline{\Lambda})
(see the proof of Proposition
4.5
). This clearly gives a well-defined action on
\mathcal{P}(\overline{\Lambda})
. For
(x,\mathbf{m})\in\mathcal{G}_{\overline{\Lambda}}^{(0)}
,

\left(\mathbf{q}\cdot 1_{Z((\lambda,\mathbf{n}))}\right)((x,\mathbf{m}))=\left(1_{Z((\lambda,\mathbf{n}))}\circ\eta_{-\mathbf{q}}\right)((x,\mathbf{m}))=1_{Z((\lambda,\mathbf{n}))}((x,\mathbf{m}-\mathbf{q}))=1_{Z((\lambda,\mathbf{q}+\mathbf{n}))}((x,\mathbf{m})).

The last equality follows since
(x,\mathbf{m}-\mathbf{q})\in Z((\lambda,\mathbf{n}))
if and only if
(x,\mathbf{m}-\mathbf{q})=(\lambda,\mathbf{n})(y,\mathbf{p})
for some
(y,\mathbf{p})\in\overline{\Lambda}^{\infty}
i.e,
(x,\mathbf{m}-\mathbf{q})=(\lambda y,\mathbf{n})
or,
x=\lambda y
and
\mathbf{m}=\mathbf{q}+\mathbf{n}
or, equivalently
(x,\mathbf{m})\in Z((\lambda,\mathbf{q}+\mathbf{n}))
. Therefore
\mathbf{q}\cdot(\lambda,\mathbf{n})=(\lambda,\mathbf{q}+\mathbf{n})
.

Now we state the main theorem of this section.

Theorem 4.6
.

Let
\Lambda
be any row-finite
k
-graph without sources and
\mathsf{k}
any field. There exists a
\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}^{k}]
-module isomorphism

\Phi:H_{0}^{\operatorname{gr}}(\mathcal{G}_{\Lambda})\longrightarrow K_{0}^{\operatorname{gr}}(\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Lambda))

which when restricted to
H_{0}^{\operatorname{gr}}(\mathcal{G}_{\Lambda})^{+}
gives a
\mathbb{Z}^{k}
-monoid isomorphism between
H_{0}^{\operatorname{gr}}(\mathcal{G}_{\Lambda})^{+}
and
T_{\Lambda}
, the positive cone of
K_{0}^{\operatorname{gr}}(\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Lambda))
.

Proof.

The graded zeroth homology of the
\mathbb{Z}^{k}
-graded groupoid
\mathcal{G}_{\Lambda}
is the usual zeroth homology of the skew-product groupoid
\mathcal{G}_{\Lambda}\times_{\tilde{d}}\mathbb{Z}^{k}
i.e.,

H_{0}^{\operatorname{gr}}(\mathcal{G}_{\Lambda})=H_{0}(\mathcal{G}_{\Lambda}\times_{\tilde{d}}\mathbb{Z}^{k})=C_{c}((\mathcal{G}_{\Lambda}\times_{\tilde{d}}\mathbb{Z}^{k})^{(0)},\mathbb{Z})/\operatorname{Im}\partial_{1},

where
\partial_{1}:C_{c}(\mathcal{G}_{\Lambda}\times_{\tilde{d}}\mathbb{Z}^{k},\mathbb{Z})\longrightarrow C_{c}((\mathcal{G}_{\Lambda}\times_{\tilde{d}}\mathbb{Z}^{k})^{(0)},\mathbb{Z})
is the map
s_{*}-r_{*}
. By
[
30
, Lemma 5.5]
,

\operatorname{Im}\partial_{1}=\operatorname{span}_{\mathbb{Z}}\{1_{s(U)}-1_{r(U)}~|~U\in(\mathcal{G}_{\Lambda}\times_{\tilde{d}}\mathbb{Z}^{k})_{h}^{a}\}.

Any homogeneous compact open bisection of degree
\mathbf{p}
in
\mathcal{G}_{\Lambda}\times_{\tilde{d}}\mathbb{Z}^{k}
is of the form
U=\displaystyle{\bigsqcup_{i=1}^{\ell}}~Z(\lambda_{i},\mu_{i})\times\{\mathbf{n}_{i}\}
where
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
,
\lambda_{i},\mu_{i}\in\Lambda
,
s(\lambda_{i})=s(\mu_{i})
,
\mathbf{n}_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}^{k}
and
d(\lambda_{i})-d(\mu_{i})=\mathbf{p}
for all
i=1,2,\ldots,\ell
. Using this and after some simplification we obtain

\operatorname{Im}\partial_{1}=\operatorname{span}_{\mathbb{Z}}\{1_{Z(\lambda)\times\{\mathbf{n}\}}-1_{Z(s(\lambda))\times\{\mathbf{n}+d(\lambda)\}}~|~\lambda\in\Lambda,\mathbf{n}\in\mathbb{Z}^{k}\}.

By Proposition
4.5
, we have an isomorphism
\phi:C_{c}(\mathcal{G}_{\overline{\Lambda}}^{(0)},\mathbb{Z})\longrightarrow\mathcal{P}(\overline{\Lambda})
such that
\phi(1_{Z((\lambda,\mathbf{n}))})=(\lambda,\mathbf{n})
. Again, we note that
\mathcal{G}_{\overline{\Lambda}}^{(0)}\cong(\mathcal{G}_{\Lambda}\times_{\tilde{d}}\mathbb{Z}^{k})^{(0)}
. Combining these two facts we have an isomorphism

\displaystyle\xi:C_{c}((\mathcal{G}_{\Lambda}\times_{\tilde{d}}\mathbb{Z}^{k})^{(0)},\mathbb{Z})

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\mathcal{P}(\overline{\Lambda})

\displaystyle 1_{Z(\lambda)\times\{\mathbf{n}\}}

\displaystyle\longmapsto(\lambda,\mathbf{n}).

In
\mathcal{P}(\overline{\Lambda})
, consider the subgroup

J:=\langle\xi(1_{Z(\lambda)\times\{\mathbf{n}\}}-1_{Z(s(\lambda))\times\{\mathbf{n}+d(\lambda)\}})~|~\lambda\in\Lambda,\mathbf{n}\in\mathbb{Z}^{k}\rangle=\langle(\lambda,\mathbf{n})-(s(\lambda),\mathbf{n}+d(\lambda))~|~\lambda\in\Lambda,\mathbf{n}\in\mathbb{Z}^{k}\rangle.

Then
\xi(\operatorname{Im}\partial_{1})=J
and so
\xi
induces an isomorphism

\tilde{\xi}:H_{0}^{\operatorname{gr}}(\mathcal{G}_{\Lambda})=C_{c}((\mathcal{G}_{\Lambda}\times_{\tilde{d}}\mathbb{Z}^{k})^{(0)},\mathbb{Z})/\operatorname{Im}\partial_{1}\longrightarrow\mathcal{P}(\overline{\Lambda})/J.

Clearly
\xi
respects the relevant
\mathbb{Z}^{k}
-actions,
\operatorname{Im}\partial_{1}
and
J
are
\mathbb{Z}^{k}
-submodules. It follows that
\tilde{\xi}
is
\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}^{k}]
-module isomorphism. Now we show that the quotient group
\mathcal{P}(\overline{\Lambda})/J
is isomorphic to the group completion of the talented monoid of
\Lambda
, and this isomorphism will be the remaining bit of our desired isomorphism. First define a map

f:\mathcal{P}(\overline{\Lambda})\longrightarrow G(T_{\Lambda})

on the generators by

f((\lambda,\mathbf{n})):=s(\lambda)(\mathbf{n}+d(\lambda)),

and then extend linearly to all elements. For each
\mathbf{m}\in\mathbb{N}^{k}
since

\displaystyle f((\lambda,\mathbf{n}))=s(\lambda)(\mathbf{n}+d(\lambda))

\displaystyle=\displaystyle{\sum_{\mu\in s(\lambda)\Lambda^{\mathbf{m}}}}s(\mu)(\mathbf{n}+d(\lambda)+\mathbf{m})~(\text{by defining relations of}~T_{\Lambda})

\displaystyle=\displaystyle{\sum_{\mu\in s(\lambda)\Lambda^{\mathbf{m}}}}s(\lambda\mu)(\mathbf{n}+d(\lambda\mu))

\displaystyle=f\left(\displaystyle{\sum_{\mu\in s(\lambda)\Lambda^{\mathbf{m}}}}(\lambda\mu,\mathbf{n})\right)

\displaystyle=f\left(\displaystyle{\sum_{\mu\in s(\lambda)\Lambda^{\mathbf{m}}}}(\lambda,\mathbf{n})(\mu,\mathbf{n}+d(\lambda))\right)

\displaystyle=f\left(\displaystyle{\sum_{(\mu,\mathbf{p})\in\overline{s}(\lambda,\mathbf{n})\overline{\Lambda}^{\mathbf{m}}}}(\lambda,\mathbf{n})(\mu,\mathbf{p})\right)

therefore
f
is a well-defined group homomorphism. Moreover,
J\subseteq\ker(f)
since
f((\lambda,\mathbf{n})-(s(\lambda),\mathbf{n}+d(\lambda)))=s(\lambda)(\mathbf{n}+d(\lambda))-s(\lambda)(\mathbf{n}+d(\lambda))=0
. Thus
f
induces a group homomorphism

\tilde{f}:\mathcal{P}(\overline{\Lambda})/J\longrightarrow G(T_{\Lambda}).

Now consider a monoid homomorphism

g:T_{\Lambda}\longrightarrow\mathcal{P}(\overline{\Lambda})/J

defined on the generators by

g(v(\mathbf{n})):=[(v,\mathbf{n})]_{J}.

To show that
g
is well-defined, it suffices to verify that
g
is compatible with the defining relations of
T_{\Lambda}
. For any
\mathbf{m}\in\mathbb{N}^{k}
, in
T_{\Lambda}
, we have
v(\mathbf{n})=\displaystyle{\sum_{\alpha\in v\Lambda^{\mathbf{m}}}}s(\alpha)(\mathbf{n}+\mathbf{m})
. Now,

g\left(\displaystyle{\sum_{\alpha\in v\Lambda^{\mathbf{m}}}}s(\alpha)(\mathbf{n}+\mathbf{m})\right)=\displaystyle{\sum_{\alpha\in v\Lambda^{\mathbf{m}}}}[(s(\alpha),\mathbf{n}+\mathbf{m})]_{J}=\displaystyle{\sum_{(\alpha,\mathbf{n})\in(v,\mathbf{n})\overline{\Lambda}^{\mathbf{m}}}}[(\alpha,\mathbf{n})]_{J}=[(v,\mathbf{n})]_{J}=g(v(\mathbf{n})).

Hence
g
is well-defined. By the universal property of group completion, there is a unique group homomorphism

\tilde{g}:G(T_{\Lambda})\longrightarrow\mathcal{P}(\overline{\Lambda})/J

such that
\tilde{g}|_{T_{\Lambda}}=g
. It is easy to observe that
\tilde{g}\circ\tilde{f}
and
\tilde{f}\circ\tilde{g}
are respectively the identity morphisms on
\mathcal{P}(\overline{\Lambda})/J
and
G(T_{\Lambda})
which shows that
\tilde{f}
is a group isomorphism. It also preserves the
\mathbb{Z}^{k}
-action since

\tilde{f}(\mathbf{q}\cdot(\lambda,\mathbf{n}))=\tilde{f}((\lambda,\mathbf{q}+\mathbf{n}))=s(\lambda)(\mathbf{q}+\mathbf{n}+d(\lambda))=~^{\mathbf{q}}s(\lambda)(\mathbf{n}+d(\lambda))=~^{\mathbf{q}}\tilde{f}((\lambda,\mathbf{n})).

Finally defining
\Phi:=\tilde{f}\circ\tilde{\xi}
and noting that
K_{0}^{\operatorname{gr}}(\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Lambda))
is the group completion of
T_{\Lambda}
(see
[
32
, Remark 3.15]
), we have the desired
\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}^{k}]
-module isomorphism between
H_{0}^{\operatorname{gr}}(\mathcal{G}_{\Lambda})
and
K_{0}^{\operatorname{gr}}(\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Lambda))
.

For the remaining part, observe that any
f\in C_{c}((\mathcal{G}_{\Lambda}\times_{\tilde{d}}\mathbb{Z}^{k})^{(0)},\mathbb{Z})^{+}
can be written as

f=\displaystyle{\sum_{i=1}^{t}}a_{i}1_{Z(\lambda_{i})\times\{\mathbf{n}_{i}\}},

where
t\in\mathbb{N}
,
a_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}
,
\mathbf{n}_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}^{k}
for each
t
and
Z(\lambda_{i})
’s are mutually disjoint. Under the isomorphism
\tilde{\xi}
, the equivalence class of such an
f
corresponds to
\displaystyle{\sum_{i=1}^{t}}a_{i}[(\lambda_{i},\mathbf{n}_{i})]_{J}
. Therefore,

\Phi([f])=\tilde{f}\left(\displaystyle{\sum_{i=1}^{t}}a_{i}[(\lambda_{i},\mathbf{n}_{i})]_{J}\right)=\displaystyle{\sum_{i=1}^{t}}a_{i}f((\lambda_{i},\mathbf{n}_{i}))=\displaystyle{\sum_{i=1}^{t}}a_{i}s(\lambda_{i})(\mathbf{n}_{i}+d(\lambda_{i}))\in T_{\Lambda}

since each
a_{i}>0
. Conversely, if
x=\displaystyle{\sum_{j=1}^{N}}v_{j}(\mathbf{n}_{j})\in T_{\Lambda}
then

\Phi^{-1}(x)=\tilde{\xi}^{-1}\left(\displaystyle{\sum_{j=1}^{N}}[(v_{j},\mathbf{n}_{j})]_{J}\right)=[\displaystyle{\sum_{j=1}^{N}}1_{Z(v_{j})\times\{\mathbf{n}_{j}\}}]\in H_{0}^{\operatorname{gr}}(\mathcal{G}_{\Lambda})^{+},

since
\displaystyle{\sum_{j=1}^{N}}1_{Z(v_{j})\times\{\mathbf{n}_{j}\}}\in C_{c}((\mathcal{G}_{\Lambda}\times_{\tilde{d}}\mathbb{Z}^{k})^{(0)},\mathbb{Z})^{+}
. This completes the proof.
∎

Remark 4.7
.

We describe an alternative proof of Theorem
4.6
. For an ample groupoid
\mathcal{G}
with locally compact Hausdorff unit space
\mathcal{G}^{(0)}
, one can check that the rule
\psi:C_{c}(\mathcal{G}^{(0)},\mathbb{Z})^{+}\longrightarrow\operatorname{Typ}(\mathcal{G})
;
1_{U}\longmapsto\operatorname{typ}(U)
(
U
is a compact open subset of
\mathcal{G}^{(0)}
) induces an isomorphism of commutative monoids:

\Psi:H_{0}(\mathcal{G})^{+}\longrightarrow\mathcal{C}(\operatorname{Typ}(\mathcal{G})),

where
\mathcal{C}(\operatorname{Typ}(\mathcal{G}))
is the
universal cancellative abelian semigroup

[
4
]
of the
type monoid

\operatorname{Typ}(\mathcal{G})
i.e.,

\mathcal{C}(\operatorname{Typ}(\mathcal{G}))=\operatorname{Typ}(\mathcal{G})/\sim

where
\sim
is the equivalence relation on
\operatorname{Typ}(\mathcal{G})
given by
x\sim y
if and only if
x+z=y+z
for some
z\in\operatorname{Typ}(\mathcal{G})
. For an ample groupoid
\mathcal{G}
with compact unit space, an isomorphism between
H_{0}(\mathcal{G})^{+}
and
\mathcal{C}(\operatorname{Typ}(\mathcal{G}))
was established in
[
4
, Proposition 1.6]
with a different (but canonically equivalent) description of type monoid of
\mathcal{G}
. Now if
\mathcal{G}
is a
\Gamma
-graded groupoid (graded by a cocycle
c:\mathcal{G}\longrightarrow\Gamma
) and is equipped with a left
\Gamma
-action then we have a
\Gamma
-monoid isomorphism

H_{0}^{\operatorname{gr}}(\mathcal{G})^{+}=H_{0}(\mathcal{G}\times_{c}\Gamma)^{+}\cong\mathcal{C}(\operatorname{Typ}(\mathcal{G}\times_{c}\Gamma))=\mathcal{C}(\operatorname{Typ}^{\operatorname{gr}}(\mathcal{G})).

Applying this to the path groupoid
\mathcal{G}_{\Lambda}
together with the fact that the graded type monoid of
\mathcal{G}_{\Lambda}
is isomorphic to the talented monoid of
\Lambda
(see
[
32
, Theorem 3.18]
) we have an isomorphism of
\mathbb{Z}^{k}
-monoids

H_{0}^{\operatorname{gr}}(\mathcal{G}_{\Lambda})^{+}\cong\mathcal{C}(\operatorname{Typ}^{\operatorname{gr}}(\mathcal{G}_{\Lambda}))\cong\mathcal{C}(T_{\Lambda})=T_{\Lambda},

the last equality follows since
T_{\Lambda}
is a cancellative monoid (see
[
32
, Proposition 3.14
(ii)
]
). Finally, taking the group completion of both sides, we obtain an order-preserving isomorphism between
H_{0}^{\operatorname{gr}}(\mathcal{G}_{\Lambda})
and
K_{0}^{\operatorname{gr}}(\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Lambda))
. On the level of Leavitt path algebras, the said argument can be employed in view of
[
18
, Lemma 3.6]
, to provide another proof of
[
30
, Theorem 6.6]
.

Given a
k
-graph
\Lambda
and a monoid homomorphism
f:\mathbb{N}^{\ell}\longrightarrow\mathbb{N}^{k}
, recall that we can form the pullback graph
f^{*}(\Lambda)
along
f
which is an
\ell
-graph. We finish this section by exhibiting some pleasant connections between the homology theories of the groupoids
\mathcal{G}_{f^{*}(\Lambda)}
and
\mathcal{G}_{\Lambda}
.

Proposition 4.8
.

Let
\ell,k
be positive integers,
\Lambda
a row-finite
k
-graph without sources and
f:\mathbb{N}^{\ell}\longrightarrow\mathbb{N}^{k}
a surjective monoid homomorphism. Then

(i)

H_{0}(\mathcal{G}_{f^{*}(\Lambda)})\cong H_{0}(\mathcal{G}_{\Lambda})
;

(ii)
there exists a surjective group homomorphism from
H_{0}^{\operatorname{gr}}(\mathcal{G}_{f^{*}(\Lambda)})
onto
H_{0}^{\operatorname{gr}}(\mathcal{G}_{\Lambda})
.

Proof.

(i)
By
[
34
, Proposition 2.10]
we have,
\mathcal{G}_{f^{*}(\Lambda)}\cong\mathcal{G}_{\Lambda}\times\mathbb{Z}^{\ell-k}
. Applying Künneth theorem
[
39
, Theorem 2.4 & Corollary 2.5]
for product of étale groupoids, we obtain

H_{0}(\mathcal{G}_{f^{*}(\Lambda)})\cong H_{0}(\mathcal{G}_{\Lambda}\times\mathbb{Z}^{\ell-k})\cong H_{0}(\mathcal{G}_{\Lambda})\otimes\underset{\longleftarrow(\ell-k)\longrightarrow}{H_{0}(\mathbb{Z})\otimes\cdots\otimes H_{0}(\mathbb{Z})}.

But the integral zeroth homology of
\mathbb{Z}
is
\mathbb{Z}
and for any abelian group (equivalently,
\mathbb{Z}
-module)
\Gamma
,
\Gamma\otimes\mathbb{Z}\cong\Gamma
via the canonical isomorphism
\gamma\otimes n\longmapsto n\cdot\gamma
. In view of these facts, we finally have
H_{0}(\mathcal{G}_{f^{*}(\Lambda)})\cong H_{0}(\mathcal{G}_{\Lambda})
.

(ii)
Note that the set of objects
f^{*}(\Lambda)^{0}
can be identified with
\Lambda^{0}
. The homomorphism
f
clearly extends to a surjective homomorphism
f:\mathbb{Z}^{\ell}\longrightarrow\mathbb{Z}^{k}
. Consider the following correspondence

\displaystyle\rho:T_{f^{*}(\Lambda)}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow T_{\Lambda}

\displaystyle v(\mathbf{n})

\displaystyle\longmapsto v(f(\mathbf{n})),

for all
v\in f^{*}(\Lambda)^{0}
and
\mathbf{n}\in\mathbb{Z}^{\ell}
. We claim that
\rho
when extended linearly to all elements of
T_{f^{*}(\Lambda)}
gives a well-defined monoid homomorphism. It suffices to check that the rule is compatible with the defining relations of talented monoid. Let
v\in\Lambda^{0},\mathbf{n}\in\mathbb{Z}^{\ell}
and
\mathbf{m}\in\mathbb{N}^{\ell}
. Then in
T_{f^{*}(\Lambda)}
, we have

v(\mathbf{n})=\displaystyle{\sum_{(\alpha,\mathbf{m})\in vf^{*}(\Lambda)^{\mathbf{m}}}}s((\alpha,\mathbf{m}))(\mathbf{n}+\mathbf{m}).

Now,

\rho\left(\displaystyle{\sum_{(\alpha,\mathbf{m})\in vf^{*}(\Lambda)^{\mathbf{m}}}}s((\alpha,\mathbf{m}))(\mathbf{n}+\mathbf{m})\right)=\displaystyle{\sum_{\alpha\in v\Lambda^{f(\mathbf{m})}}}\rho\left(s(\alpha)(\mathbf{n}+\mathbf{m})\right)=\displaystyle{\sum_{\alpha\in v\Lambda^{f(\mathbf{m})}}}s(\alpha)(f(\mathbf{n})+f(\mathbf{m}))=v(f(\mathbf{n}))=\rho(v(\mathbf{n})).

Hence our claim is established. Since
f
is surjective, it follows from the definition that
\rho
is also surjective. Since the group completion functor preserves epimorphisms, so
\rho
induces a surjective group homomorphism
\tilde{\rho}:K_{0}^{\operatorname{gr}}(\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(f^{*}(\Lambda)))\longrightarrow K_{0}^{\operatorname{gr}}(\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Lambda))
. This together with Theorem
4.6
finishes the proof.
∎

Example 4.9
.

Let
n\geq 2
be an integer. Suppose
\Lambda
is the
2
-graph with
1
-skeleton:

.
\cdot
\cdot
\cdot
\cdot
(n)
v
f_{1}
f_{2}
f_{n}
h

and factorization rules:
hf_{i}=f_{i}h
for all
i=1,2,...,n
. We want to compute the zeroth homology of
\mathcal{G}_{\Lambda}
. For this, we will apply Proposition
4.8

(i)
. Consider the surjective monoid homomorphism
f:\mathbb{N}^{2}\longrightarrow\mathbb{N}
defined by
f((a,b)):=a
. We observe that the pullback
2
-graph
f^{*}(R_{n})
for the rose with
n
petals

.
\cdot
\cdot
\cdot
\cdot
(n)
u
R_{n}\equiv

is isomorphic to
\Lambda
. If we name the loops of
R_{n}
as
c_{1},c_{2},\ldots,c_{n}
and identify
u
as
v
,
(u,(0,1))
as
h
and
(c_{i},(1,0))
as
f_{i}
for each
i=1,2,\ldots,n
, we get a coloured graph isomorphism between the
1
-skeletons of
f^{*}(R_{n})
and
\Lambda
. Moreover, the factorization rules for both these
2
-graphs agree and hence
\Lambda\cong f^{*}(R_{n})
. Since the graph groupoid of
R_{n}
is isomorphic to the groupoid arising from the one-sided full shift on a finite alphabet with
n
symbols, therefore
H_{0}(\mathcal{G}_{R_{n}})\cong\mathbb{Z}_{n-1}
. One can also establish this using the following relationships:
H_{0}(\mathcal{G}_{R_{n}})^{+}\cong\mathcal{C}(\operatorname{Typ}(\mathcal{G}_{R_{n}}))
and
\operatorname{Typ}(\mathcal{G}_{R_{n}})\cong M_{R_{n}}\cong\mathbb{Z}_{n-1}\cup\{z\}
(see Remark
4.7
,
[
3
, Theorem 7.5]
and
[
1
, Examples 3.2.2
(i)
]
). By applying Proposition
4.8

(i)
, we finally obtain
H_{0}(\mathcal{G}_{\Lambda})\cong H_{0}(\mathcal{G}_{R_{n}})\cong\mathbb{Z}_{n-1}
.

5.
The dimension group arising from a
k
-graph

Throughout this section we fix
\Lambda
to be a row-finite
k
-graph without sources such that the set of objects
\Lambda^{0}
is finite. For
\mathbf{n}\in\mathbb{N}^{k}
, we denote
A_{\mathbf{n}}
to be the matrix in
\mathbb{M}_{\Lambda^{0}}(\mathbb{N})
such that

A_{\mathbf{n}}(u,v):=|u\Lambda^{\mathbf{n}}v|=|\{\lambda\in\Lambda~|~s(\lambda)=v,r(\lambda)=u,d(\lambda)=\mathbf{n}\}|,

for all
u,v\in\Lambda^{0}
(see
[
24
,
34
,
42
]
). These matrices are called the
vertex matrices
of
\Lambda
. Using the unique factorization property, it can be shown that
A_{\mathbf{n}}A_{\mathbf{m}}=A_{\mathbf{n}+\mathbf{m}}
, and so
A_{\mathbf{n}}A_{\mathbf{m}}=A_{\mathbf{m}}A_{\mathbf{n}}
for all
\mathbf{n},\mathbf{m}\in\mathbb{N}^{k}
. Moreover for
\mathbf{n}=(n_{1},n_{2},...,n_{k})
, we can write

(3)

A_{\mathbf{n}}=A_{\mathbf{e}_{1}}^{n_{1}}A_{\mathbf{e}_{2}}^{n_{2}}\cdots A_{\mathbf{e}_{k}}^{n_{k}}.

We now define a directed system of partially ordered groups using the matrices
A_{\mathbf{n}}
,
\mathbf{n}\in\mathbb{N}^{k}
. For each
\mathbf{m}\in\mathbb{Z}^{k}
, write
G_{\mathbf{m}}:=\mathbb{Z}\Lambda^{0}
, the free abelian group generated by
\Lambda^{0}
. Let
\mathbf{m}\leq\mathbf{n}\in\mathbb{Z}^{k}
. Consider the group homomorphisms

\displaystyle f_{\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n}}:G_{\mathbf{m}}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow G_{\mathbf{n}}

\displaystyle x

\displaystyle\longmapsto xA_{\mathbf{n}-\mathbf{m}}.

Since
A_{0}=I_{|\Lambda^{0}|}
,
(G_{\mathbf{m}},f_{\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n}})
is a directed system. We define the
dimension group

H_{\Lambda}
of
\Lambda
to be the direct limit of this directed system, i.e.,

H_{\Lambda}:=\displaystyle{\varinjlim_{\mathbb{Z}^{k}}}~(G_{\mathbf{n}},f_{\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n}}).

Another way to realise the dimension group is via an equivalence relation. Define a relation
\sim_{\Lambda}
on
\mathbb{Z}\Lambda^{0}\times\mathbb{Z}^{k}
by
(x,\mathbf{n})\sim_{\Lambda}(y,\mathbf{m})
if
xA_{\mathbf{l}-\mathbf{n}}=yA_{\mathbf{l}-\mathbf{m}}
for some
\mathbf{l}\in\mathbb{Z}^{k}
with
\mathbf{l}\geq\mathbf{n}\vee\mathbf{m}
or, equivalently if

x\left(\displaystyle{\prod_{i=1}^{k}}A_{\mathbf{e}_{i}}^{l_{i}-n_{i}}\right)=y\left(\displaystyle{\prod_{i=1}^{k}}A_{\mathbf{e}_{i}}^{l_{i}-m_{i}}\right).

It is easy to check that
\sim_{\Lambda}
is an equivalence relation and
H_{\Lambda}=(\mathbb{Z}\Lambda^{0}\times\mathbb{Z}^{k})/\sim_{\Lambda}
.

The group
H_{\Lambda}
is partially ordered with respect to the usual coordinate-wise ordering of
\mathbb{Z}\Lambda^{0}
and the positive cone is

H_{\Lambda}^{+}:=\{[x,\mathbf{n}]\in H_{\Lambda}~|~x\in\mathbb{N}\Lambda^{0}\}=\displaystyle{\varinjlim_{\mathbb{Z}^{k}}}~(\mathbb{N}\Lambda^{0},f_{\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n}}).

We call
H_{\Lambda}^{+}
, the
dimension monoid
of
\Lambda
. The addition in the group
H_{\Lambda}
(and hence in the monoid
H_{\Lambda}^{+}
) is defined as

[x,\mathbf{n}]+[y,\mathbf{m}]:=[xA_{(\mathbf{n}\vee\mathbf{m})-\mathbf{n}}+yA_{(\mathbf{n}\vee\mathbf{m})-\mathbf{m}},\mathbf{n}\vee\mathbf{m}].

For each
i=1,2,\ldots,k
, the map

(4)

\displaystyle\delta_{i}:H_{\Lambda}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow H_{\Lambda}

\displaystyle[x,\mathbf{n}]

\displaystyle\longmapsto[xA_{\mathbf{e}_{i}},\mathbf{n}]=[x,\mathbf{n}-\mathbf{e}_{i}],

is an automorphism with inverse
\delta_{i}^{-1}:H_{\Lambda}\longrightarrow H_{\Lambda}
;
[x,\mathbf{n}]\longmapsto[x,\mathbf{n}+\mathbf{e}_{i}]
. These automorphisms are mutually commuting and preserve the positive cone
H_{\Lambda}^{+}
. As a consequence,
H_{\Lambda}^{+}
becomes a
\mathbb{Z}^{k}
-monoid with respect to the following action:

(5)

^{\mathbf{m}}[x,\mathbf{n}]:=(\delta_{1}^{-m_{1}}\circ\delta_{2}^{-m_{2}}\circ\cdots\circ\delta_{k}^{-m_{k}})([x,\mathbf{n}])=[x,\mathbf{n}+\mathbf{m}],

for all
\mathbf{m}=(m_{1},m_{2},\ldots,m_{k})\in\mathbb{N}^{k}
and
[x,\mathbf{n}]\in H_{\Lambda}^{+}
.

In
[
32
, Proposition 3.14]
, the talented monoid
T_{\Lambda}
was shown to be the direct limit of the directed system
(\mathbb{N}\Lambda^{0},\phi_{\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n}})
where the monoid homomorphisms
\phi_{\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n}}
are defined as
\phi_{\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n}}(v):=\displaystyle{\sum_{w\in\Lambda^{0}}}|v\Lambda^{\mathbf{n}-\mathbf{m}}w|w
. Now for any
\mathbf{m}\leq\mathbf{n}\in\mathbb{Z}^{k}
, we observe

f_{\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n}}(\epsilon_{v})(w)=\displaystyle{\sum_{u\in\Lambda^{0}}}\epsilon_{v}(u)A_{\mathbf{n}-\mathbf{m}}(u,w)=A_{\mathbf{n}-\mathbf{m}}(v,w)=|v\Lambda^{\mathbf{n}-\mathbf{m}}w|=\phi_{\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n}}(\epsilon_{v})(w),

for all
v,w\in\Lambda^{0}
. This shows that
f_{\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n}}=\phi_{\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n}}
in
\operatorname{End}(\mathbb{N}\Lambda^{0})
and consequently the talented monoid
T_{\Lambda}
and the positive cone
H_{\Lambda}^{+}
of the dimension group of
\Lambda
are isomorphic as monoids. In fact, we exhibit an explicit
\mathbb{Z}^{k}
-monoid isomorphism between these two in the following theorem.

Theorem 5.1
.

Let
\Lambda
be a row-finite
k
-graph with no sources such that
|\Lambda^{0}|<\infty
. Consider an enumeration of the set of objects,
\Lambda^{0}=\{v_{1},v_{2},\ldots,v_{|\Lambda^{0}|}\}
. Suppose
\psi:T_{\Lambda}\longrightarrow H_{\Lambda}^{+}
is a map defined on generators by

v_{i}(\mathbf{n})\longmapsto[\epsilon_{i},\mathbf{n}],

and then extended linearly. Then
\psi
is a
\mathbb{Z}^{k}
-monoid isomorphism. Consequently, there is an order-preserving
\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}^{k}]
-module isomorphism
\gamma_{\Lambda}
between the graded Grothendieck group
K_{0}^{\operatorname{gr}}(\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Lambda))
and the dimension group
H_{\Lambda}
.

Proof.

First, we show that
\psi
is well-defined. It suffices to show that
\psi
is compatible with the defining relations of
T_{\Lambda}
. Let
i\in\{1,2,\ldots,|\Lambda^{0}|\}
,
\mathbf{n}\in\mathbb{Z}^{k}
and
\mathbf{m}\in\mathbb{N}^{k}
. Then in
T_{\Lambda}
we have,

v_{i}(\mathbf{n})=\displaystyle{\sum_{\alpha\in v_{i}\Lambda^{\mathbf{m}}}}s(\alpha)(\mathbf{n}+\mathbf{m}).

Now

\displaystyle\psi\left(\displaystyle{\sum_{\alpha\in v_{i}\Lambda^{\mathbf{m}}}}s(\alpha)(\mathbf{n}+\mathbf{m})\right)

\displaystyle=\psi\left(\displaystyle{\sum_{j=1}^{|\Lambda^{0}|}}|v_{i}\Lambda^{m}v_{j}|v_{j}(\mathbf{n}+\mathbf{m})\right)

\displaystyle=\displaystyle{\sum_{j=1}^{|\Lambda^{0}|}}|v_{i}\Lambda^{m}v_{j}|[\epsilon_{j},\mathbf{n}+\mathbf{m}]

\displaystyle=\displaystyle{\sum_{j=1}^{|\Lambda^{0}|}}[|v_{i}\Lambda^{m}v_{j}|\epsilon_{j},\mathbf{n}+\mathbf{m}]

\displaystyle=[\displaystyle{\sum_{j=1}^{|\Lambda^{0}|}}|v_{i}\Lambda^{m}v_{j}|\epsilon_{j},\mathbf{n}+\mathbf{m}]=[\epsilon_{i}A_{\mathbf{m}},\mathbf{n}+\mathbf{m}]=[\epsilon_{i},\mathbf{n}]=\psi(v_{i}(\mathbf{n})).

Hence
\psi
is a well-defined monoid homomorphism. To show that
\psi
is injective, choose any
x,y\in T_{\Lambda}
such that
\psi(x)=\psi(y)
. Since
\Lambda
has no sources, we can write
x=\displaystyle{\sum_{i=1}^{|\Lambda^{0}|}}x_{i}v_{i}(\mathbf{n})
and similarly
y=\displaystyle{\sum_{i=1}^{|\Lambda^{0}|}}y_{i}v_{i}(\mathbf{m})
where
\mathbf{n},\mathbf{m}\in\mathbb{Z}^{k}
and
x_{i},y_{i}\in\mathbb{N}
for all
i=1,2,...,|\Lambda^{0}|
. Then
\psi(x)=\psi(y)
implies that there exists
\mathbf{l}\in\mathbb{Z}^{k}
with
\mathbf{l}\geq\mathbf{n}\vee\mathbf{m}
such that

(6)

(x_{1},x_{2},...,x_{|\Lambda^{0}|})A_{\mathbf{l}-\mathbf{n}}=(y_{1},y_{2},...,y_{|\Lambda^{0}|})A_{\mathbf{l}-\mathbf{m}}.

Now using the relations of
T_{\Lambda}
and Equation (
6
), we have

\displaystyle x=\displaystyle{\sum_{i=1}^{|\Lambda^{0}|}}x_{i}v_{i}(\mathbf{n})

\displaystyle=\displaystyle{\sum_{i=1}^{|\Lambda^{0}|}}x_{i}\left(\displaystyle{\sum_{j=1}^{|\Lambda^{0}|}}|v_{i}\Lambda^{\mathbf{l}-\mathbf{n}}v_{j}|v_{j}(\mathbf{l})\right)

\displaystyle=\displaystyle{\sum_{j=1}^{|\Lambda^{0}|}}\left(\displaystyle{\sum_{i=1}^{|\Lambda^{0}|}}|v_{i}\Lambda^{\mathbf{l}-\mathbf{n}}v_{j}|x_{i}\right)v_{j}(\mathbf{l})

\displaystyle=\displaystyle{\sum_{j=1}^{|\Lambda^{0}|}}\left(\displaystyle{\sum_{i=1}^{|\Lambda^{0}|}}|v_{i}\Lambda^{\mathbf{l}-\mathbf{m}}v_{j}|y_{i}\right)v_{j}(\mathbf{l})=\displaystyle{\sum_{i=1}^{|\Lambda^{0}|}}y_{i}\left(\displaystyle{\sum_{j=1}^{|\Lambda^{0}|}}|v_{i}\Lambda^{\mathbf{l}-\mathbf{m}}v_{j}|v_{j}(\mathbf{l})\right)=\displaystyle{\sum_{i=1}^{|\Lambda^{0}|}}y_{i}v_{i}(\mathbf{m})=y.

By definition
\psi
is surjective. Again it is easy to see that
\psi
respects the
\mathbb{Z}^{k}
-action on the two monoids and this completes the proof of the first part. For the remaining part, consider
\gamma_{\Lambda}:G(T_{\Lambda})\longrightarrow G(H_{\Lambda}^{+})
to be the map induced by
\psi
under the group completion functor. Then the result follows immediately, in view of
[
32
, Remark 3.15]
.
∎

Remark 5.2
.

The directed system we have considered, is the same as that of Evans
[
24
]
. In
[
24
, Lemma 3.9]
he established that
K_{0}(C^{*}(\Lambda\times_{d}\mathbb{Z}^{k}))\cong\displaystyle{\varinjlim_{\mathbb{Z}^{k}}}~(G_{\mathbf{n}},f_{\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n}})
. Relating this with Theorem
5.1
, one immediate obtains
K_{0}^{\operatorname{gr}}(\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Lambda))\cong K_{0}(C^{*}(\Lambda\times_{d}\mathbb{Z}^{k}))
.

Theorem
5.1
sets up the intrinsic connection between the graded
K
-theory and dimension group on the level of
k
-graphs and Kumjian-Pask algebras. Via the isomorphism
\gamma_{\Lambda}
we can conveniently switch between these two; this is going to be a key tool in our investigation.

Lemma 5.3
.

Let
\Lambda
and
\Omega
be row-finite
k
-graphs without sources such that both
\Lambda^{0},\Omega^{0}
are finite. For each
\mathbf{n}\in\mathbb{N}^{k}
, suppose
A_{\mathbf{n}}\in\mathbb{M}_{\Lambda^{0}}(\mathbb{N})
and
B_{\mathbf{n}}\in\mathbb{M}_{\Omega^{0}}(\mathbb{N})
are matrices such that
A_{\mathbf{n}}(u,v):=|u\Lambda^{\mathbf{n}}v|
for
u,v\in\Lambda^{0}
and
B_{\mathbf{n}}(x,y):=|x\Omega^{\mathbf{n}}y|
for
x,y\in\Omega^{0}
.

(i)
Suppose there exists a matrix
R\in\mathbb{M}_{\Lambda^{0}\times\Omega^{0}}(\mathbb{N})
such that
A_{\mathbf{e}_{i}}R=RB_{\mathbf{e}_{i}}
for all
i=1,2,\ldots,k
. Then the map

\displaystyle R:H_{\Lambda}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow H_{\Omega}

\displaystyle[x,\mathbf{n}]

\displaystyle\longmapsto[xR,\mathbf{n}]

is an order-preserving
\mathbb{Z}[x_{1}^{\pm 1},x_{2}^{\pm 1},\ldots,x_{k}^{\pm 1}]
-module homomorphism. Consequently, there exists a unique order-preserving
\mathbb{Z}[x_{1}^{\pm 1},x_{2}^{\pm 1},...,x_{k}^{\pm 1}]
-module homomorphism
\mathfrak{h}_{R}:K_{0}^{\operatorname{gr}}(\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Lambda))\longrightarrow K_{0}^{\operatorname{gr}}(\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Omega))
such that the diagram

(7)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K_{0}^{\operatorname{gr}}(\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Lambda))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K_{0}^{\operatorname{gr}}(\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Omega))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{\Lambda}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{\Omega}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{h}_{R}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma_{\Lambda}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma_{\Omega}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R}

commutes.

(ii)
If there exists an order-preserving
\mathbb{Z}[x_{1}^{\pm 1},x_{2}^{\pm 1},\ldots,x_{k}^{\pm 1}]
-module homomorphism

\mathfrak{h}:K_{0}^{\operatorname{gr}}(\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Lambda))\longrightarrow K_{0}^{\operatorname{gr}}(\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Omega)),

then there is a matrix
R\in\mathbb{M}_{\Lambda^{0}\times\Omega^{0}}(\mathbb{N})
and integers
r_{1},r_{2},\ldots,r_{k}\in\mathbb{Z}
such that
A_{\mathbf{e}_{i}}R=RB_{\mathbf{e}_{i}}
for all
i=1,2,\ldots,k
and

\mathfrak{h}(w)=~^{x_{1}^{r_{1}}x_{2}^{r_{2}}\cdots x_{k}^{r_{k}}}\mathfrak{h}_{R}(w),

for all
w\in K_{0}^{\operatorname{gr}}(\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Lambda))
.

Proof.

(i)
It is easy to show that
A_{\mathbf{e}_{i}}^{n}R=RB_{\mathbf{e}_{i}}^{n}
for all
N\in\mathbb{N}
. Using (
3
) now it follows that

(8)

A_{\mathbf{n}}R=RB_{\mathbf{n}}

for any
\mathbf{n}\in\mathbb{N}^{k}
. Suppose
[x,\mathbf{n}]=[y,\mathbf{m}]
in
H_{\Lambda}
. Then there is some
\mathbf{l}\in\mathbb{Z}^{k}
with
\mathbf{l}\geq\mathbf{n}\vee\mathbf{m}
, such that
xA_{\mathbf{l}N}=yA_{\mathbf{l}-\mathbf{m}}
. Using this and Equation (
8
) we have

(xR)B_{\mathbf{l}-\mathbf{n}}=(xA_{\mathbf{l}-\mathbf{n}})R=(yA_{\mathbf{l}-\mathbf{m}})R=(yR)B_{\mathbf{l}-\mathbf{m}}.

So
[xR,\mathbf{n}]=[yR,\mathbf{m}]
, and hence
R
is well-defined. A routine computation using (
8
), yields that it is a group homomorphism. It is also order-preserving since the matrix
R
is a non-negative integral matrix. Note that the
\mathbb{Z}[x_{1}^{\pm 1},x_{2}^{\pm 1},...,x_{k}^{\pm 1}]
-module structure on the dimension groups is coming from the
\mathbb{Z}^{k}
-monoid actions on the respective positive cones (see (
5
)). So

{}^{x_{1}^{m_{1}}x_{2}^{m_{2}}\cdots x_{k}^{m_{k}}}[xR,\mathbf{n}]=[xR,\mathbf{n}+\displaystyle{\sum_{i=1}^{k}}m_{i}\mathbf{e}_{i}]=R([x,\mathbf{n}+\displaystyle{\sum_{i=1}^{k}}m_{i}\mathbf{e}_{i}])=R(^{x_{1}^{m_{1}}x_{2}^{m_{2}}\cdots x_{k}^{m_{k}}}[x,\mathbf{n}]).

which shows that
R
is a module homomorphism. Now define a map

\displaystyle\mathfrak{h}_{R}:K_{0}^{\operatorname{gr}}(\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Lambda))

\displaystyle\longrightarrow K_{0}^{\operatorname{gr}}(\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Omega))

\displaystyle w

\displaystyle\longmapsto(\gamma_{\Omega}^{-1}\circ R\circ\gamma_{\Lambda})(w)

for all
w\in K_{0}^{\operatorname{gr}}(\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Lambda))
. Clearly
\mathfrak{h}_{R}
is the unique
\mathbb{Z}[x_{1}^{\pm 1},x_{2}^{\pm 1},\ldots,x_{k}^{\pm 1}]
-module homomorphism making diagram (
7
) commute.

(ii)
Suppose we have an order-preserving
\mathbb{Z}[x_{1}^{\pm 1},x_{2}^{\pm 1},\ldots,x_{k}^{\pm 1}]
-module homomorphism

\mathfrak{h}:K_{0}^{\operatorname{gr}}(\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Lambda))\longrightarrow K_{0}^{\operatorname{gr}}(\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Omega)).

Then we can obtain an order-preserving
\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}^{k}]
-module homomorphism
\theta:H_{\Lambda}\longrightarrow H_{\Omega}
such that the diagram
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K_{0}^{\operatorname{gr}}(\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Lambda))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K_{0}^{\operatorname{gr}}(\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Omega))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{\Lambda}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{\Omega}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{h}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma_{\Lambda}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma_{\Omega}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta}

commutes, namely,
\theta=\gamma_{\Omega}\circ\mathfrak{h}\circ\gamma_{\Lambda}^{-1}
.

To show
(ii)
, it suffices to find a matrix
R\in\mathbb{M}_{\Lambda^{0}\times\Omega^{0}}(\mathbb{N})
and integers
r_{1},r_{2},...,r_{k}
such that

A_{\mathbf{e}_{i}}R=RB_{\mathbf{e}_{i}}

for all
i=1,2,...,k
and

\theta([x,\mathbf{n}])=~^{x_{1}^{r_{1}}x_{2}^{r_{2}}\cdots x_{k}^{r_{k}}}[xR,\mathbf{n}]

for all
[x,\mathbf{n}]\in H_{\Lambda}
.

Let
\Lambda^{0}=\{v_{1},v_{2},...,v_{|\Lambda^{0}|}\}
and
[y^{(i)},\mathbf{l}^{(i)}]:=\theta([\epsilon_{i},0])
for all
i=1,2,\ldots,|\Lambda^{0}|
. Set
\mathbf{m}:=\displaystyle{\bigvee_{i=1}^{|\Lambda^{0}|}}\mathbf{l}^{(i)}
. Then

[y^{(i)},\mathbf{l}^{(i)}]=[y^{(i)}B_{\mathbf{m}-\mathbf{l}^{(i)}},\mathbf{m}]

for all
i=1,2,...,|\Lambda^{0}|
. Since
\theta
is order-preserving, each
y^{(i)}
and consequently
y^{(i)}B_{\mathbf{m}-\mathbf{l}^{(i)}}\in\mathbb{N}^{\Omega^{0}}
. Now we define a matrix
R^{\prime}\in\mathbb{M}_{\Lambda^{0}\times\Omega^{0}}(\mathbb{N})
such that the
i^{\text{th}}
row of
R^{\prime}
is
y^{(i)}B_{\mathbf{m}-\mathbf{l}^{(i)}}
for every
1\leq i\leq|\Lambda^{0}|
. Let
[x,\mathbf{n}]\in H_{\Lambda}
. Since
\theta
is a
\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}^{k}]
-module homomorphism we have,

\displaystyle\theta([x,\mathbf{n}])=\theta([\displaystyle{\sum_{i=1}^{|\Lambda^{0}|}}x_{i}\epsilon_{i},\mathbf{n}])

\displaystyle=\displaystyle{\sum_{i=1}^{|\Lambda^{0}|}}\theta([x_{i}\epsilon_{i},\mathbf{n}])

\displaystyle=~^{\mathbf{n}}\left(\displaystyle{\sum_{i=1}^{|\Lambda^{0}|}}x_{i}\theta([\epsilon_{i},0])\right)

\displaystyle=~^{\mathbf{n}}(\displaystyle{\sum_{i=1}^{|\Lambda^{0}|}}x_{i}[y^{(i)}B_{\mathbf{m}-\mathbf{l}^{(i)}},\mathbf{m}])

\displaystyle=~^{\mathbf{n}}[\displaystyle{\sum_{i=1}^{|\Lambda^{0}|}}x_{i}y^{(i)}B_{\mathbf{m}-\mathbf{l}^{(i)}},\mathbf{m}]=~^{\mathbf{n}}[xR^{\prime},\mathbf{m}]=[xR^{\prime},\mathbf{m}+\mathbf{n}].

Now for any
i\in\{1,2,\ldots,k\}
and
j\in\{1,2,\ldots,|\Lambda^{0}|\}
we have,

[\epsilon_{j}A_{\mathbf{e}_{i}}R^{\prime},\mathbf{m}]=\theta([\epsilon_{j}A_{\mathbf{e}_{i}},0])=\theta([\epsilon_{j},-\mathbf{e}_{i}])=[\epsilon_{j}R^{\prime},\mathbf{m}-\mathbf{e}_{i}]=[\epsilon_{j}R^{\prime}B_{\mathbf{e}_{i}},\mathbf{m}].

Therefore there is some
\mathbf{r}(i,j)\in\mathbb{N}^{k}
such that
(\epsilon_{j}A_{\mathbf{e}_{i}}R^{\prime})B_{\mathbf{r}(i,j)}=(\epsilon_{j}R^{\prime}B_{\mathbf{e}_{i}})B_{\mathbf{r}(i,j)}
. Set
\mathbf{r}=\displaystyle{\bigvee_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq i\leq k\\
1\leq j\leq|\Lambda^{0}|\end{subarray}}}\mathbf{r}(i,j)
. Then
\epsilon_{j}A_{\mathbf{e}_{i}}R^{\prime}B_{\mathbf{r}}=\epsilon_{j}R^{\prime}B_{\mathbf{e}_{i}}B_{\mathbf{r}}
for all
1\leq i\leq k
and
1\leq j\leq|\Lambda^{0}|
. Finally, we define the matrix
R:=R^{\prime}B_{\mathbf{r}}
. So for any
1\leq i\leq k
,
\epsilon_{j}A_{\mathbf{e}_{i}}R=\epsilon_{j}RB_{\mathbf{e}_{i}}
for all
j=1,2,\ldots,|\Lambda^{0}|
. Thus
A_{\mathbf{e}_{i}}R=RB_{\mathbf{e}_{i}}
for all
i=1,2,\ldots,k
. Also

\theta([x,\mathbf{n}])=[xR^{\prime},\mathbf{m}+\mathbf{n}]=[xR^{\prime}B_{\mathbf{r}},\mathbf{m}+\mathbf{n}+\mathbf{r}]=[xR,\mathbf{m}+\mathbf{n}+\mathbf{r}]=~^{x_{1}^{m_{1}+r_{1}}x_{2}^{m_{2}+r_{2}}\cdots x_{k}^{m_{k}+r_{k}}}[xR,\mathbf{n}],

for all
[x,\mathbf{n}]\in H_{\Lambda}
and we are done.
∎

In the following result we connect isomorphism between dimension groups of two
k
-graphs with a certain relation between their matrices and also with isomorphism between the graded homologies of their respective path groupoids.

Theorem 5.4
.

Let
\Lambda
and
\Omega
be row-finite
k
-graphs without sources such that both
\Lambda^{0},\Omega^{0}
are finite. For each
\mathbf{n}\in\mathbb{N}^{k}
, suppose
A_{\mathbf{n}}\in\mathbb{M}_{\Lambda^{0}}(\mathbb{N})
and
B_{\mathbf{n}}\in\mathbb{M}_{\Omega^{0}}(\mathbb{N})
are matrices such that
A_{\mathbf{n}}(u,v):=|u\Lambda^{\mathbf{n}}v|
for
u,v\in\Lambda^{0}
and
B_{\mathbf{n}}(x,y):=|x\Omega^{\mathbf{n}}y|
for
x,y\in\Omega^{0}
. Then the following are equivalent.

(i)
There exist
R\in\mathbb{M}_{\Lambda^{0}\times\Omega^{0}}(\mathbb{N})
,
S\in\mathbb{M}_{\Omega^{0}\times\Lambda^{0}}(\mathbb{N})
and
\mathbf{p}\in\mathbb{N}^{k}
such that

A_{\mathbf{p}}=RS,~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~B_{\mathbf{p}}=SR

and

A_{\mathbf{e}_{i}}R=RB_{\mathbf{e}_{i}},~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~B_{\mathbf{e}_{i}}S=SA_{\mathbf{e}_{i}}

for all
i=1,2,\ldots,k
.

(ii)
There exists a group isomorphism
\phi:H_{\Lambda}\longrightarrow H_{\Omega}
such that
\phi(H_{\Lambda}^{+})=H_{\Omega}^{+}
and
\phi\circ\delta_{i}^{\Lambda}=\delta_{i}^{\Omega}\circ\phi
for all
i=1,2,...,k
, where
\delta_{i}^{\Lambda}
,
\delta_{i}^{\Omega}
are the respective automorphisms of
H_{\Lambda}
and
H_{\Omega}
described in (
4
).

(iii)
The talented monoids
T_{\Lambda}
and
T_{\Omega}
are isomorphic as
\mathbb{Z}^{k}
-monoids.

(iv)
There is a
\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}^{k}]
-module isomorphism
\varphi:H_{0}^{\operatorname{gr}}(\mathcal{G}_{\Lambda})\longrightarrow H_{0}^{\operatorname{gr}}(\mathcal{G}_{\Omega})
such that
\varphi(H_{0}^{\operatorname{gr}}(\mathcal{G}_{\Lambda})^{+})=H_{0}^{\operatorname{gr}}(\mathcal{G}_{\Omega})^{+}
.

Furthermore, the isomorphism
\phi
in
(ii)
is pointed, i.e., preserves the order units if and only if in
(iv)
,
\varphi([1_{\Lambda^{\infty}\times\{0\}}])=[1_{\Omega^{\infty}\times\{0\}}]
.

Proof.

The implications
(ii)\Longleftrightarrow(iii)
and
(ii)\Longleftrightarrow(iv)
follow from Theorem
4.6
and Theorem
5.1
. Note that since
\Lambda^{0},\Omega^{0}
are finite, so
\Lambda^{\infty}\times\{0\}
and
\Omega^{\infty}\times\{0\}
are compact. The isomorphism in
(ii)
is pointed i.e., takes
[(1~1~\cdots~1)_{1\times|\Lambda^{0}|},0]
to
[(1~1~\cdots~1)_{1\times|\Omega^{0}|},0]
if and only if, at the level of talented monoids,
\displaystyle{\sum_{v\in\Lambda^{0}}}v(0)\longmapsto\displaystyle{\sum_{w\in\Omega^{0}}}w(0)
, which in turn is equivalent, in view of the isomorphism
\Phi
of Theorem
4.6
, to the condition that

\varphi([1_{\Lambda^{\infty}\times\{0\}}])=\varphi\left(\displaystyle{\sum_{v\in\Lambda^{0}}}[1_{Z(v)\times\{0\}}]\right)=\displaystyle{\sum_{w\in\Omega^{0}}}[1_{Z(w)\times\{0\}}]=[1_{\Omega^{\infty}\times\{0\}}].

In the remaining part of the proof we show that
(i)\Longleftrightarrow(ii)
.

(i)\Longrightarrow(ii)
. Consider the following map

\displaystyle\phi:H_{\Lambda}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow H_{\Omega}

\displaystyle[x,\mathbf{n}]

\displaystyle\longmapsto[xR,\mathbf{n}]

for all
[x,\mathbf{n}]\in H_{\Lambda}
. Using the same argument as in the proof of Lemma
5.3

(i)
, it can be shown that
\phi
is a well-defined group homomorphism. For injectivity, choose
[x,\mathbf{n}],[y,\mathbf{m}]\in H_{\Lambda}
such that
\phi([x,\mathbf{n}])=\phi([y,\mathbf{m}])
. Then
[xR,\mathbf{n}]=[yR,\mathbf{m}]
in
H_{\Omega}
. Hence, we have an
\mathbf{l}\in\mathbb{Z}^{k}
with
\mathbf{l}\geq\mathbf{n}\vee\mathbf{m}
such that
(xR)B_{\mathbf{l}-\mathbf{n}}=(yR)B_{\mathbf{l}-\mathbf{m}}
. Since
\mathbf{p}\in\mathbb{N}^{k}
,
\mathbf{l}+\mathbf{p}\geq\mathbf{n}\vee\mathbf{m}
. Now we have

xA_{(\mathbf{l}+\mathbf{p})-\mathbf{n}}=xA_{\mathbf{l}-\mathbf{n}}A_{\mathbf{p}}=(xA_{\mathbf{l}-\mathbf{n}}R)S=(xRB_{\mathbf{l}-\mathbf{n}})S=(yRB_{\mathbf{l}-\mathbf{m}})S=(yA_{\mathbf{l}-\mathbf{m}}R)S=yA_{\mathbf{l}-\mathbf{m}}A_{\mathbf{p}}=yA_{(\mathbf{l}+\mathbf{p})-\mathbf{m}}.

Thus
[x,\mathbf{n}]=[y,\mathbf{m}]
. For surjectivity, let
[w,\mathbf{m}]\in H_{\Omega}
. Then
[wS,\mathbf{m}+\mathbf{p}]\in H_{\Lambda}
. Now

\phi([wS,\mathbf{m}+\mathbf{p}])=[wSR,\mathbf{m}+\mathbf{p}]=[wB_{\mathbf{p}},\mathbf{m}+\mathbf{p}]=[w,\mathbf{m}].

Therefore
\phi
is an isomorphism. Since
R
and
S
are non-negative integral matrices, it follows that
\phi(H_{\Lambda}^{+})=H_{\Omega}^{+}
. That
\phi
intertwines
\delta_{i}^{\Lambda}
with
\delta_{i}^{\Omega}
is evident from its definition.

(i)\Longleftarrow(ii)
. Suppose
\phi:H_{\Lambda}\longrightarrow H_{\Omega}
is an order-preserving
\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}^{k}]
-module isomorphism with inverse
\psi:H_{\Omega}\longrightarrow H_{\Lambda}
. In view of Lemma
5.3

(ii)
, we have matrices
R\in\mathbb{M}_{\Lambda^{0}\times\Omega^{0}}(\mathbb{N})
,
S\in\mathbb{M}_{\Omega^{0}\times\Lambda^{0}}(\mathbb{N})
and
\mathbf{r},\mathbf{s}\in\mathbb{Z}^{k}
such that

A_{\mathbf{e}_{i}}R=RB_{\mathbf{e}_{i}},~~~~~~~~~B_{\mathbf{e}_{i}}S=SA_{\mathbf{e}_{i}}

for all
i=1,2,...,k
and

\phi([x,\mathbf{n}])=~^{\mathbf{r}}[xR,\mathbf{n}],~~~~~~~~~\psi([y,\mathbf{m}])=~^{\mathbf{s}}[yS,\mathbf{m}]

for all
[x,\mathbf{n}]\in H_{\Lambda}
and
[y,\mathbf{m}]\in H_{\Omega}
.

Now for any
j=1,2,\ldots,|\Omega^{0}|
,
(\phi\psi)([\epsilon_{j},0])=([\epsilon_{j},0])
. So
[\epsilon_{j}SR,\mathbf{s}+\mathbf{r}]=[\epsilon_{j},0]
. Therefore,

(\epsilon_{j}SR)B_{\mathbf{t}^{(j)}-(\mathbf{s}+\mathbf{r})}=\epsilon_{j}B_{\mathbf{t}^{(j)}}

for some
\mathbf{t}^{(j)}\geq(\mathbf{s}+\mathbf{r})\vee 0
. Let
\mathbf{t}=\displaystyle{\bigvee_{j=1}^{|\Omega^{0}|}}\mathbf{t}^{(j)}
. Then for all
j=1,2,\ldots,|\Omega^{0}|
we have
(\epsilon_{j}SR)B_{\mathbf{t}-(\mathbf{s}+\mathbf{r})}=\epsilon_{j}B_{\mathbf{t}}
which implies
S(RB_{\mathbf{t}-(\mathbf{s}+\mathbf{r})})=B_{\mathbf{t}}
. Similarly, using the fact that
\psi\phi=id_{H_{\Lambda}}
, we can find
\mathbf{q}\geq(\mathbf{s}+\mathbf{r})\vee 0
such that
R(SA_{\mathbf{q}-(\mathbf{s}+\mathbf{r})})=A_{\mathbf{q}}
. Now set
\mathcal{R}=RB_{\mathbf{t}-(\mathbf{s}+\mathbf{r})}
and
\mathcal{S}=SA_{\mathbf{q}-(\mathbf{s}+\mathbf{r})}
. Then

A_{\mathbf{e}_{i}}\mathcal{R}=A_{\mathbf{e}_{i}}RB_{\mathbf{t}-(\mathbf{s}+\mathbf{r})}=RB_{\mathbf{e}_{i}}B_{\mathbf{t}-(\mathbf{s}+\mathbf{r})}=\mathcal{R}B_{\mathbf{e}_{i}}.

Similarly
B_{\mathbf{e}_{i}}\mathcal{S}=\mathcal{S}B_{\mathbf{e}_{i}}
for all
i=1,2,...,k
. Finally

\mathcal{R}\mathcal{S}=(RB_{\mathbf{t}-(\mathbf{s}+\mathbf{r})})(SA_{\mathbf{q}-(\mathbf{s}+\mathbf{r})})=RSA_{\mathbf{t}-(\mathbf{s}+\mathbf{r})}A_{\mathbf{q}-(\mathbf{s}+\mathbf{r})}=A_{\mathbf{q}}A_{\mathbf{t}-(\mathbf{s}+\mathbf{r})}=A_{(\mathbf{q}+\mathbf{t})-(\mathbf{s}+\mathbf{r})},

and

\mathcal{S}\mathcal{R}=(SA_{\mathbf{q}-(\mathbf{s}+\mathbf{r})})(RRB_{\mathbf{t}-(\mathbf{s}+\mathbf{r})})=SRB_{\mathbf{q}-(\mathbf{s}+\mathbf{r})}B_{\mathbf{t}-(\mathbf{s}+\mathbf{r})}=B_{\mathbf{t}}B_{\mathbf{q}-(\mathbf{s}+\mathbf{r})}=B_{(\mathbf{q}+\mathbf{t})-(\mathbf{s}+\mathbf{r})}.

Now letting
\mathbf{p}=(\mathbf{q}+\mathbf{t})-(\mathbf{s}+\mathbf{r})
, we are done.
∎

Remark 5.5
.

Suppose
v
is a vertex in
\Lambda
and
\Lambda_{I}
is the in-split of
\Lambda
at
v
with respect to a partition
v\Lambda^{\mathbf{1}}=\mathcal{E}_{1}\cup\mathcal{E}_{2}
. Moreover, suppose
A_{\mathbf{n}},B_{\mathbf{n}}
,
\mathbf{n}\in\mathbb{N}^{k}
are the vertex matrices for
\Lambda
and
\Lambda_{I}
respectively. We observe that these matrices are related as in Condition
(i)
of Theorem
5.4
. Fix any
j\in\{1,2,\ldots,k\}
and define two matrices
R\in\mathbb{M}_{\Lambda^{0}\times\Lambda_{I}^{0}}(\mathbb{N})
,
S\in\mathbb{M}_{\Lambda_{I}^{0}\times\Lambda^{0}}(\mathbb{N})
as

R(u,w):=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}1&\mbox{if }u=par(w),\\
0&\mbox{otherwise}\end{array}\right.~\text{and}~S(u,w):=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}|\Lambda^{\mathbf{e}_{j}}w\cap\mathcal{E}_{i}|&\mbox{if }u=v^{i}~\text{for}~i=1,2,\\
|u\Lambda^{\mathbf{e}_{j}}w|&\mbox{otherwise. }\end{array}\right.

It is not hard to show that
RS=A_{\mathbf{e}_{j}}
,
SR=B_{\mathbf{e}_{j}}
and
A_{\mathbf{e}_{i}}R=RB_{\mathbf{e}_{i}}
,
B_{\mathbf{e}_{i}}S=SA_{\mathbf{e}_{i}}
for all
i=1,2,...,k
. So we can provide an alternative proof of Theorem
3.2
in view of the equivalence
(i)\Longleftrightarrow(iii)
of Theorem
5.4
.

In symbolic dynamics, state splitting i.e., in-splitting and out-splitting of directed graphs preserve conjugacy of their edge shifts and consequently the corresponding adjacency matrices of the original graph and the transformed graph are
strongly shift equivalent
hence
shift equivalent
(see
[
37
, Theorems 7.2.7 and 7.3.3]
). These are among the key moves preserving Morita equivalence of Leavitt path algebras. In the study of
k
-graphs, in-splitting is expected to be a crucial move in connection with the geometric classification of higher-rank graph
C^{*}
-algebras (as well as of Kumjian-Pask algebras in view of Proposition
3.1
). The vertex matrices of a
k
-graph
\Lambda
and its in-split
\Lambda_{I}
naturally satisfy condition
(i)
of Theorem
5.4
, which suggests that this matrix condition is not an abstract one; rather, it may be considered as a suitable analogue of the notion of shift equivalence (eventual conjugacy) in the higher-dimensional setting. However, in two dimensions, though the solution to the conjugacy problem for shifts of finite type is fairly clear, it does not use matrices. In-splitting of a finite
2
-graph may be studied using the textile systems introduced in
[
40
]
. In
[
14
]
it is shown that any
2
-graph in-splitting may be described by a finite sequence of textile moves as described by Johnson and Madden in
[
33
]
. Each of the moves given in
[
33
]
induces a conjugacy of the underlying two-dimensional shift of finite type.

6.
Lifting morphisms of graded
K
-theories of Kumjian-Pask algebras

Let
\mathsf{k}
be a field. By
\mathbb{K}\mathbb{P}_{\mathsf{k}}
, we denote the category whose objects are Kumjian-Pask
\mathsf{k}
-algebras of row-finite
k
-graphs without sources with the vertex sets finite and morphisms are
\mathbb{Z}^{k}
-graded algebra homomorphisms modulo conjugation by invertible elements of degree
0
. On the other hand, let
\mathbb{P}_{\mathbb{Z}^{k}}
be the category of pointed
\mathbb{Z}^{k}
-pre-ordered abelian groups with pointed order-preserving group homomorphisms (or equivalently, pointed order-preserving
\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}^{k}]
-module homomorphisms).

In this section we wish to find an answer to the following:

Question 6.1
.

Consider the functor
K_{0}^{\operatorname{gr}}:\mathbb{K}\mathbb{P}_{\mathsf{k}}\longrightarrow\mathbb{P}_{\mathbb{Z}^{k}}
defined as follows:

\displaystyle K_{0}^{\operatorname{gr}}:\operatorname{Obj}(\mathbb{K}\mathbb{P}_{\mathsf{k}})

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\operatorname{Obj}(\mathbb{P}_{\mathbb{Z}^{k}})

\displaystyle\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Lambda)

\displaystyle\longmapsto K_{0}^{\operatorname{gr}}(\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Lambda)),

\displaystyle K_{0}^{\operatorname{gr}}:\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbb{K}\mathbb{P}_{\mathsf{k}}}(\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Lambda),\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Omega))

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbb{P}_{\mathbb{Z}^{k}}}(K_{0}^{\operatorname{gr}}(\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Lambda)),K_{0}^{\operatorname{gr}}(\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Omega)))

\displaystyle\psi

\displaystyle\longmapsto K_{0}^{\operatorname{gr}}(\psi),

where
K_{0}^{\operatorname{gr}}(\psi)([P]):=[P\otimes_{\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Lambda)}\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Omega)]
for all graded finitely generated projective right
\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Lambda)
-module
P
. Given
\mathfrak{h}\in\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbb{P}_{\mathbb{Z}^{k}}}(K_{0}^{\operatorname{gr}}(\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Lambda)),K_{0}^{\operatorname{gr}}(\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Omega)))
, when there exists
\psi\in\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbb{K}\mathbb{P}_{\mathsf{k}}}(\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Lambda),\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Omega))
such that
K_{0}^{\operatorname{gr}}(\psi)=\mathfrak{h}
?

6.1.
Polymorphisms and bimodules associated to
k
-graphs

For any
k
-graph
\Lambda
and
\mathbf{n}\in\mathbb{N}^{k}
, we define the
\mathbf{n}
-shaped directed graph

E_{\mathbf{n}}^{\Lambda}
where the vertex set is
\Lambda^{0}
and edge set is
\Lambda^{\mathbf{n}}
; the
range
and
source
maps being the restrictions of
r
and
s
on
\Lambda^{n}
respectively. Considering
E_{\mathbf{n}}^{\Lambda}
as the polymorphism
1
1

1

In
[
2
]
, a polymorphism
E=(U,V,E^{1},r,s)
is regarded as a set of arrows originated from
U
and terminated at
V
; whereas we choose a categorical convention in our polymorphisms i.e., the arrows are directed from
V
to
U
; consequently the product of our polymorphisms is considered from right to left.

(\Lambda^{0},\Lambda^{0},\Lambda^{\mathbf{n}},r,s)
, one can note that
E_{\mathbf{n}}^{\Lambda}\cong\displaystyle{\prod_{i=1}^{k}}(E_{\mathbf{e}_{i}}^{\Lambda})^{n_{i}}
, where
E_{\mathbf{e}_{i}}^{\Lambda}
is the
i^{\text{th}}
-coordinate graph associated with
\Lambda
and the product is the composition of polymorphisms. The order of the components does not matter in the product since for any two distinct
i,j\in\{1,2,...,k\}
,
E_{\mathbf{e}_{i}}^{\Lambda}\circ E_{\mathbf{e}_{j}}^{\Lambda}\cong E_{\mathbf{e}_{j}}^{\Lambda}\circ E_{\mathbf{e}_{i}}^{\Lambda}
via the isomorphism
\tilde{\kappa}_{\Lambda,i,j}:=\kappa_{j,i}^{-1}\circ\kappa_{i,j}
where

\displaystyle\kappa_{i,j}:E_{\mathbf{e}_{i}}^{\Lambda}\circ E_{\mathbf{e}_{j}}^{\Lambda}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow E_{\mathbf{e}_{i}+\mathbf{e}_{j}}^{\Lambda}

\displaystyle(\lambda,\mu)

\displaystyle\longmapsto\lambda\mu.

Now for any
\mathbf{n}\in\mathbb{N}^{k}
, suppose
\mathsf{k}\Lambda^{\mathbf{n}}
is the
\mathsf{k}
-vector space with basis
\Lambda^{\mathbf{n}}
. Then
\mathsf{k}\Lambda^{\mathbf{n}}
can be considered as a
\mathsf{k}\Lambda^{0}-\mathsf{k}\Lambda^{0}
-bimodule with respect to the actions:

v\lambda:=\delta_{v,r(\lambda)}\lambda,~~~~~~~~~~~~~\lambda w:=\delta_{w,s(\lambda)}\lambda

for all
v,w\in\Lambda^{0}
and
\lambda\in\Lambda^{\mathbf{n}}
. In other words,
\mathsf{k}\Lambda^{\mathbf{n}}
is the bimodule of the polymorphism
E_{\mathbf{n}}^{\Lambda}
.

Lemma 6.2
.

Let
\Lambda
be a row-finite
k
-graph without sources such that
|\Lambda^{0}|<\infty
. Then for
\mathbf{n},\mathbf{m}\in\mathbb{N}^{k}
, we have a
\mathsf{k}\Lambda^{0}-\mathsf{k}\Lambda^{0}
-bimodule isomorphism

\phi_{\mathbf{n},\mathbf{m}}^{\Lambda}:\mathsf{k}\Lambda^{\mathbf{n}+\mathbf{m}}\longrightarrow\mathsf{k}\Lambda^{\mathbf{n}}\otimes_{\mathsf{k}\Lambda^{0}}\mathsf{k}\Lambda^{\mathbf{m}},

such that for any
\lambda\in\Lambda^{\mathbf{n}+\mathbf{m}}
,
\phi_{\mathbf{n},\mathbf{m}}^{\Lambda}(\lambda)=\lambda_{1}\otimes\lambda_{2}
, where
\lambda=\lambda_{1}\lambda_{2}
is the unique factorization of
\lambda
into paths of shape
\mathbf{n},\mathbf{m}
respectively.

Proof.

Applying
[
2
, Proposition 3.8]
, we have
\mathsf{k}\Lambda^{\mathbf{n}}\otimes_{\mathsf{k}\Lambda^{0}}\mathsf{k}\Lambda^{\mathbf{m}}=\mathsf{k}(E_{\mathbf{n}}^{\Lambda})^{1}\otimes_{\mathsf{k}\Lambda^{0}}\mathsf{k}(E_{\mathbf{m}}^{\Lambda})^{1}\cong\mathsf{k}(E_{\mathbf{n}}^{\Lambda}\circ E_{\mathbf{m}}^{\Lambda})^{1}
via the isomorphism

\displaystyle\chi:\mathsf{k}(E_{\mathbf{n}}^{\Lambda})^{1}\otimes_{\mathsf{k}\Lambda^{0}}\mathsf{k}(E_{\mathbf{m}}^{\Lambda})^{1}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\mathsf{k}(E_{\mathbf{n}}^{\Lambda}\circ E_{\mathbf{m}}^{\Lambda})^{1}

\displaystyle\left(\displaystyle{\sum_{\alpha\in\Lambda^{\mathbf{n}}}}a_{\alpha}\alpha\otimes\displaystyle{\sum_{\beta\in\Lambda^{\mathbf{m}}}}b_{\beta}\beta\right)

\displaystyle\longmapsto\displaystyle{\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(\alpha,\beta)\in\Lambda^{\mathbf{n}}\times\Lambda^{\mathbf{m}}\\
r(\beta)=s(\alpha)\end{subarray}}}a_{\alpha}b_{\beta}(\alpha,\beta).

Again the isomorphism

\displaystyle\kappa_{\mathbf{n},\mathbf{m}}:E_{\mathbf{n}}^{\Lambda}\circ E_{\mathbf{m}}^{\Lambda}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow E_{\mathbf{n}+\mathbf{m}}^{\Lambda}

\displaystyle(\alpha,\beta)

\displaystyle\longmapsto\alpha\beta,

of polymorphisms induces an isomorphism of
\mathsf{k}\Lambda^{0}-\mathsf{k}\Lambda^{0}
-bimodules
\overline{\kappa_{\mathbf{n},\mathbf{m}}}:\mathsf{k}(E_{\mathbf{n}}^{\Lambda}\circ E_{\mathbf{m}}^{\Lambda})^{1}\longrightarrow\mathsf{k}(E_{\mathbf{n}+\mathbf{m}}^{\Lambda})^{1}
. Finally set
\phi_{\mathbf{n},\mathbf{m}}^{\Lambda}=\chi^{-1}\circ\overline{\kappa_{\mathbf{n},\mathbf{m}}}^{-1}
. Then
\phi_{\mathbf{n},\mathbf{m}}^{\Lambda}
is the required isomorphism.
∎

Remark 6.3
.

One can extend the above lemma for any number of shapes rather than just two. For instance, if
\mathbf{n},\mathbf{m},\mathbf{p}\in\mathbb{N}^{k}
then clearly the composition
(\phi_{\mathbf{n},\mathbf{m}}^{\Lambda}\otimes id_{\mathsf{k}\Lambda^{\mathbf{p}}})\circ\phi_{\mathbf{n}+\mathbf{m},\mathbf{p}}^{\Lambda}
is an isomorphism from
\mathsf{k}\Lambda^{\mathbf{n}+\mathbf{m}+\mathbf{p}}
to
(\mathsf{k}\Lambda^{\mathbf{n}}\otimes_{\mathsf{k}\Lambda^{0}}\mathsf{k}\Lambda^{\mathbf{m}})\otimes_{\mathsf{k}\Lambda^{0}}\mathsf{k}\Lambda^{\mathbf{p}}
. The readers should be convinced that the other possible isomorphism of this type (obtained from different association of the shapes) is in fact the same as the said one due to the unique factorization property. Taking into account the associativity of tensor products, we regard this as a canonical isomorphism

\phi_{\mathbf{n},\mathbf{m},\mathbf{p}}^{\Lambda}:\mathsf{k}\Lambda^{\mathbf{n}+\mathbf{m}+\mathbf{p}}\longrightarrow\mathsf{k}\Lambda^{\mathbf{n}}\otimes_{\mathsf{k}\Lambda^{0}}\mathsf{k}\Lambda^{\mathbf{m}}\otimes_{\mathsf{k}\Lambda^{0}}\mathsf{k}\Lambda^{\mathbf{p}}.

In general for
\mathbf{n}=(n_{1},n_{2},...,n_{k})\in\mathbb{N}^{k}
, we can proceed in this way to obtain an isomorphism

\phi^{\Lambda}:\mathsf{k}\Lambda^{\mathbf{n}}\longrightarrow(\mathsf{k}\Lambda^{\mathbf{e}_{1}})^{\otimes n_{1}}\otimes_{\mathsf{k}\Lambda^{0}}(\mathsf{k}\Lambda^{\mathbf{e}_{2}})^{\otimes n_{2}}\otimes_{\mathsf{k}\Lambda^{0}}\dots\otimes_{\mathsf{k}\Lambda^{0}}(\mathsf{k}\Lambda^{\mathbf{e}_{k}})^{\otimes n_{k}}.

Proposition 6.4
.

Let
\Lambda
be a row-finite
k
-graph without sources such that
|\Lambda^{0}|<\infty
. Then for any
\mathbf{n}\in\mathbb{N}^{k}
, there exists a
\mathsf{k}\Lambda^{0}-\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Lambda)
-bimodule isomorphism

\rho_{\Lambda,\mathbf{n}}:\mathsf{k}\Lambda^{\mathbf{n}}\otimes_{\mathsf{k}\Lambda^{0}}\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Lambda)\longrightarrow\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Lambda),

such that for any
x=\displaystyle{\sum_{\lambda\in\Lambda^{\mathbf{n}}}}a_{\lambda}\lambda\in\mathsf{k}\Lambda^{\mathbf{n}}
and
w\in\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Lambda)
,
\rho_{\Lambda,\mathbf{n}}(x\otimes w)=(\displaystyle{\sum_{\lambda\in\Lambda^{\mathbf{n}}}}a_{\lambda}s_{\lambda})w
. Furthermore, the diagram

(9)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{k}\Lambda^{\mathbf{n}+\mathbf{m}}\otimes_{\mathsf{k}\Lambda^{0}}\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Lambda)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Lambda)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{k}\Lambda^{\mathbf{n}}\otimes_{\mathsf{k}\Lambda^{0}}\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Lambda)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathsf{k}\Lambda^{\mathbf{n}}\otimes_{\mathsf{k}\Lambda^{0}}\mathsf{k}\Lambda^{\mathbf{m}})\otimes_{\mathsf{k}\Lambda^{0}}\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Lambda)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{k}\Lambda^{\mathbf{n}}\otimes_{\mathsf{k}\Lambda^{0}}(\mathsf{k}\Lambda^{\mathbf{m}}\otimes_{\mathsf{k}\Lambda^{0}}\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Lambda))}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho_{\Lambda,\mathbf{n}+\mathbf{m}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi_{\mathbf{n},\mathbf{m}}^{\Lambda}\otimes id_{\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Lambda)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho_{\Lambda,\mathbf{n}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{{}_{\mathsf{k}\Lambda^{\mathbf{n}},\mathsf{k}\Lambda^{\mathbf{m}},\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Lambda)}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces id_{\mathsf{k}\Lambda^{\mathbf{n}}}\otimes\rho_{\Lambda,\mathbf{m}}}

is commutative for all
\mathbf{n},\mathbf{m}\in\mathbb{N}^{k}
.

Proof.

Let
X:=\Lambda^{0}\cup\Lambda^{\neq 0}\cup G(\Lambda^{\neq 0})
and
\mathbb{F}_{\mathsf{k}}(\omega(X))
, the free
\mathsf{k}
-algebra on
X
. Consider the canonical surjection
\pi:\mathbb{F}_{\mathsf{k}}(\omega(X))\longrightarrow\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Lambda)
defined on generators as

v\longmapsto p_{v},~~~~\lambda\longmapsto s_{\lambda},~~~~~\mu^{*}\longmapsto s_{\mu^{*}}

for all
v\in\Lambda^{0}
,
\lambda,\mu\in\Lambda^{\neq 0}
. Now with the help of the map
\pi
we define

\displaystyle\rho_{\Lambda,\mathbf{n}}:\mathsf{k}\Lambda^{\mathbf{n}}\otimes_{\mathsf{k}\Lambda^{0}}\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Lambda)

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Lambda)

\displaystyle\displaystyle{\sum_{i=1}^{N}}x_{i}\otimes w_{i}

\displaystyle\longmapsto\displaystyle{\sum_{i=1}^{N}}\pi(x_{i})w_{i}.

A routine verification yields that
\rho_{\Lambda,\mathbf{n}}
is a well-defined
\mathsf{k}\Lambda^{0}-\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Lambda)
-bimodule homomorphism. For surjectivity, it suffices to show that the elements of the form
s_{\lambda}s_{\mu^{*}}
of
\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Lambda)
are in the image of
\rho_{\Lambda,\mathbf{n}}
. So take
\lambda,\mu\in\Lambda
such that
s(\lambda)=s(\mu)
. By using
(KP4)
, we can write
p_{r(\lambda)}=\displaystyle{\sum_{\alpha\in r(\lambda)\Lambda^{n}}}s_{\alpha}s_{\alpha^{*}}
. Thus we have

s_{\lambda}s_{\mu^{*}}=p_{r(\lambda)}s_{\lambda}s_{\mu^{*}}=\displaystyle{\sum_{\alpha\in r(\lambda)\Lambda^{n}}}s_{\alpha}(s_{\alpha^{*}}s_{\lambda}s_{\mu^{*}})=\displaystyle{\sum_{\alpha\in r(\lambda)\Lambda^{n}}}\pi(\alpha)(s_{\alpha^{*}}s_{\lambda}s_{\mu^{*}})=\rho_{\Lambda,\mathbf{n}}\left(\displaystyle{\sum_{\alpha\in r(\lambda)\Lambda^{\mathbf{n}}}}\alpha\otimes s_{\alpha^{*}}s_{\lambda}s_{\mu^{*}}\right).

Before proving that
\rho_{\Lambda,\mathbf{n}}
is injective let us check the commutativity of Diagram (
9
). Since all the maps involved are bimodule homomorphisms, it suffices to show that
\rho_{\Lambda,\mathbf{n}}\circ(id_{\mathsf{k}\Lambda^{\mathbf{n}}}\otimes\rho_{\Lambda,\mathbf{m}})\circ\alpha_{\mathsf{k}\Lambda^{\mathbf{n}},\mathsf{k}\Lambda^{\mathbf{m}},\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Lambda)}\circ(\phi_{\mathbf{n},\mathbf{m}}^{\Lambda}\otimes id_{\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Lambda)})
and
\rho_{\Lambda,\mathbf{n}+\mathbf{m}}
agree on each simple tensor
\lambda\otimes w
where
\lambda\in\mathsf{k}\Lambda^{\mathbf{n}+\mathbf{m}}
and
w\in\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Lambda)
. But this is evident since
