\displaystyle\left(\rho_{\Lambda,\mathbf{n}}\circ(id_{\mathsf{k}\Lambda^{\mathbf{n}}}\otimes\rho_{\Lambda,\mathbf{m}})\circ\alpha_{\mathsf{k}\Lambda^{\mathbf{n}},\mathsf{k}\Lambda^{\mathbf{m}},\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Lambda)}\circ(\phi_{\mathbf{n},\mathbf{m}}^{\Lambda}\otimes id_{\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Lambda)})\right)(\lambda\otimes w)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(\rho_{\Lambda,\mathbf{n}}\circ(id_{\mathsf{k}\Lambda^{\mathbf{n}}}\otimes\rho_{\Lambda,\mathbf{m}})\circ\alpha_{\mathsf{k}\Lambda^{\mathbf{n}},\mathsf{k}\Lambda^{\mathbf{m}},\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Lambda)}\right)((\lambda_{1}\otimes\lambda_{2})\otimes w)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(\rho_{\Lambda,\mathbf{n}}\circ(id_{\mathsf{k}\Lambda^{\mathbf{n}}}\otimes\rho_{\Lambda,\mathbf{m}})\right)(\lambda_{1}\otimes(\lambda_{2}\otimes w))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\rho_{\Lambda,\mathbf{n}}(\lambda_{1}\otimes s_{\lambda_{2}}w)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle s_{\lambda_{1}}(s_{\lambda_{2}}w)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle s_{\lambda}w

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\rho_{\Lambda,\mathbf{n}+\mathbf{m}}(\lambda\otimes w).

Now we prove that
\rho_{\Lambda,\mathbf{n}}
is injective by induction on
\ell(\mathbf{n}):=n_{1}+n_{2}+...+n_{k}
. The case
\ell(\mathbf{n})=0
is trivial since it is possible only if
\mathbf{n}=0\in\mathbb{N}^{k}
and in this case
\rho_{\Lambda,0}
is the canonical isomorphism
\mathsf{k}\Lambda^{0}\otimes_{\mathsf{k}\Lambda^{0}}\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Lambda)\longrightarrow\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Lambda)
given by multiplication, and hence injective. So let us consider the case
\ell(\mathbf{n})=1
. Then
\mathbf{n}=\mathbf{e}_{i}
for some
i=1,2,...,k
. Suppose
x=\displaystyle{\sum_{j=1}^{t}}x_{j}\otimes w_{j}\in\mathsf{k}\Lambda^{\mathbf{e}_{i}}\otimes_{\mathsf{k}\Lambda^{0}}\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Lambda)
is such that
\rho_{\Lambda,\mathbf{e}_{i}}(x)=0
. For each
j=1,2,...,t
write
x_{j}=\displaystyle{\sum_{\lambda\in\Lambda^{\mathbf{e}_{i}}}}c_{j,\lambda}\lambda
. Then

\displaystyle 0=\displaystyle{\sum_{\alpha\in\Lambda^{\mathbf{e}_{i}}}}\alpha\otimes s_{\alpha^{*}}\left(\displaystyle{\sum_{j=1}^{t}}\pi(x_{j})w_{j}\right)

\displaystyle=\displaystyle{\sum_{\alpha\in\Lambda^{\mathbf{e}_{i}}}}\alpha\otimes\left(\displaystyle{\sum_{j=1}^{t}}(s_{\alpha^{*}}\pi(x_{j}))w_{j}\right)

\displaystyle=\displaystyle{\sum_{\alpha\in\Lambda^{\mathbf{e}_{i}}}}\displaystyle{\sum_{j=1}^{t}}\alpha\pi^{-1}(s_{\alpha^{*}}\pi(x_{j}))\otimes w_{j}

\displaystyle=\displaystyle{\sum_{\alpha\in\Lambda^{\mathbf{e}_{i}}}}\displaystyle{\sum_{j=1}^{t}}\alpha\pi^{-1}\left(s_{\alpha^{*}}\displaystyle{\sum_{\lambda\in\Lambda^{\mathbf{e}_{i}}}}c_{j,\lambda}s_{\lambda}\right)\otimes w_{j}

\displaystyle=\displaystyle{\sum_{\alpha\in\Lambda^{\mathbf{e}_{i}}}}\displaystyle{\sum_{j=1}^{t}}\alpha\pi^{-1}\left(\displaystyle{\sum_{\lambda\in\Lambda^{\mathbf{e}_{i}}}}c_{j,\lambda}s_{\alpha^{*}}s_{\lambda}\right)\otimes w_{j}

\displaystyle=\displaystyle{\sum_{\alpha\in\Lambda^{\mathbf{e}_{i}}}}\displaystyle{\sum_{j=1}^{t}}\alpha\pi^{-1}(c_{j,\alpha}p_{s(\alpha)})\otimes w_{j}=\displaystyle{\sum_{j=1}^{t}}\left(\displaystyle{\sum_{\alpha\in\Lambda^{\mathbf{e}_{i}}}}c_{j,\alpha}\alpha\right)\otimes w_{j}=\displaystyle{\sum_{j=1}^{t}}x_{j}\otimes w_{j}=x.

Therefore,
\rho_{\Lambda,\mathbf{e}_{i}}
is injective. Now take
\ell\geq 1
and assume that
\rho_{\Lambda,\mathbf{n}}
is injective for all
\mathbf{n}\in\mathbb{N}^{k}
with
0\leq\ell(\mathbf{n})\leq\ell
. Let
n\in\mathbb{N}^{k}
be such that
\ell(\mathbf{n})=\ell+1
. Writing
\mathbf{n}=\mathbf{m}+\mathbf{p}
for some
0<\mathbf{m},\mathbf{p}\in\mathbb{N}^{k}
, we have

\rho_{\Lambda,\mathbf{m}}\circ(id_{\mathsf{k}\Lambda^{\mathbf{m}}}\otimes\rho_{\Lambda,\mathbf{p}})\circ\alpha_{\mathsf{k}\Lambda^{\mathbf{m}},\mathsf{k}\Lambda^{\mathbf{p}},\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Lambda)}\circ(\phi_{\mathbf{m},\mathbf{p}}^{\Lambda}\otimes id_{\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Lambda)})=\rho_{\Lambda,\mathbf{n}},

by commutativity of (
9
). Now by induction hypothesis both
\rho_{\Lambda,\mathbf{m}}
and
\rho_{\Lambda,\mathbf{p}}
are injective and so is
\rho_{\Lambda,\mathbf{n}}
. This completes the proof.
∎

6.2.
Bridging matrix and the formation of bridging bimodule

If
E=(U,V,E^{1},r,s)
is a polymorphism, then the
adjacency matrix
of
E
is defined to be a matrix
A_{E}\in\mathbb{M}_{U\times V}(\mathbb{N})
such that

A_{E}(u,v):=|\{e\in E^{1}~|~s(e)=v~\text{and}~r(e)=u\}|.

Note that our adjacency matrix
A_{E}
is the transpose of the adjacency matrix defined in
[
2
, Definition 3.2]
. This is simply because the edges in our polymorphism are directed from
V
to
U
.

Let
\Lambda,\Omega
be row-finite
k
-graphs without sources such that
|\Lambda^{0}|,|\Omega^{0}|<\infty
. Given a matrix
R\in\mathbb{M}_{\Lambda^{0}\times\Omega^{0}}(\mathbb{N})
, we can associate with it, a polymorphism
E_{R}\equiv(\Lambda^{0},\Omega^{0},R^{1},r,s)
where

R^{1}:=\{g_{i}^{v,w}~|~v\in\Lambda^{0},w\in\Omega^{0},1\leq i\leq R(v,w)\},

and
r:R^{1}\longrightarrow\Lambda^{0}
,
s:R^{1}\longrightarrow\Omega^{0}
are respectively defined as

r(g_{i}^{v,w}):=v,~~~~~~~s(g_{i}^{v,w}):=w.

It is then easy to note that the adjacency matrix of the polymorphism
E_{R}
is
R
. Also
E_{R}
gives a
\mathsf{k}\Lambda^{0}-\mathsf{k}\Omega^{0}
-bimodule
\mathsf{k}R^{1}
with respect to the bi-action

vgw:=\delta_{v,r(g)}\delta_{w,s(g)}g

for all
v\in\Lambda^{0},w\in\Omega^{0}
and
g\in R^{1}
.

Definition 6.5
.

A matrix
R\in\mathbb{M}_{\Lambda^{0}\times\Omega^{0}}(\mathbb{N})
is called a
bridging matrix
(respectively,
E_{R}
, a
bridging polymorphism
) for the
k
-graphs
\Lambda
and
\Omega
if there is a family of isomorphisms (called
flips
)
\mathfrak{F}=(\mathfrak{f}_{i})_{1\leq i\leq k}
;

\mathfrak{f}_{i}:E_{\mathbf{e}_{i}}^{\Lambda}\circ E_{R}\longrightarrow E_{R}\circ E_{\mathbf{e}_{i}}^{\Omega},

such that the diagram

(10)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E_{\mathbf{e}_{i}}^{\Lambda}\circ E_{\mathbf{e}_{j}}^{\Lambda}\circ E_{R}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E_{\mathbf{e}_{i}}^{\Lambda}\circ E_{R}\circ E_{\mathbf{e}_{j}}^{\Omega}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E_{R}\circ E_{\mathbf{e}_{i}}^{\Omega}\circ E_{\mathbf{e}_{j}}^{\Omega}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E_{\mathbf{e}_{j}}^{\Lambda}\circ E_{\mathbf{e}_{i}}^{\Lambda}\circ E_{R}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E_{\mathbf{e}_{j}}^{\Lambda}\circ E_{R}\circ E_{\mathbf{e}_{i}}^{\Omega}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E_{R}\circ E_{\mathbf{e}_{j}}^{\Omega}\circ E_{\mathbf{e}_{i}}^{\Omega}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces id_{E_{\mathbf{e}_{i}}^{\Lambda}}\times\mathfrak{f}_{j}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{\kappa}_{\Lambda,i,j}\times id_{E_{R}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{f}_{i}\times id_{E_{\mathbf{e}_{j}}^{\Omega}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces id_{E_{R}}\times\tilde{\kappa}_{\Omega,i,j}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces id_{E_{\mathbf{e}_{j}}^{\Lambda}}\times\mathfrak{f}_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{f}_{j}\times id_{E_{\mathbf{e}_{i}}^{\Omega}}}

commutes for all
1\leq i<j\leq k
. We call
(R,\mathfrak{F})
, a
specified
\Lambda-\Omega
bridging pair
.

Remarks 6.6
.

(i)
Since composition of polymorphisms is associative, we ignore the unnecessary parenthesis in (
10
). In this way we avoid the extra layers of associativity isomorphisms. The following figure explains the
bridging
criterion in the case
k=2
.

v
w
\Lambda
\Omega

The two blue-red commutative squares are parts of the
1
-skeletons of the
k
-graphs; the left one is on the level of
\Lambda
and the right one is on the level of
\Omega
. The edges of the polymorphism
E_{R}
, shown as dotted black lines, connect vertices of
\Omega
to vertices of
\Lambda
in such a way that the remaining squares are commutative (due to the flips) and all the tricoloured path from
w\in\Omega^{0}
to
v\in\Lambda^{0}
are same.

(ii)
We remark that the commutativity of the above diagram has close similarity with the necessary and sufficient requirement (see
[
25
, Remark 2.3]
and
[
26
, Theorem 4.4]
) for a given set of
k
directed graphs on a common vertex set to be the coordinate graphs of a
k
-graph.

To be more precise, suppose we have two
k
-graphs
\Lambda,\Omega
and a matrix
R\in\mathbb{M}_{\Lambda^{0}\times\Omega^{0}}(\mathbb{N})
along with a family of isomorphisms
(\mathfrak{f}_{i}:E_{\mathbf{e}_{i}}^{\Lambda}\circ E_{R}\longrightarrow E_{R}\circ E_{\mathbf{e}_{i}}^{\Omega})_{1\leq i\leq k}
. Form a directed graph
G
where
G^{0}:=\Lambda^{0}\sqcup\Omega^{0}
and
G^{1}:=\Lambda^{\mathbf{1}}\sqcup\Omega^{\mathbf{1}}\sqcup R^{1}
. The range and source maps are defined by extending naturally, the individual range and source maps of
\Lambda,\Omega
and
E_{R}
. Now we colour the edges of
G
with
k+1
colours using the map
d:G^{1}\longrightarrow\mathbb{N}^{k+1}
defined by

d(\alpha):=\left\{\begin{array}[]{lll}\iota(d_{\Lambda}(\alpha))&\mbox{if }\alpha\in\Lambda^{\mathbf{1}}\\
\iota(d_{\Omega}(\alpha))&\mbox{if }\alpha\in\Omega^{\mathbf{1}}\\
\mathbf{e}_{k+1}&\mbox{if }\alpha\in R^{1}\end{array}\right.

where
\iota:\mathbb{N}^{k}\longrightarrow\mathbb{N}^{k+1}
is the inclusion sending
(n_{1},n_{2},\ldots,n_{k})
to
(n_{1},n_{2},\ldots,n_{k},0)
. One can naturally extend
d
to colour all the paths in
G
(e.g., if
k=2
and
\alpha g\beta
is a path with
\alpha\in\Lambda^{\mathbf{1}}
,
d_{\Lambda}(\alpha)=(1,0)
,
g\in R_{1}
and
\beta\in\Omega^{\mathbf{1}}
,
d_{\Omega}(\beta)=(0,1)
then
d(\alpha g\beta)=(1,0,0)+(0,0,1)+(0,1,0)=(1,1,1)
). Thus we have a
(k+1)
-coloured directed graph
G
with
G_{i}=E_{\mathbf{e}_{i}}^{\Lambda}\sqcup E_{\mathbf{e}_{i}}^{\Omega}
for
i=1,2,...,k
and
G_{k+1}=E_{R}
. If
i,j\neq k+1
, we define isomorphisms
G_{i}\circ G_{j}\longrightarrow G_{j}\circ G_{i}
by using the factorization rules of
\Lambda
or
\Omega
, whichever is suitable. For each
i\neq k+1
, the isomorphism
G_{i}\circ G_{k+1}\longrightarrow G_{k+1}\circ G_{i}
is given by the flip
\mathfrak{f}_{i}
. Now the readers can easily verify (in view of
[
25
, Remark 2.3]
) that the set of paths of
G
together with the map
d
and the said factorization rules, forms a well-defined
(k+1)
-graph if and only if the diagram
(\ref{comd3})
commutes.

(iii)
Given a specified
\Lambda-\Omega
bridging pair
(R,\mathfrak{F})
, we can naturally form a
\Lambda-\Omega

morph

[
36
, Definition 3.1]
. Let
g\in R^{1}
and
\omega\in\Omega
. If
\omega\notin\Omega^{0}
then we can write
\omega=\omega_{1}\omega_{2}\cdots\omega_{t}
where
\omega_{i}\in\Omega^{\mathbf{1}}
for all
i=1,2,...,t
and
d(\omega_{1})\leq d(\omega_{2})\leq\cdots\leq d(\omega_{t})
. Let
d(\omega_{i})=e_{d_{i}}
,
\mathfrak{f}_{d_{1}}^{-1}((g,\omega_{1}))=(\lambda_{1},g_{1})
and
\mathfrak{f}_{d_{i+1}}^{-1}((g_{i},\omega_{i+1})=(\lambda_{i+1},g_{i+1})
for all
i=1,2,...,t-1
. Let
\lambda=\lambda_{1}\lambda_{2}\cdots\lambda_{t}
. Define a map
\phi:R^{1}*_{\Omega^{0}}\Omega\longrightarrow\Lambda*_{\Lambda^{0}}R^{1}
by

\phi((g,\omega)):=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}(\lambda,g_{t})&\mbox{if }\omega\notin\Omega^{0}\\
(r(g),g)&\mbox{if }\omega\in\Omega^{0}.\end{array}\right.

The commutativity of diagram
(\ref{comd3})
then guarantees that
\phi
is a well-defined bijection and subsequently
(R^{1},r,s,\phi)
is a
\Lambda-\Omega
morph.

A necessary condition for
R
to be a bridging matrix for
\Lambda
and
\Omega
is that
A_{\mathbf{e}_{i}}R=RB_{\mathbf{e}_{i}}
for all
i=1,2,...,k
. This is also sufficient for
k=1
(diagram (
10
) vacuously commutes in this case) since to form a
2
-graph (see Remark
6.6

(ii)
) from a given
2
-coloured directed graph it is enough to specify the factorization rules for bicoloured paths of degree
(1,1)
and that can be done by any isomorphism
\mathfrak{f}:E_{1}^{\Lambda}\circ E_{R}\longrightarrow E_{R}\circ E_{1}^{\Omega}
(see
[
34
, §6]
). However, for
k\geq 2
, only commutativity on the level of matrices is not sufficient. We give some examples.

Example 6.7
.

Suppose
\Lambda
is a
2
-graph with following
1
-skeleton:

.
u
\beta_{1},\beta_{2}
\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}

and
\Omega
is a
2
-graph with the following
1
-skeleton:

w
v
f_{1},f_{2}
,
~~e_{3},e_{4}
e_{1},e_{2}
,
~~f_{3},f_{4}

The factorization rules for
\Lambda
is given by
\alpha_{i}\beta_{j}=\beta_{j}\alpha_{i}
for all
1\leq i,j\leq 2
. We fix the factorization rules for
\Omega
as follows:

\displaystyle v-v~

\displaystyle\text{bicoloured paths}\hskip 56.9055ptw-w~\text{bicoloured paths}

\displaystyle f_{1}e_{1}=e_{3}f_{3}\hskip 119.50148ptf_{3}e_{3}=e_{2}f_{2}

\displaystyle f_{1}e_{2}=e_{4}f_{3}\hskip 119.50148ptf_{3}e_{4}=e_{2}f_{1}

\displaystyle f_{2}e_{1}=e_{3}f_{4}\hskip 119.50148ptf_{4}e_{3}=e_{1}f_{2}

\displaystyle f_{2}e_{2}=e_{4}f_{4}\hskip 119.50148ptf_{4}e_{4}=e_{1}f_{1}

The matrices for
\Lambda
are:
A_{(1,0)}=A_{(0,1)}=(2)
and the matrices for
\Omega
are:

B_{(1,0)}=B_{(0,1)}=\begin{pmatrix}0&2\\
2&0\end{pmatrix}.

Take
R=\begin{pmatrix}1&1\end{pmatrix}
. Then
A_{(1,0)}R=RB_{(1,0)}
and
A_{(0,1)}R=RB_{(0,1)}
. The following figure shows the edges of the polymorphism

connecting the vertices of

and

.

For each

, there are four possible choices for an isomorphism
\mathfrak{f}_{i}:E_{\mathbf{e}_{i}}^{\Lambda}\circ E_{R}\longrightarrow E_{R}\circ E_{\mathbf{e}_{i}}^{\Omega}
. Thus, in total, we have
16
different families of flips. A tedious verification confirms that no such family makes the diagram
(\ref{comd3})
commute, for instance if we choose

\displaystyle\mathfrak{f}_{1}:E_{(1,0)}^{\Lambda}\circ E_{R}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow E_{R}\circ E_{(1,0)}^{\Omega}

\displaystyle(\alpha_{1},g_{1})

\displaystyle\longmapsto(g_{2},f_{2})

\displaystyle(\alpha_{1},g_{2})

\displaystyle\longmapsto(g_{1},f_{3})

\displaystyle(\alpha_{2},g_{1})

\displaystyle\longmapsto(g_{2},f_{1})

\displaystyle(\alpha_{2},g_{2})

and

\displaystyle\mathfrak{f}_{2}:E_{(0,1)}^{\Lambda}\circ E_{R}

\displaystyle(\beta_{1},g_{1})

then

by taking the top-right path in
(\ref{comd3})
; whereas

by taking the left-bottom path.

Example 6.8
.

1
-skeletons of two

.

In
\Lambda
suppose

for

. For

, suppose the factorization rules are:
\alpha_{i}\gamma_{1}=\gamma_{2}\alpha_{i}
for

.

The existence of a bridging matrix has the following consequence in terms of bimodules of polymorphisms.

Lemma 6.9
.

Let
\Lambda,\Omega
be row-finite
k
-graphs without sources such that
|\Lambda^{0}|,|\Omega^{0}|<\infty
. Suppose
(R,\mathfrak{F}=(\mathfrak{f}_{i})_{1\leq i\leq k})
is a specified
\Lambda-\Omega
bridging pair. Then we have
\mathsf{k}\Lambda^{0}-\mathsf{k}\Omega^{0}
-bimodule isomorphisms

\sigma_{\mathbf{n}}:\mathsf{k}\Lambda^{\mathbf{n}}\otimes_{\mathsf{k}\Lambda^{0}}\mathsf{k}R^{1}\longrightarrow\mathsf{k}R^{1}\otimes_{\mathsf{k}\Omega^{0}}\mathsf{k}\Omega^{\mathbf{n}},

for all
\mathbf{n}\in\mathbb{N}^{k}
such that the diagram

(11)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{k}\Lambda^{\mathbf{n}+\mathbf{m}}\otimes_{\mathsf{k}\Lambda^{0}}\mathsf{k}R^{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{k}R^{1}\otimes_{\mathsf{k}\Omega^{0}}\mathsf{k}\Omega^{\mathbf{n}+\mathbf{m}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathsf{k}\Lambda^{\mathbf{n}}\otimes_{\mathsf{k}\Lambda^{0}}\mathsf{k}\Lambda^{\mathbf{m}})\otimes_{\mathsf{k}\Lambda^{0}}\mathsf{k}R^{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{k}R^{1}\otimes_{\mathsf{k}\Omega^{0}}(\mathsf{k}\Omega^{\mathbf{n}}\otimes_{\mathsf{k}\Omega^{0}}\mathsf{k}\Omega^{\mathbf{m}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{k}\Lambda^{\mathbf{n}}\otimes_{\mathsf{k}\Lambda^{0}}(\mathsf{k}\Lambda^{\mathbf{m}}\otimes_{\mathsf{k}\Lambda^{0}}\mathsf{k}R^{1})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathsf{k}R^{1}\otimes_{\mathsf{k}\Omega^{0}}\mathsf{k}\Omega^{\mathbf{n}})\otimes_{\mathsf{k}\Omega^{0}}\mathsf{k}\Omega^{\mathbf{m}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{k}\Lambda^{\mathbf{n}}\otimes_{\mathsf{k}\Lambda^{0}}(\mathsf{k}R^{1}\otimes_{\mathsf{k}\Omega^{0}}\mathsf{k}\Omega^{\mathbf{m}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathsf{k}\Lambda^{\mathbf{n}}\otimes_{\mathsf{k}\Lambda^{0}}\mathsf{k}R^{1})\otimes_{\mathsf{k}\Omega^{0}}\mathsf{k}\Omega^{\mathbf{m}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma_{\mathbf{n}+\mathbf{m}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi_{\mathbf{n},\mathbf{m}}^{\Lambda}\otimes id_{\mathsf{k}R^{1}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces id_{\mathsf{k}R^{1}}\otimes\phi_{\mathbf{n},\mathbf{m}}^{\Omega}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{{}_{\mathsf{k}\Lambda^{\mathbf{n}},\mathsf{k}\Lambda^{\mathbf{m}},\mathsf{k}R^{1}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{{}_{\mathsf{k}R^{1},\mathsf{k}\Omega^{\mathbf{n}},\mathsf{k}\Omega^{\mathbf{m}}}}^{-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces id_{\mathsf{k}\Lambda^{\mathbf{n}}}\otimes\sigma_{\mathbf{m}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma_{\mathbf{n}}^{-1}\otimes id_{\mathsf{k}\Omega^{\mathbf{m}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{{}_{\mathsf{k}\Lambda^{\mathbf{n}},\mathsf{k}R^{1},\mathsf{k}\Omega^{\mathbf{m}}}}^{-1}}

commutes for all
\mathbf{n},\mathbf{m}\in\mathbb{N}^{k}
.

Proof.

For any
i=1,2,...,k
, since
\mathsf{k}\Lambda^{\mathbf{e}_{i}}\otimes_{\mathsf{k}\Lambda^{0}}\mathsf{k}R^{1}\cong\mathsf{k}(E_{\mathbf{e}_{i}}^{\Lambda}\circ E_{R})^{1}
and
\mathsf{k}R^{1}\otimes_{\mathsf{k}\Omega^{0}}\mathsf{k}\Omega^{\mathbf{e}_{i}}\cong\mathsf{k}(E_{R}\circ E_{\mathbf{e}_{i}}^{\Omega})^{1}
, the flip

\mathfrak{f}_{i}:E_{\mathbf{e}_{i}}^{\Lambda}\circ E_{R}\longrightarrow E_{R}\circ E_{\mathbf{e}_{i}}^{\Omega}

induces isomorphism

\tilde{\mathfrak{f}}_{i}:\mathsf{k}\Lambda^{\mathbf{e}_{i}}\otimes_{\mathsf{k}\Lambda^{0}}\mathsf{k}R^{1}\longrightarrow\mathsf{k}R^{1}\otimes_{\mathsf{k}\Omega^{0}}\mathsf{k}\Omega^{\mathbf{e}_{i}}.

Let
\sigma_{\mathbf{e}_{i}}:=\tilde{\mathfrak{f}}_{i}
for all
i=1,2,...,k
. Suppose we have defined
\sigma_{\mathbf{m}}
for some
m\in\mathbb{N}^{k}
. Then we define

\sigma_{\mathbf{m}+\mathbf{e}_{i}}:\mathsf{k}\Lambda^{\mathbf{m}+\mathbf{e}_{i}}\otimes_{\mathsf{k}\Lambda^{0}}\mathsf{k}R^{1}\longrightarrow\mathsf{k}R^{1}\otimes_{\mathsf{k}\Omega^{0}}\mathsf{k}\Omega^{\mathbf{m}+\mathbf{e}_{i}}

as

\sigma_{\mathbf{m}+\mathbf{e}_{i}}:=\left(id_{\mathsf{k}R^{1}}\otimes(\phi_{\mathbf{m},\mathbf{e}_{i}}^{\Omega})^{-1}\right)\circ\alpha_{{}_{\mathsf{k}R^{1},\mathsf{k}\Omega^{\mathbf{m}},\mathsf{k}\Omega^{\mathbf{e}_{i}}}}\circ\left(\sigma_{\mathbf{m}}\otimes id_{\mathsf{k}\Omega^{\mathbf{e}_{i}}}\right)\circ\alpha_{{}_{\mathsf{k}\Lambda^{\mathbf{m}},\mathsf{k}R^{1},\mathsf{k}\Omega^{\mathbf{e}_{i}}}}^{-1}\circ\left(id_{\mathsf{k}\Lambda^{\mathbf{m}}}\otimes\tilde{\mathfrak{f}}_{i}\right)\circ\alpha_{{}_{\mathsf{k}\Lambda^{\mathbf{m}},\mathsf{k}\Lambda^{\mathbf{e}_{i}},\mathsf{k}R^{1}}}\circ\left(\phi_{\mathbf{m},\mathbf{e}_{i}}^{\Lambda}\otimes id_{\mathsf{k}R^{1}}\right).

The commutativity of (
10
) guarantees that if
\sigma_{\mathbf{m}},\sigma_{\mathbf{m}^{\prime}}
are already defined and
\mathbf{m}+\mathbf{e}_{i}=\mathbf{m}^{\prime}+\mathbf{e}_{j}
, for
i\neq j
then
\sigma_{\mathbf{m}+\mathbf{e}_{i}}=\sigma_{\mathbf{m}^{\prime}+\mathbf{e}_{j}}
. Therefore, we can define
\sigma_{\mathbf{n}}
for each
\mathbf{n}\in\mathbb{N}^{k}
without bothering about which basic
k
-tuple to choose first in the factorization
\mathbf{n}=n_{1}\mathbf{e}_{1}+n_{2}\mathbf{e}_{2}+\dots+n_{k}\mathbf{e}_{k}
.

Now we prove the commutativity of (
11
). Fix any
\mathbf{n}\in\mathbb{N}^{k}
. We claim that (
11
) commutes for every
\mathbf{m}\in\mathbb{N}^{k}
. For this, we proceed by induction on
\ell(\mathbf{m})=m_{1}+m_{2}+\dots+m_{k}
. The case
\ell(\mathbf{m})=1
is already covered in view of our inductive definition of the maps
\sigma_{p}
’s. Assume that (
11
) commutes for all
\mathbf{m}\in\mathbb{N}^{k}
with
\ell(\mathbf{m})=N(\geq 1)
and choose any
\mathbf{p}\in\mathbb{N}^{k}
with
\ell(\mathbf{p})=N+1
. Then
\mathbf{p}=\mathbf{m}+\mathbf{e}_{i}
for some
i\in\{1,2,...,k\}
. Now

\displaystyle\left(id_{\mathsf{k}R^{1}}\otimes(\phi_{\mathbf{n},\mathbf{p}}^{\Omega})^{-1}\right)\circ\alpha_{{}_{\mathsf{k}R^{1},\mathsf{k}\Omega^{\mathbf{n}},\mathsf{k}\Omega^{\mathbf{p}}}}\circ\left(\sigma_{\mathbf{n}}\otimes id_{\mathsf{k}\Omega^{\mathbf{p}}}\right)\circ\alpha_{{}_{\mathsf{k}\Lambda^{\mathbf{n}},\mathsf{k}R^{1},\mathsf{k}\Omega^{\mathbf{p}}}}^{-1}\circ\left(id_{\mathsf{k}\Lambda^{\mathbf{n}}}\otimes\sigma_{\mathbf{p}}\right)\circ\alpha_{{}_{\mathsf{k}\Lambda^{\mathbf{n}},\mathsf{k}\Lambda^{\mathbf{p}},\mathsf{k}R^{1}}}\circ\left(\phi_{\mathbf{n},\mathbf{p}}^{\Lambda}\otimes id_{\mathsf{k}R^{1}}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(id_{\mathsf{k}R^{1}}\otimes(\phi_{\mathbf{n}+\mathbf{m},\mathbf{e}_{i}}^{\Omega})^{-1}\right)\circ\left(id_{\mathsf{k}R^{1}}\otimes(\phi_{\mathbf{n},\mathbf{m}}^{\Omega})^{-1}\otimes id_{\mathsf{k}\Omega^{\mathbf{e}_{i}}}\right)\circ\left(id_{\mathsf{k}R^{1}}\otimes\alpha_{{}_{\mathsf{k}\Omega^{\mathbf{n}},\mathsf{k}\Omega^{\mathbf{m}},\mathsf{k}\Omega^{\mathbf{e}_{i}}}}^{-1}\right)\circ\left(id_{\mathsf{k}R^{1}}\otimes id_{\mathsf{k}\Omega^{\mathbf{n}}}\otimes\phi_{\mathbf{m},\mathbf{e}_{i}}^{\Omega}\right)\circ

\displaystyle\alpha_{{}_{\mathsf{k}R^{1},\mathsf{k}\Omega^{\mathbf{n}},\mathsf{k}\Omega^{\mathbf{p}}}}\circ\left(\sigma_{\mathbf{n}}\otimes id_{\mathsf{k}\Omega^{\mathbf{p}}}\right)\circ\alpha_{{}_{\mathsf{k}\Lambda^{\mathbf{n}},\mathsf{k}R^{1},\mathsf{k}\Omega^{\mathbf{p}}}}^{-1}\circ\left(id_{\mathsf{k}\Lambda^{\mathbf{n}}}\otimes\sigma_{\mathbf{p}}\right)\circ\alpha_{{}_{\mathsf{k}\Lambda^{\mathbf{n}},\mathsf{k}\Lambda^{\mathbf{p}},\mathsf{k}R^{1}}}\circ\left(\phi_{\mathbf{n},\mathbf{p}}^{\Lambda}\otimes id_{\mathsf{k}R^{1}}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(id_{\mathsf{k}R^{1}}\otimes(\phi_{\mathbf{n}+\mathbf{m},\mathbf{e}_{i}}^{\Omega})^{-1}\right)\circ\left(id_{\mathsf{k}R^{1}}\otimes(\phi_{\mathbf{n},\mathbf{m}}^{\Omega})^{-1}\otimes id_{\mathsf{k}\Omega^{\mathbf{e}_{i}}}\right)\circ\left(id_{\mathsf{k}R^{1}}\otimes\alpha_{{}_{\mathsf{k}\Omega^{\mathbf{n}},\mathsf{k}\Omega^{\mathbf{m}},\mathsf{k}\Omega^{\mathbf{e}_{i}}}}^{-1}\right)\circ\left(id_{\mathsf{k}R^{1}}\otimes id_{\mathsf{k}\Omega^{\mathbf{n}}}\otimes\phi_{\mathbf{m},\mathbf{e}_{i}}^{\Omega}\right)\circ

\displaystyle\alpha_{{}_{\mathsf{k}R^{1},\mathsf{k}\Omega^{\mathbf{n}},\mathsf{k}\Omega^{\mathbf{p}}}}\circ\left(\sigma_{\mathbf{n}}\otimes id_{\mathsf{k}\Omega^{\mathbf{p}}}\right)\circ\alpha_{{}_{\mathsf{k}\Lambda^{\mathbf{n}},\mathsf{k}R^{1},\mathsf{k}\Omega^{\mathbf{p}}}}^{-1}\circ\left(id_{\mathsf{k}\Lambda^{\mathbf{n}}}\otimes\sigma_{\mathbf{p}}\right)\circ\alpha_{{}_{\mathsf{k}\Lambda^{\mathbf{n}},\mathsf{k}\Lambda^{\mathbf{p}},\mathsf{k}R^{1}}}\circ\left(id_{\mathsf{k}\Lambda^{\mathbf{n}}}\otimes(\phi_{\mathbf{m},\mathbf{e}_{i}}^{\Lambda})^{-1}\otimes id_{\mathsf{k}R^{1}}\right)\circ

\displaystyle\left(\alpha_{{}_{\mathsf{k}\Lambda^{\mathbf{n}},\mathsf{k}\Lambda^{\mathbf{m}},\mathsf{k}\Lambda^{\mathbf{e}_{i}}}}\otimes id_{\mathsf{k}R^{1}}\right)\circ\left(\phi_{\mathbf{n},\mathbf{m}}^{\Lambda}\otimes id_{\mathsf{k}\Lambda^{\mathbf{e}_{i}}}\otimes id_{\mathsf{k}R^{1}}\right)\circ\left(\phi_{\mathbf{n}+\mathbf{m},\mathbf{e}_{i}}^{\Lambda}\otimes id_{\mathsf{k}R^{1}}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(id_{\mathsf{k}R^{1}}\otimes(\phi_{\mathbf{n}+\mathbf{m},\mathbf{e}_{i}}^{\Omega})^{-1}\right)\circ\alpha_{{}_{\mathsf{k}R^{1},\mathsf{k}\Omega^{\mathbf{n}+\mathbf{m}},\mathsf{k}\Omega^{\mathbf{e}_{i}}}}\circ\overline{\left(id_{\mathsf{k}R^{1}}\otimes(\phi_{\mathbf{n},\mathbf{m}}^{\Omega})^{-1}\otimes id_{\mathsf{k}\Omega^{\mathbf{e}_{i}}}\right)\circ\left(\alpha_{{}_{\mathsf{k}R^{1},\mathsf{k}\Omega^{\mathbf{n}},\mathsf{k}\Omega^{\mathbf{m}}}}\otimes id_{\mathsf{k}\Omega^{\mathbf{e}_{i}}}\right)\circ}

\displaystyle\overline{\left(\sigma_{\mathbf{n}}\otimes id_{\mathsf{k}\Omega^{\mathbf{m}}}\otimes id_{\mathsf{k}\Omega^{\mathbf{e}_{i}}}\right)\circ\left(\alpha_{{}_{\mathsf{k}\Lambda^{\mathbf{n}},\mathsf{k}R^{1},\mathsf{k}\Omega^{\mathbf{m}}}}^{-1}\otimes id_{\mathsf{k}\Omega^{\mathbf{e}_{i}}}\right)\circ\left(id_{\mathsf{k}\Lambda^{\mathbf{n}}}\otimes\sigma_{\mathbf{m}}\otimes id_{\mathsf{k}\Omega^{\mathbf{e}_{i}}}\right)\circ\left(\alpha_{{}_{\mathsf{k}\Lambda^{\mathbf{n}},\mathsf{k}\Lambda^{\mathbf{m}},\mathsf{k}R^{1}}}\otimes id_{\mathsf{k}\Omega^{\mathbf{e}_{i}}}\right)\circ}

\displaystyle\overline{\left(\phi_{\mathbf{n},\mathbf{m}}^{\Lambda}\otimes id_{\mathsf{k}R^{1}}\otimes id_{\mathsf{k}\Omega^{\mathbf{e}_{i}}}\right)}\circ\alpha_{{}_{\mathsf{k}\Lambda^{\mathbf{n}+\mathbf{m}},\mathsf{k}R^{1},\mathsf{k}\Omega^{\mathbf{e}_{i}}}}^{-1}\circ\left(id_{\mathsf{k}\Lambda^{\mathbf{n}+\mathbf{m}}}\otimes\tilde{\mathfrak{f}}_{i}\right)\circ\alpha_{{}_{\mathsf{k}\Lambda^{\mathbf{n}+\mathbf{m}},\mathsf{k}\Lambda^{\mathbf{e}_{i}},\mathsf{k}R^{1}}}\circ\left(\phi_{\mathbf{n}+\mathbf{m},\mathbf{e}_{i}}^{\Lambda}\otimes id_{\mathsf{k}R^{1}}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(id_{\mathsf{k}R^{1}}\otimes(\phi_{\mathbf{n}+\mathbf{m},\mathbf{e}_{i}}^{\Omega})^{-1}\right)\circ\alpha_{{}_{\mathsf{k}R^{1},\mathsf{k}\Omega^{\mathbf{n}+\mathbf{m}},\mathsf{k}\Omega^{\mathbf{e}_{i}}}}\circ{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\left(\sigma_{\mathbf{n}+\mathbf{m}}\otimes id_{\mathsf{k}\Omega^{\mathbf{e}_{i}}}\right)}\circ\alpha_{{}_{\mathsf{k}\Lambda^{\mathbf{n}+\mathbf{m}},\mathsf{k}R^{1},\mathsf{k}\Omega^{\mathbf{e}_{i}}}}^{-1}\circ\left(id_{\mathsf{k}\Lambda^{\mathbf{n}+\mathbf{m}}}\otimes\tilde{\mathfrak{f}}_{i}\right)\circ\alpha_{{}_{\mathsf{k}\Lambda^{\mathbf{n}+\mathbf{m}},\mathsf{k}\Lambda^{\mathbf{e}_{i}},\mathsf{k}R^{1}}}\circ

\displaystyle\left(\phi_{\mathbf{n}+\mathbf{m},\mathbf{e}_{i}}^{\Lambda}\otimes id_{\mathsf{k}R^{1}}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sigma_{\mathbf{n}+\mathbf{m}+\mathbf{e}_{i}}=\sigma_{\mathbf{n}+\mathbf{p}}.

We use the induction hypothesis on the overlined composition and replace it in the next step by the map marked in blue. All other replacements follow from associativity of tensor products. Hence, by induction, (
11
) commutes for all
\mathbf{m}\in\mathbb{N}^{k}
. Since
\mathbf{n}
was chosen arbitrarily, this completes the proof.
∎

Now we are in a position to proceed towards the construction of the bridging bimodule in our higher-rank setting.

We continue with the assumption that
\Lambda
and
\Omega
are row-finite
k
-graphs without sources and with finite object sets. Suppose we have a matrix
R\in\mathbb{M}_{\Lambda^{0}\times\Omega^{0}}(\mathbb{N})
such that
A_{\mathbf{e}_{i}}R=RB_{\mathbf{e}_{i}}
for all
i=1,2,...,k
. Note that the matrix of the product polymorphism
E_{\mathbf{e}_{i}}^{\Lambda}\circ E_{R}
is the product of the individual matrices, i.e.,
A_{\mathbf{e}_{i}}R
and similarly the matrix of
E_{R}\circ E_{\mathbf{e}_{i}}^{\Omega}
is
RB_{\mathbf{e}_{i}}
. Therefore, the polymorphisms
E_{\mathbf{e}_{i}}^{\Lambda}\circ E_{R}
and
E_{R}\circ E_{\mathbf{e}_{i}}^{\Omega}
are isomorphic for each
i=1,2,...,k
.

Let us choose isomorphisms

\mathfrak{f}_{i}:E_{\mathbf{e}_{i}}^{\Lambda}\circ E_{R}\longrightarrow E_{R}\circ E_{\mathbf{e}_{i}}^{\Omega},

such that (
10
) commutes, i.e., we choose a specific family of isomorphisms
\mathfrak{F}=(\mathfrak{f}_{i})_{i\leq i\leq k}
such that
(R,\mathfrak{F})
is a
\Lambda-\Omega
bridging pair.

We set

M(R):=\mathsf{k}R^{1}\otimes_{\mathsf{k}\Omega^{0}}\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Omega).

Then
M(R)
is automatically a
\mathsf{k}\Lambda^{0}-\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Omega)
-bimodule. We now impose a certain left
\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Lambda)
action on
M(R)
which will make it a
\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Lambda)-\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Omega)
-bimodule. We begin by fixing some notation.

•

For any
A-B
-bimodule
M
and
a\in A
,
\mu_{a}:M\longrightarrow M
is the
B
-module endomorphism defined as left multiplication by
a
.

•

For any
\lambda\in\Lambda
and any
\mathsf{k}\Lambda^{0}-\mathsf{k}\Omega^{0}
-bimodule
M
, define
\theta_{\lambda}:M\longrightarrow\mathsf{k}\Lambda^{d(\lambda)}\otimes_{\mathsf{k}\Lambda^{0}}M
to be the
\mathsf{k}\Lambda^{0}-\mathsf{k}\Omega^{0}
-bimodule homomorphisms such that

\theta_{\lambda}(m):=\lambda\otimes m,

for all
m\in M
.

•

For any
\mu\in\Lambda
and any
\mathsf{k}\Lambda^{0}-\mathsf{k}\Omega^{0}
-bimodule
M
, define
\theta_{\mu^{*}}:\mathsf{k}\Lambda^{d(\mu)}\otimes_{\mathsf{k}\Lambda^{0}}M\longrightarrow M
to be the
\mathsf{k}\Lambda^{0}-\mathsf{k}\Omega^{0}
-bimodule homomorphism such that

\theta_{\mu^{*}}(x\otimes m):=\left(\pi^{-1}(s_{\mu^{*}}\pi(x))\right)m,

for all
x\in\mathsf{k}\Lambda^{d(\mu)}
and
m\in M
.

Next, we construct a certain Kumjian-Pask
\Lambda
-family inside the
\mathsf{k}
-algebra
\operatorname{End}_{\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Omega)}(M(R))
. Define

P_{v}:=\mu_{v}\otimes id_{\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Omega)}
;

S_{\lambda}:=\left(id_{\mathsf{k}R^{1}}\otimes\rho_{\Omega,d(\lambda)}\right)\circ\alpha_{{}_{\mathsf{k}R^{1},\mathsf{k}\Omega^{d(\lambda)},\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Omega)}}\circ\left(\sigma_{d(\lambda)}\otimes id_{\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Omega)}\right)\circ\left(\theta_{\lambda}\otimes id_{\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Omega)}\right)
;

S_{\mu^{*}}:=\left(\theta_{\mu^{*}}\otimes id_{\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Omega)}\right)\circ\left(\sigma_{d(\mu)}^{-1}\otimes id_{\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Omega)}\right)\circ\alpha_{{}_{\mathsf{k}R^{1},\mathsf{k}\Omega^{d(\mu)},\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Omega)}}^{-1}\circ\left(id_{\mathsf{k}R^{1}}\otimes\rho_{\Omega,d(\mu)}^{-1}\right)
;

for all
v\in\Lambda^{0}
and
\lambda,\mu\in\Lambda
.

The relations
(KP1)
,
(KP3)
and
(KP4)
can be verified exactly in the same way as it was done for Relations
1,4
and
5
in the setting of Leavitt path algebras (see the proof of
[
2
, Theorem 4.11]
). So we do not repeat these here. Also, one can verify that

\displaystyle P_{r(\lambda)}S_{\lambda}=S_{\lambda}=S_{\lambda}P_{s(\lambda)}~\text{and}~P_{s(\lambda)}S_{\lambda^{*}}=S_{\lambda^{*}}=S_{\lambda^{*}}P_{s(\lambda)}

for all
\lambda\in\Lambda^{\neq 0}
. Below we only verify the remaining part of
(KP2)
i.e.,
S_{\lambda}S_{\mu}=S_{\lambda\mu}
and
S_{\mu^{*}}S_{\lambda^{*}}=S_{(\lambda\mu)^{*}}
for all
\lambda,\mu\in\Lambda^{\neq 0}
such that
s(\lambda)=r(\mu)
.

Let
\lambda,\mu\in\Lambda^{\neq 0}
be such that
s(\lambda)=r(\mu)
,
d(\lambda)=\mathbf{n}
and
d(\mu)=\mathbf{m}
. Then we have

\displaystyle S_{\lambda}S_{\mu}=

\displaystyle\left(\left(id_{\mathsf{k}R^{1}}\otimes\rho_{\Omega,\mathbf{n}}\right)\circ\alpha_{{}_{\mathsf{k}R^{1},\mathsf{k}\Omega^{\mathbf{n}},\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Omega)}}\circ\left(\sigma_{\mathbf{n}}\otimes id_{\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Omega)}\right)\circ\left(\theta_{\lambda}\otimes id_{\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Omega)}\right)\right)\circ

\displaystyle\left(\left(id_{\mathsf{k}R^{1}}\otimes\rho_{\Omega,\mathbf{m}}\right)\circ\alpha_{{}_{\mathsf{k}R^{1},\mathsf{k}\Omega^{\mathbf{m}},\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Omega)}}\circ\left(\sigma_{\mathbf{m}}\otimes id_{\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Omega)}\right)\circ\left(\theta_{\mu}\otimes id_{\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Omega)}\right)\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(id_{\mathsf{k}R^{1}}\otimes\rho_{\Omega,\mathbf{n}}\right)\circ\alpha_{{}_{\mathsf{k}R^{1},\mathsf{k}\Omega^{\mathbf{n}},\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Omega)}}\circ\left(\sigma_{\mathbf{n}}\theta_{\lambda}\otimes\rho_{\Omega,\mathbf{m}}\right)\circ\alpha_{{}_{\mathsf{k}R^{1},\mathsf{k}\Omega^{\mathbf{m}},\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Omega)}}\circ\left(\sigma_{\mathbf{m}}\otimes id_{\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Omega)}\right)\circ\left(\theta_{\mu}\otimes id_{\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Omega)}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(id_{\mathsf{k}R^{1}}\otimes\rho_{\Omega,\mathbf{n}}\right)\circ\alpha_{{}_{\mathsf{k}R^{1},\mathsf{k}\Omega^{\mathbf{n}},\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Omega)}}\circ\left((id_{\mathsf{k}R^{1}}\otimes id_{\mathsf{k}\Omega^{\mathbf{n}}})\otimes\rho_{\Omega,\mathbf{m}}\right)\circ\left(\sigma_{\mathbf{n}}\theta_{\lambda}\otimes(id_{\mathsf{k}\Omega^{\mathbf{m}}}\otimes id_{\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Omega)})\right)\circ

\displaystyle\alpha_{{}_{\mathsf{k}R^{1},\mathsf{k}\Omega^{\mathbf{m}},\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Omega)}}\circ\left(\sigma_{\mathbf{m}}\otimes id_{\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Omega)}\right)\circ\left(\theta_{\mu}\otimes id_{\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Omega)}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(id_{\mathsf{k}R^{1}}\otimes\rho_{\Omega,\mathbf{n}}\right)\circ\left(id_{\mathsf{k}R^{1}}\otimes(id_{\mathsf{k}\Omega^{\mathbf{n}}}\otimes\rho_{\Omega,\mathbf{m}})\right)\circ\alpha_{{}_{\mathsf{k}R^{1},\mathsf{k}\Omega^{\mathbf{n}},\mathsf{k}\Omega^{\mathbf{m}}\otimes\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Omega)}}\circ\alpha_{{}_{\mathsf{k}R^{1}\otimes\mathsf{k}\Omega^{\mathbf{n}},\mathsf{k}\Omega^{\mathbf{m}},\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Omega)}}\circ

\displaystyle\left((\sigma_{\mathbf{n}}\theta_{\lambda}\otimes(id_{\mathsf{k}\Omega^{\mathbf{m}}})\otimes id_{\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Omega)}\right)\circ\left(\sigma_{\mathbf{m}}\otimes id_{\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Omega)}\right)\circ\left(\theta_{\mu}\otimes id_{\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Omega)}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(id_{\mathsf{k}R^{1}}\otimes\rho_{\Omega,\mathbf{n}}\right)\circ\left(id_{\mathsf{k}R^{1}}\otimes(id_{\mathsf{k}\Omega^{\mathbf{n}}}\otimes\rho_{\Omega,\mathbf{m}})\right)\circ\left(id_{\mathsf{k}R^{1}}\otimes\alpha_{{}_{\mathsf{k}\Omega^{\mathbf{n}},\mathsf{k}\Omega^{\mathbf{m}},\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Omega)}}\right)\circ\left(id_{\mathsf{k}R^{1}}\otimes(\phi_{\mathbf{n},\mathbf{m}}^{\Omega}\otimes id_{\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Omega)})\right)\circ

\displaystyle\alpha_{{}_{\mathsf{k}R^{1},\mathsf{k}\Omega^{\mathbf{n}+\mathbf{m}},\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Omega)}}\circ\left((id_{\mathsf{k}R^{1}}\otimes(\phi_{\mathbf{n},\mathbf{m}}^{\Omega})^{-1})\otimes id_{\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Omega)}\right)\circ\left(\alpha_{{}_{\mathsf{k}R^{1},\mathsf{k}\Omega^{\mathbf{n}},\mathsf{k}\Omega^{\mathbf{m}}}}\otimes id_{\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Omega)}\right)\circ

\displaystyle\left((\sigma_{\mathbf{n}}\theta_{\lambda}\otimes(id_{\mathsf{k}\Omega^{\mathbf{m}}})\otimes id_{\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Omega)}\right)\circ\left(\sigma_{\mathbf{m}}\otimes id_{\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Omega)}\right)\circ\left(\theta_{\mu}\otimes id_{\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Omega)}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(id_{\mathsf{k}R^{1}}\otimes\left(\rho_{\Omega,\mathbf{n}}\circ(id_{\mathsf{k}\Omega^{\mathbf{n}}}\otimes\rho_{\Omega,\mathbf{m}})\circ\alpha_{{}_{\mathsf{k}\Omega^{\mathbf{n}},\mathsf{k}\Omega^{\mathbf{m}},\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Omega)}}\circ(\phi_{\mathbf{n},\mathbf{m}}^{\Omega}\otimes id_{\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Omega)})\right)\right)\circ\alpha_{{}_{\mathsf{k}R^{1},\mathsf{k}\Omega^{\mathbf{n}+\mathbf{m}},\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Omega)}}\circ

\displaystyle\left((id_{\mathsf{k}R^{1}}\otimes(\phi_{\mathbf{n},\mathbf{m}}^{\Omega})^{-1})\otimes id_{\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Omega)}\right)\circ\left(\alpha_{{}_{\mathsf{k}R^{1},\mathsf{k}\Omega^{\mathbf{n}},\mathsf{k}\Omega^{\mathbf{m}}}}\otimes id_{\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Omega)}\right)\circ\left((\sigma_{\mathbf{n}}\otimes id_{\mathsf{k}\Omega^{\mathbf{m}}})\otimes id_{\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Omega)}\right)\circ

\displaystyle\overline{\left((\theta_{\lambda}\otimes id_{\mathsf{k}\Omega^{\mathbf{m}}})\otimes id_{\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Omega)}\right)\circ\left(\sigma_{\mathbf{m}}\otimes id_{\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Omega)}\right)\circ\left(\theta_{\mu}\otimes id_{\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Omega)}\right)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(id_{\mathsf{k}R^{1}}\otimes\rho_{\Omega,\mathbf{n}+\mathbf{m}}\right)\circ\alpha_{{}_{\mathsf{k}R^{1},\mathsf{k}\Omega^{\mathbf{n}+\mathbf{m}},\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Omega)}}\circ\left((id_{\mathsf{k}R^{1}}\otimes(\phi_{\mathbf{n},\mathbf{m}}^{\Omega})^{-1})\otimes id_{\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Omega)}\right)\circ\left(\alpha_{{}_{\mathsf{k}R^{1},\mathsf{k}\Omega^{\mathbf{n}},\mathsf{k}\Omega^{\mathbf{m}}}}\otimes id_{\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Omega)}\right)\circ

\displaystyle\left((\sigma_{\mathbf{n}}\otimes id_{\mathsf{k}\Omega^{\mathbf{m}}})\otimes id_{\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Omega)}\right)\circ{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\left(\alpha_{{}_{\mathsf{k}\Lambda^{\mathbf{n}},\mathsf{k}R^{1},\mathsf{k}\Omega^{\mathbf{m}}}}^{-1}\otimes id_{\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Omega)}\right)\circ\left((id_{\mathsf{k}\Lambda^{\mathbf{n}}}\otimes\sigma_{\mathbf{m}})\otimes id_{\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Omega)}\right)\circ}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\left(\alpha_{{}_{\mathsf{k}\Lambda^{\mathbf{n}},\mathsf{k}\Lambda^{\mathbf{m}},\mathsf{k}R^{1}}}\otimes id_{\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Omega)}\right)\circ\left((\phi_{\mathbf{n},\mathbf{m}}^{\Lambda}\otimes id_{\mathsf{k}R^{1}})\otimes id_{\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Omega)}\right)\circ\left(\theta_{\lambda\mu}\otimes id_{\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Omega)}\right)}~\text{(Using (\ref{comd2}) for $\Omega$)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(id_{\mathsf{k}R^{1}\otimes\rho_{\Omega,\mathbf{n}+\mathbf{m}}}\right)\circ\alpha_{{}_{\mathsf{k}R^{1},\mathsf{k}\Omega^{\mathbf{n}+\mathbf{m}},\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Omega)}}\circ\left(\sigma_{\mathbf{n}+\mathbf{m}}\otimes id_{\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Omega)}\right)\circ\left(\theta_{\lambda\mu}\otimes id_{\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Omega)}\right)~\text{(Using (\ref{comd4}))}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle S_{\lambda\mu}

\displaystyle S_{\mu^{*}}S_{\lambda^{*}}=

\displaystyle\left((\theta_{\mu^{*}}\otimes id_{\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Omega)})\circ(\sigma_{\mathbf{m}}^{-1}\otimes id_{\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Omega)})\circ\alpha_{{}_{\mathsf{k}R^{1},\mathsf{k}\Omega^{\mathbf{m}},\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Omega)}}\circ(id_{\mathsf{k}R^{1}}\otimes\rho_{\Omega,\mathbf{m}}^{-1})\right)\circ

\displaystyle\left((\theta_{\lambda^{*}}\otimes id_{\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Omega)})\circ(\sigma_{\mathbf{n}}^{-1}\otimes id_{\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Omega)})\circ\alpha_{{}_{\mathsf{k}R^{1},\mathsf{k}\Omega^{\mathbf{n}},\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Omega)}}\circ(id_{\mathsf{k}R^{1}}\otimes\rho_{\Omega,\mathbf{n}}^{-1})\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(\theta_{\mu^{*}}\otimes id_{\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Omega)}\right)\circ(\sigma_{\mathbf{m}}^{-1}\otimes id_{\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Omega)})\circ\alpha_{{}_{\mathsf{k}R^{1},\mathsf{k}\Omega^{\mathbf{m}},\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Omega)}}^{-1}\circ\left(\theta_{\lambda^{*}}\otimes\rho_{\Omega,\mathbf{m}}^{-1}\right)\circ\left(\sigma_{\mathbf{n}}^{-1}\otimes id_{\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Omega)}\right)\circ

\displaystyle\alpha_{{}_{\mathsf{k}R^{1},\mathsf{k}\Omega^{\mathbf{n}},\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Omega)}}^{-1}\circ\left(id_{\mathsf{k}R^{1}}\otimes\rho_{\Omega,\mathbf{n}}^{-1}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\overline{\left(\theta_{\mu^{*}}\otimes id_{\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Omega)}\right)\circ\left(\sigma_{\mathbf{m}}^{-1}\otimes id_{\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Omega)}\right)\circ\left((\theta_{\lambda^{*}}\otimes id_{\mathsf{k}\Omega^{\mathbf{m}}})\otimes id_{\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Omega)}\right)}\circ\alpha_{{}_{\mathsf{k}\Lambda^{\mathbf{n}}\otimes\mathsf{k}R^{1},\mathsf{k}\Omega^{\mathbf{m}},\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Omega)}}^{-1}\circ

\displaystyle\left((id_{\mathsf{k}\Lambda^{\mathbf{n}}}\otimes id_{\mathsf{k}R^{1}})\otimes\rho_{\Omega,\mathbf{m}}^{-1}\right)\circ\left(\sigma_{\mathbf{n}}^{-1}\otimes id_{\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Omega)}\right)\circ\alpha_{{}_{\mathsf{k}R^{1},\mathsf{k}\Omega^{\mathbf{n}},\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Omega)}}^{-1}\circ\left(id_{\mathsf{k}R^{1}}\otimes\rho_{\Omega,\mathbf{n}}^{-1}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\left(\theta_{(\lambda\mu)^{*}}\otimes id_{\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Omega)}\right)\circ\left(((\phi_{\mathbf{n},\mathbf{m}}^{\Lambda})^{-1}\otimes id_{\mathsf{k}R^{1}})\otimes id_{\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Omega)}\right)\circ\left(\alpha_{{}_{\mathsf{k}\Lambda^{\mathbf{n}},\mathsf{k}\Lambda^{\mathbf{m}},\mathsf{k}R^{1}}}^{-1}\otimes id_{\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Omega)}\right)\circ}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\left((id_{\mathsf{k}\Lambda^{\mathbf{n}}}\otimes\sigma_{\mathbf{m}}^{-1})\otimes id_{\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Omega)}\right)\circ\left(\alpha_{{}_{\mathsf{k}\Lambda^{\mathbf{n}},\mathsf{k}R^{1},\mathsf{k}\Omega^{\mathbf{m}}}}\otimes id_{\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Omega)}\right)}\circ\alpha_{{}_{\mathsf{k}\Lambda^{\mathbf{n}}\otimes\mathsf{k}R^{1},\mathsf{k}\Omega^{\mathbf{m}},\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Omega)}}^{-1}\circ\left((id_{\mathsf{k}\Lambda^{\mathbf{n}}}\otimes id_{\mathsf{k}R^{1}})\otimes\rho_{\Omega,\mathbf{m}}^{-1}\right)\circ

\displaystyle\left(\sigma_{\mathbf{n}}^{-1}\otimes id_{\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Omega)}\right)\circ\alpha_{{}_{\mathsf{k}R^{1},\mathsf{k}\Omega^{\mathbf{n}},\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Omega)}}^{-1}\circ\left(id_{\mathsf{k}R^{1}}\otimes\rho_{\Omega,\mathbf{n}}^{-1}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(\theta_{(\lambda\mu)^{*}}\otimes id_{\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Omega)}\right)\circ\left(((\phi_{\mathbf{n},\mathbf{m}}^{\Lambda})^{-1}\otimes id_{\mathsf{k}R^{1}})\otimes id_{\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Omega)}\right)\circ\left(\alpha_{{}_{\mathsf{k}\Lambda^{\mathbf{n}},\mathsf{k}\Lambda^{\mathbf{m}},\mathsf{k}R^{1}}}^{-1}\otimes id_{\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Omega)}\right)\circ

\displaystyle\left((id_{\mathsf{k}\Lambda^{\mathbf{n}}}\otimes\sigma_{\mathbf{m}}^{-1})\otimes id_{\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Omega)}\right)\circ\left(\alpha_{{}_{\mathsf{k}\Lambda^{\mathbf{n}},\mathsf{k}R^{1},\mathsf{k}\Omega^{\mathbf{m}}}}\otimes id_{\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Omega)}\right)\circ\alpha_{{}_{\mathsf{k}\Lambda^{\mathbf{n}}\otimes\mathsf{k}R^{1},\mathsf{k}\Omega^{\mathbf{m}},\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Omega)}}^{-1}\circ\left(\sigma_{\mathbf{n}}^{-1}\otimes(id_{\mathsf{k}\Omega^{\mathbf{m}}}\otimes id_{\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Omega)})\right)\circ

\displaystyle\left((id_{\mathsf{k}R^{1}}\otimes id_{\mathsf{k}\Omega^{\mathbf{n}}})\otimes\rho_{\Omega,\mathbf{m}}^{-1}\right)\circ\alpha_{{}_{\mathsf{k}R^{1},\mathsf{k}\Omega^{\mathbf{n}},\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Omega)}}^{-1}\circ\left(id_{\mathsf{k}R^{1}}\otimes\rho_{\Omega,\mathbf{n}}^{-1}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(\theta_{(\lambda\mu)^{*}}\otimes id_{\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Omega)}\right)\circ\left(((\phi_{\mathbf{n},\mathbf{m}}^{\Lambda})^{-1}\otimes id_{\mathsf{k}R^{1}})\otimes id_{\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Omega)}\right)\circ\left(\alpha_{{}_{\mathsf{k}\Lambda^{\mathbf{n}},\mathsf{k}\Lambda^{\mathbf{m}},\mathsf{k}R^{1}}}^{-1}\otimes id_{\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Omega)}\right)\circ

\displaystyle\left((id_{\mathsf{k}\Lambda^{\mathbf{n}}}\otimes\sigma_{\mathbf{m}}^{-1})\otimes id_{\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Omega)}\right)\circ\left(\alpha_{{}_{\mathsf{k}\Lambda^{\mathbf{n}},\mathsf{k}R^{1},\mathsf{k}\Omega^{\mathbf{m}}}}\otimes id_{\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Omega)}\right)\circ\left((\sigma_{\mathbf{n}}^{-1}\otimes id_{\mathsf{k}\Omega^{\mathbf{m}}})\otimes id_{\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Omega)}\right)\circ

\displaystyle\alpha_{{}_{\mathsf{k}R^{1}\otimes\mathsf{k}\Omega^{\mathbf{n}},\mathsf{k}\Omega^{\mathbf{m}},\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Omega)}}^{-1}\circ\alpha_{{}_{\mathsf{k}R^{1},\mathsf{k}\Omega^{\mathbf{n}},\mathsf{k}\Omega^{\mathbf{m}}\otimes\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Omega)}}^{-1}\circ\left(id_{\mathsf{k}R^{1}}\otimes(id_{\mathsf{k}\Omega^{\mathbf{n}}}\otimes\rho_{\Omega,\mathbf{m}}^{-1})\right)\circ\left(id_{\mathsf{k}R^{1}}\otimes\rho_{\Omega,\mathbf{n}}^{-1}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(\theta_{(\lambda\mu)^{*}}\otimes id_{\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Omega)}\right)\circ\left(((\phi_{\mathbf{n},\mathbf{m}}^{\Lambda})^{-1}\otimes id_{\mathsf{k}R^{1}})\otimes id_{\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Omega)}\right)\circ\left(\alpha_{{}_{\mathsf{k}\Lambda^{\mathbf{n}},\mathsf{k}\Lambda^{\mathbf{m}},\mathsf{k}R^{1}}}^{-1}\otimes id_{\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Omega)}\right)\circ

\displaystyle\left((id_{\mathsf{k}\Lambda^{\mathbf{n}}}\otimes\sigma_{\mathbf{m}}^{-1})\otimes id_{\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Omega)}\right)\circ\left(\alpha_{{}_{\mathsf{k}\Lambda^{\mathbf{n}},\mathsf{k}R^{1},\mathsf{k}\Omega^{\mathbf{m}}}}\otimes id_{\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Omega)}\right)\circ\left((\sigma_{\mathbf{n}}^{-1}\otimes id_{\mathsf{k}\Omega^{\mathbf{m}}})\otimes id_{\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Omega)}\right)\circ

\displaystyle\left(\alpha_{{}_{\mathsf{k}R^{1},\mathsf{k}\Omega^{\mathbf{n}},\mathsf{k}\Omega^{\mathbf{m}}}}^{-1}\otimes id_{\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Omega)}\right)\circ\left((id_{\mathsf{k}R^{1}}\otimes\phi_{\mathbf{n},\mathbf{m}}^{\Omega})\otimes id_{\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Omega)}\right)\circ\alpha_{{}_{\mathsf{k}R^{1},\mathsf{k}\Omega^{\mathbf{n}+\mathbf{m}},\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Omega)}}^{-1}\circ

\displaystyle\left(id_{\mathsf{k}R^{1}}\otimes((\phi_{\mathbf{n},\mathbf{m}}^{\Omega})^{-1}\otimes id_{\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Omega)})\right)\circ\left(id_{\mathsf{k}R^{1}}\otimes\alpha_{{}_{\mathsf{k}\Omega^{\mathbf{n}},\mathsf{k}\Omega^{\mathbf{m}},\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Omega)}}^{-1}\right)\circ\left(id_{\mathsf{k}R^{1}}\otimes(id_{\mathsf{k}\Omega^{\mathbf{n}}}\otimes\rho_{\Omega,\mathbf{m}}^{-1})\right)\circ\left(id_{\mathsf{k}R^{1}}\otimes\rho_{\Omega,\mathbf{n}}^{-1}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(\theta_{(\lambda\mu)^{*}}\otimes id_{\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Omega)}\right)\circ\left(\sigma_{\mathbf{n}+\mathbf{m}}^{-1}\otimes id_{\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Omega)}\right)\circ\alpha_{{}_{\mathsf{k}R^{1},\mathsf{k}\Omega^{\mathbf{n}+\mathbf{m}},\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Omega)}}^{-1}\circ\left(id_{\mathsf{k}R^{1}}\otimes\rho_{\Omega,\mathbf{n}+\mathbf{m}}^{-1}\right)~\text{(Using (\ref{comd3}) and (\ref{comd4}))}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle S_{(\lambda\mu)^{*}}

With this we have shown that
\{P_{v},S_{\lambda},S_{\mu^{*}}~|~v\in\Lambda^{0},\lambda,\mu\in\Lambda^{\neq 0}\}
is a Kumjian-Pask
\Lambda
-family in
\operatorname{End}_{\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Omega)}(M(R))
. Hence, by the universal property of
\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Lambda)
(see
[
8
, Theorem 3.4]
), there exists a unique
\mathsf{k}
-algebra homomorphism

\varphi:\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Lambda)\longrightarrow\operatorname{End}_{\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Omega)}(M(R))

such that

\varphi(p_{v})=P_{v}
,
\varphi(s_{\lambda})=S_{\lambda}
and
\varphi(s_{\mu^{*}})=S_{\mu^{*}}

for all
v\in\Lambda^{0}
and
\lambda,\mu\in\Lambda^{\neq 0}
. Now we can define a left
\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Lambda)
-action on
M(R)
as

w\cdot x:=\varphi(w)(x)

for all
w\in\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Lambda)
and
x\in M(R)
. As each
\varphi(w)
is a
\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Omega)
-module endomorphism, the left
\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Lambda)
-action and the right
\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Omega)
-action commute, which makes
M(R)
a
\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Lambda)-\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Omega)
-bimodule as desired.

For each
\mathbf{n}\in\mathbb{N}^{k}
, set

M(R)_{\mathbf{n}}:=\mathsf{k}R^{1}\otimes_{\mathsf{k}\Omega^{0}}(\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Omega))_{\mathbf{n}}.

Clearly the above grading makes
M(R)
, a graded right
\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Omega)
-module.

Any simple tensor in
M(R)_{\mathbf{n}}
can be written as
x\otimes w
where
x\in\mathsf{k}R^{1}
and
w\in(\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Omega))_{\mathbf{n}}
. Then

P_{v}(x\otimes w)=\left(\mu_{v}\otimes id_{\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Omega)}\right)(x\otimes w)=vx\otimes w\in M(R)_{\mathbf{n}},

for all
v\in\Lambda^{0}
. Again

\displaystyle S_{\lambda}(x\otimes w)=

\displaystyle\left(\left(id_{\mathsf{k}R^{1}}\otimes\rho_{\Omega,d(\lambda)}\right)\circ\alpha_{{}_{\mathsf{k}R^{1},\mathsf{k}\Omega^{d(\lambda)},\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Omega)}}\circ\left((\sigma_{d(\lambda)}\circ\theta_{\lambda})\otimes id_{\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Omega)}\right)\right)(x\otimes w)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(\left(id_{\mathsf{k}R^{1}}\otimes\rho_{\Omega,d(\lambda)}\right)\circ\alpha_{{}_{\mathsf{k}R^{1},\mathsf{k}\Omega^{d(\lambda)},\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Omega)}}\right)\left(\displaystyle{\sum_{j=1}^{t}}(y_{j}\otimes\mu_{j})\otimes w\right)

\displaystyle~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~(\text{Assuming}~\sigma_{d(\lambda)}(\lambda\otimes x)=\displaystyle{\sum_{j=1}^{t}}(y_{j}\otimes\mu_{j})\in\mathsf{k}R^{1}\otimes_{\mathsf{k}\Omega^{0}}\mathsf{k}\Omega^{d(\lambda)})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(id_{\mathsf{k}R^{1}}\otimes\rho_{\Omega,d(\lambda)}\right)\left(\displaystyle{\sum_{j=1}^{t}}y_{j}\otimes(\mu_{j}\otimes w)\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\displaystyle{\sum_{j=1}^{t}}y_{j}\otimes\pi(\mu_{j})w\in M(R)_{d(\lambda)+\mathbf{n}}

for all
\lambda\in\Lambda^{\neq 0}
.

Similarly,
S_{\mu^{*}}(x\otimes w)\in M(R)_{\mathbf{n}-d(\mu)}
for all
\mu\in\Lambda^{\neq 0}
. This shows that
P_{v},S_{\lambda}
and
S_{\mu^{*}}
are graded
\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Omega)
-module endomorphisms of degree
0,d(\lambda)
and
-d(\mu)
respectively. Thus the homomorphism

\varphi:\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Lambda)\longrightarrow\displaystyle{\bigoplus_{\mathbf{n}\in\mathbb{Z}^{k}}}\left(\operatorname{End}_{\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Omega)}(M(R))\right)_{\mathbf{n}}\subseteq\operatorname{End}_{\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Omega)}(M(R))

preserves degrees of generators and hence a graded
\mathsf{k}
-algebra homomorphism. Consequently,

M(R)=\displaystyle{\bigoplus_{\mathbf{n}\in\mathbb{Z}^{k}}}M(R)_{\mathbf{n}}

becomes a graded left
\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Lambda)
-module.

6.3.
A sufficient condition for lifting

The graded
\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Lambda)-\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Omega)
-bimodule
M(R)
, obtained in the previous subsection, gives rise to the tensor product functor

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{M(R)}:=-\otimes_{\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Lambda)}M(R):\operatorname{\textbf{Gr}}\!-\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Lambda)

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\operatorname{\textbf{Gr}}\!-\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Omega).

Lemma 6.10
.

Suppose
R
is a bridging matrix for the
k
-graphs
\Lambda
and
\Omega
. The functor
\mathcal{F}_{M(R)}
induces an order preserving
\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}^{k}]
-module homomorphism

\displaystyle K_{0}^{\operatorname{gr}}(\mathcal{F}_{M(R)}):K_{0}^{\operatorname{gr}}(\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Lambda))\longrightarrow K_{0}^{\operatorname{gr}}(\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Omega))

such that
K_{0}^{\operatorname{gr}}(\mathcal{F}_{M(R)})([P])=[P\otimes_{\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Lambda)}M(R)]
for all graded projective right
\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Lambda)
-module
P
and
\mathfrak{h}_{R}=K_{0}^{\operatorname{gr}}(\mathcal{F}_{M(R)})
, where
\mathfrak{h}_{R}
is the unique module homomorphism mentioned in Lemma
5.3

(i)
.

Proof.

We first define a
\mathbb{Z}^{k}
-monoid homomorphism from
\mathcal{V}^{\operatorname{gr}}(\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Lambda))
to
\mathcal{V}^{\operatorname{gr}}(\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Omega))
. Since
T_{\Lambda}\cong\mathcal{V}^{\operatorname{gr}}(\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Lambda))
via the
\mathbb{Z}^{k}
-monoid isomorphism
v(\mathbf{n})\longmapsto[(p_{v}\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Lambda))(-\mathbf{n})]
and
v(\mathbf{n})
’s are generators of
T(\Lambda)
, it suffices to define the images of the graded projective right
\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Lambda)
-modules of the form
(p_{v}\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Lambda))(\mathbf{n})
where
v\in\Lambda^{0}
and
\mathbf{n}\in\mathbb{Z}^{k}
. Define
\mathcal{V}^{\operatorname{gr}}(\mathcal{F}_{M(R)}):\mathcal{V}^{\operatorname{gr}}(\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Lambda))\longrightarrow\mathcal{V}^{\operatorname{gr}}(\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Omega))
by

\mathcal{V}^{\operatorname{gr}}(\mathcal{F}_{M(R)})\left([(p_{v}\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Lambda))(\mathbf{n})]\right):=[(p_{v}\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Lambda))(\mathbf{n})\otimes_{\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Lambda)}M(R)].

Note that

\displaystyle(p_{v}\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Lambda))(\mathbf{n})\otimes_{\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Lambda)}M(R)\cong

\displaystyle\left(\mathsf{k}v\otimes_{\mathsf{k}\Lambda^{0}}\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Lambda)(\mathbf{n})\right)\otimes_{\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Lambda)}M(R)

\displaystyle\cong

\displaystyle~\mathsf{k}v\otimes_{\mathsf{k}\Lambda^{0}}\left(\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Lambda)(\mathbf{n})\otimes_{\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Lambda)}M(R)\right)

\displaystyle\cong

\displaystyle~\mathsf{k}v\otimes_{\mathsf{k}\Lambda^{0}}M(R)(\mathbf{n}),

where the grading of
\mathsf{k}v
is concentrated in degree zero and each isomorphism is a graded module isomorphism of graded right
\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Omega)
-modules. For any
g\in R^{1}
, the assignment
(g\otimes x)\longmapsto p_{s(g)}x
extends to a graded right
\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Omega)
-module isomorphism from
\mathsf{k}g\otimes_{\mathsf{k}\Omega^{0}}\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Omega)
to
p_{s(g)}\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Omega)
. Therefore,

\displaystyle\mathsf{k}v\otimes_{\mathsf{k}\Lambda^{0}}M(R)(\mathbf{n})\cong

\displaystyle~\mathsf{k}v\otimes_{\mathsf{k}\Lambda^{0}}\left(\mathsf{k}R^{1}\otimes_{\mathsf{k}\Omega^{0}}\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Omega)(\mathbf{n})\right)

\displaystyle\cong

\displaystyle~\left(\mathsf{k}v\otimes_{\mathsf{k}\Lambda^{0}}\mathsf{k}R^{1}\right)\otimes_{\mathsf{k}\Omega^{0}}\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Omega)(\mathbf{n})

\displaystyle\cong

\displaystyle~\left(\displaystyle{\bigoplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}w\in\Omega^{0}\\
R(v,w)\neq 0\end{subarray}}}\left(\displaystyle{\bigoplus_{i=1}^{R(v,w)}}\mathsf{k}g_{i}^{v,w}\right)\right)\otimes_{\mathsf{k}\Omega^{0}}\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Omega)(\mathbf{n})

\displaystyle\cong

\displaystyle~\displaystyle{\bigoplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}w\in\Omega^{0}\\
R(v,w)\neq 0\end{subarray}}}\left(\displaystyle{\bigoplus_{i=1}^{R(v,w)}}(\mathsf{k}g_{i}^{v,w}\otimes_{\mathsf{k}\Omega^{0}}\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Omega)(\mathbf{n}))\right)

\displaystyle\cong

\displaystyle~\displaystyle{\bigoplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}w\in\Omega^{0}\\
R(v,w)\neq 0\end{subarray}}}(p_{w}\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Omega))(\mathbf{n})^{R(v,w)}

and consequently
\mathcal{V}^{\operatorname{gr}}(\mathcal{F}_{M(R)})\left([(p_{v}\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Lambda))(\mathbf{n})]\right)=\displaystyle{\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}w\in\Omega^{0}\\
R(v,w)\neq 0\end{subarray}}}R(v,w)[(p_{w}\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Omega))(\mathbf{n})]
. This description shows that
\mathcal{V}^{\operatorname{gr}}(\mathcal{F}_{M(R)})
is a well-defined monoid homomorphism between the graded
\mathcal{V}
-monoids. Evidently, it respects the
\mathbb{Z}^{k}
-action and hence induces an order-preserving
\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}^{k}]
-module homomorphism

K_{0}^{\operatorname{gr}}(\mathcal{F}_{M(R)}):K_{0}^{\operatorname{gr}}(\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Lambda))\longrightarrow K_{0}^{\operatorname{gr}}(\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Omega)),

via the group completion functor. Clearly
K_{0}^{\operatorname{gr}}(\mathcal{F}_{M(R)})([P])=[P\otimes_{\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Lambda)}M(R)]
for all graded projective right
\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Lambda)
-module
P
. Now

\displaystyle\gamma_{\Omega}\left(K_{0}^{\operatorname{gr}}(\mathcal{F}_{M(R)})([(p_{v}\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Lambda))(\mathbf{n})])\right)=

\displaystyle\gamma_{\Omega}\left(\displaystyle{\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}w\in\Omega^{0}\\
R(v,w)\neq 0\end{subarray}}}R(v,w)[(p_{w}\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Omega))(\mathbf{n})]\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\displaystyle{\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}w\in\Omega^{0}\\
R(v,w)\neq 0\end{subarray}}}[R(v,w)\epsilon_{w},-\mathbf{n}]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle[\displaystyle{\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}w\in\Omega^{0}\\
R(v,w)\neq 0\end{subarray}}}R(v,w)\epsilon_{w},-\mathbf{n}]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle[\epsilon_{v}R,-\mathbf{n}]=R\left([\epsilon_{v},-\mathbf{n}]\right)=R\left(\gamma_{\Lambda}([(p_{v}\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Lambda))(\mathbf{n})])\right).

Thus by uniqueness of the map
\mathfrak{h}_{R}
(see Lemma
5.3

(i)
), it follows that
\mathfrak{h}_{R}=K_{0}^{\operatorname{gr}}(\mathcal{F}_{M(R)})
.
∎

Definition 6.11
.

Suppose
\Lambda
and
\Omega
are row-finite
k
-graphs without sources such that
|\Lambda^{0}|,|\Omega^{0}|<\infty
. A morphism
\mathfrak{h}\in\mathbb{P}_{\mathbb{Z}^{k}}\left(K_{0}^{\operatorname{gr}}(\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Lambda)),K_{0}^{\operatorname{gr}}(\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Omega))\right)
is called a
bridging homomorphism
if the matrix
R
obtained in Lemma
5.3

(ii)
is a bridging matrix for
\Lambda
and
\Omega
(see Definition
6.5
).

In the following theorem, we show that any bridging homomorphism between graded
K
-theories of Kumjian-Pask algebras can be lifted to a graded homomorphism between the algebras.

Theorem 6.12
.

Suppose
\Lambda
and
\Omega
are row-finite
k
-graphs without sources such that
|\Lambda^{0}|,|\Omega^{0}|<\infty
. Let
\mathfrak{h}:K_{0}^{\operatorname{gr}}(\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Lambda))\longrightarrow K_{0}^{\operatorname{gr}}(\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Omega))
be any bridging homomorphism. Then there exists a unital graded homomorphism
\psi:\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Lambda)\longrightarrow\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Omega)
such that
K_{0}^{\operatorname{gr}}(\psi)=\mathfrak{h}
.

Proof.

By Lemma
5.3

(ii)
, there exists a matrix
R\in\mathbb{M}_{\Lambda^{0}\times\Omega^{0}}(\mathbb{N})
and a
k
-tuple
\mathbf{r}\in\mathbb{Z}^{k}
such that
\mathfrak{h}(w)=~^{\mathbf{r}}\mathfrak{h}_{R}(w)
for all
w\in K_{0}^{\operatorname{gr}}(\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Lambda))
. Since
\mathfrak{h}
is a bridging homomorphism,
R
is a bridging matrix for
\Lambda
and
\Omega
whence
\mathfrak{h}_{R}=K_{0}^{\operatorname{gr}}(\mathcal{F}_{M(R)})
by Lemma
6.10
. In view of the description of the map
\mathcal{V}^{\operatorname{gr}}(\mathcal{F}_{M(R)})
, one can easily verify that for each
w\in K_{0}^{\operatorname{gr}}(\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Lambda))
,

~{}^{\mathbf{r}}K_{0}^{\operatorname{gr}}(\mathcal{F}_{M(R)})(w)=K_{0}^{\operatorname{gr}}(\mathcal{F}_{M(R)(-\mathbf{r})})(w).

Therefore
\mathfrak{h}=K_{0}^{\operatorname{gr}}(\mathcal{F}_{M(R)(-\mathbf{r})})
. Since
\mathfrak{h}
is pointed so

[M(R)(-\mathbf{r})]=[\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Lambda)\otimes_{\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Lambda)}M(R)(-\mathbf{r})]=K_{0}^{\operatorname{gr}}(\mathcal{F}_{M(R)(-\mathbf{r})})([\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Lambda)])=[\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Omega)]

in
K_{0}^{\operatorname{gr}}(\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Omega))
. Hence,
M(R)(-\mathbf{r})
and
\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Omega)
are isomorphic as graded right
\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Omega)
-modules. Finally, by
[
13
, Proposition 3.1]
, there exists a unital graded homomorphism
\psi:\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Lambda)\longrightarrow\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Omega)
such that
K_{0}^{\operatorname{gr}}(\psi)=K_{0}^{\operatorname{gr}}(\mathcal{F}_{M(R)(-\mathbf{r})})=\mathfrak{h}
.
∎

Remark 6.13
.

The lifting result for Leavitt path algebras is perfectly generalised in Theorem
6.12
. The matrix
R
of any pointed order-preserving
\mathbb{Z}[x^{-1},x]
-module homomorphism between graded Grothendieck groups of Leavitt path algebras is trivially a bridging matrix. As a result, the lifting can always be done in the level of
1
-graphs and consequently
K_{0}^{\operatorname{gr}}
is a full functor.

7.
Further directions

Given two finite directed graphs
E
and
F
, the Graded Classification Conjecture (
[
19
,
27
]
) states that the Leavitt path algebras
L_{\mathsf{k}}(E)
and
L_{\mathsf{k}}(F)
, with coefficient field
\mathsf{k}
, are graded isomorphic if and only if there is an order preserving
\mathbb{Z}[x^{-1},x]
-module isomorphism

\phi:K_{0}^{\operatorname{gr}}(L_{\mathsf{k}}(E))\longrightarrow K_{0}^{\operatorname{gr}}(L_{\mathsf{k}}(F))

such that
\phi([L_{\mathsf{k}}(E)])=[L_{\mathsf{k}}(F)]
(equivalently, if there exists a
\mathbb{Z}
-monoid isomorphism between
T_{E}
and
T_{F}
which preserves the order units). So far, no counterexample has been found contradicting the conjecture. However in the case of higher-rank graphs, we found the following example showing that the graded
K
-theory (or equivalently the talented monoid), in general, may fail to classify Kumjian-Pask algebras up to graded isomorphism.

Example 7.1
.

Consider the following coloured directed graph:

.
v
e_{1}
e_{2}
f

We can obtain two non-isomorphic
2
-graphs by setting two different factorization rules for the bicoloured paths in the above directed graph.

(I)
The
2
-graph
\Lambda_{1}
: We define the factorization rules as follows:

e_{1}f=fe_{1},~~e_{2}f=fe_{2}.

(II)
The
2
-graph
\Lambda_{2}
: Here the factorization rules are defined as:

e_{1}f=fe_{2},~~e_{2}f=fe_{1}.

The defining relations for both the talented monoids
T_{\Lambda_{1}}
and
T_{\Lambda_{2}}
are the same:

v((i,j))=v((i+1,j))=2v((i,j+1));~(i,j)\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}.

Consequently, the map
v((i,j))\longmapsto v((i,j))
is a pointed
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
-isomorphism between
T_{\Lambda_{1}}
and
T_{\Lambda_{2}}
are. In fact
T_{\Lambda_{1}}\cong T_{\Lambda_{2}}\cong\mathbb{N}[\frac{1}{2}]
(see Example
3.5
).

Let
\mathsf{k}
be any field. We denote the Kumjian-Pask families in
\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Lambda_{1})
and
\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Lambda_{2})
by
(p,s)
and
(P,S)
respectively.

Note that if
\mathcal{A}
is the subalgebra of
\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Lambda_{2})
generated by
S_{e_{1}},S_{e_{2}},S_{e_{1}^{*}},S_{e_{2}^{*}}
, then
\mathcal{A}\cong L_{\mathsf{k}}(1,2)
as
\mathsf{k}
-algebras. Setting
e_{1}
as a special edge emanating from
v
, we have a basis for the
\mathsf{k}
-vector space
\mathcal{A}
consisting of the following elements:

•

P_{v}
;

•

S_{\lambda},S_{\lambda^{*}}
where
\lambda\in\Lambda_{2}
and
d(\lambda)=(0,n)
for some
n\in\mathbb{N}\setminus\{0\}
;

•

S_{\lambda}S_{\mu^{*}}
where
s(\lambda)=s(\mu)
,
\lambda,\mu\in\Lambda_{2}
,
d(\lambda)=(0,n)
,
d(\mu)=(0,m)
for some
n,m\in\mathbb{N}\setminus\{0\}
and if
\lambda=\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}\cdots\alpha_{n}
,
\mu=\beta_{1}\beta_{2}\cdots\beta_{m}
,
\alpha_{i},\beta_{j}\in\{e_{1},e_{2}\}
then either
\alpha_{n}\neq\beta_{m}
or
\alpha_{n}=\beta_{m}
and that common edge is not
e_{1}
.

We observe that there can not exist any
\mathbb{Z}^{k}
-graded ring isomorphism between
\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Lambda_{1})
and
\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Lambda_{2})
. In
\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Lambda_{1})
, using relations
(KP2)
(see Definition
2.5
), we have
s_{f^{*}}s_{e_{1}^{*}}=s_{e_{1}^{*}}s_{f^{*}}
which upon multiplying from both sided by
s_{f}
yields
s_{e_{1}^{*}}s_{f}=s_{f}s_{e_{1}^{*}}
. A similar argument shows that
s_{e_{2}^{*}}s_{f}=s_{f}s_{e_{2}^{*}}
and in
\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Lambda_{2})
,
S_{e_{1}^{*}}S_{f}=S_{f}S_{e_{2}^{*}}
,
S_{e_{2}^{*}}S_{f}=S_{f}S_{e_{1}^{*}}
. It follows that
s_{f}\in Z(\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Lambda_{1}))
whereas
S_{f}\notin Z(\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Lambda_{2}))
. If we have a graded isomorphism
\phi:\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Lambda_{1})\longrightarrow\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Lambda_{2})
then it is not hard to see that
\phi(s_{f})
should be of the form
S_{f}x
where

x=\displaystyle{\sum_{i=1}^{t}}a_{i}S_{\lambda_{i}}S_{\mu_{i}^{*}},

a_{i}\in\mathsf{k}\setminus\{0\}
,
d(\lambda_{i})=d(\mu_{i})=(0,n_{i})
,
n_{i}\in\mathbb{N}
for all
i=1,2,...,t
. Clearly
x\in\mathcal{A}\setminus\{0\}
. Since
s_{f}\in Z(\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Lambda_{1}))
we should have
S_{f}x\in Z(\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Lambda_{2}))
. Assume that
x
is in normal form (if not then we can easily transform those monomials which are not in the basis of
\mathcal{A}
to express
x
as a linear combination of basic vectors and all the monomials of such a basic representation satisfy the same condition which is satisfied by the monomials of the starting representation). Now two cases may appear.

Case-I:

\mu_{i}(0,(0,1))=e_{2}
for some
i
. Then
\mu_{i}=e_{2}\gamma
for some
\gamma\in\Lambda_{2}
with
d(\gamma)=(0,n_{i}-1)
. Observe that if a monomial
S_{\lambda}S_{\mu^{*}}(\neq P_{v})
is in normal form (i.e., the first edges of
\lambda
and
\mu
either do not coincide or if they do then the common first edge is not
e_{1}
) and we multiply it by
S_{e_{1}}
(or
S_{e_{2}}
) from left or right then the resulting element also remains in normal form. Now

xS_{e_{1}}=a_{i}S_{\lambda_{i}}S_{\mu_{i}^{*}}S_{e_{1}}+\displaystyle{\sum_{j\neq i}}a_{j}S_{\lambda_{j}}S_{\mu_{j}^{*}}S_{e_{1}}=a_{i}S_{\lambda_{i}}S_{\gamma^{*}}S_{e_{2}^{*}}S_{e_{1}}+\displaystyle{\sum_{j\neq i}}a_{j}S_{\lambda_{j}}S_{\mu_{j}^{*}}S_{e_{1}}=\displaystyle{\sum_{j\neq i}}a_{j}S_{\lambda_{j}}S_{\mu_{j}^{*}}S_{e_{1}}.

Thus the number of basic vectors in
xS_{e_{1}}
is less than
t
whereas the number of basic vectors in
S_{e_{2}}x
is still
t
. Thus
S_{e_{2}}x\neq xS_{e_{1}}
which implies
S_{e_{1}}S_{f}x\neq S_{f}xS_{e_{1}}
, a contradiction to the fact that
S_{f}x\in Z(\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Lambda_{2}))
.

Case-II:

\mu_{i}(0,(0,1))=e_{1}
for all
i
with
d(\mu_{i})\neq 0
. Clearly
x\notin\mathsf{k}P_{v}
, otherwise
\phi(s_{f})=cS_{f}
for some
c\in\mathsf{k}
and then
\phi(s_{f})\notin Z(\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Lambda_{2}))
, as
S_{e_{1}}S_{f}=S_{f}S_{e_{2}}\neq S_{f}S_{e_{1}}
. Now
xS_{e_{2}}=0
if
\mu_{i}(0,(0,1))=e_{1}
for all
i
and
xS_{e_{2}}\in\mathsf{k}S_{e_{2}}
if
S_{\lambda_{i}}=S_{\mu_{i}}=P_{v}
for some
i
, in which case, obviously
t\geq 2
. So if
xS_{e_{2}}\neq 0
the number of summand in the basic representation of
xS_{e_{2}}
reduces to
1
which is less than
t
. On the other hand,
S_{e_{1}}x\neq 0
and the number of basic vectors in
S_{e_{1}}x
is
t
. Therefore
S_{e_{1}}x\neq xS_{e_{2}}
and so
S_{e_{2}}\phi(s_{f})\neq\phi(s_{f})S_{e_{2}}
which shows
\phi(s_{f})=S_{f}x\notin Z(\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Lambda_{2}))
, the same contradiction.

In fact, it can be shown that
\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Lambda_{1})
is graded isomorphic to
L_{\mathsf{k}}(1,2)[t^{-1},t]
, the ring of Laurent polynomials with coefficients from
L_{\mathsf{k}}(1,2)
whereas
\operatorname{KP}_{\mathsf{k}}(\Lambda_{2})
is graded isomorphic with the skew Laurent polynomial ring
L_{\mathsf{k}}(1,2)[t^{-1},t;\varphi]
where
\varphi:L_{\mathsf{k}}(1,2)\longrightarrow L_{\mathsf{k}}(1,2)
is the ring automorphism switching
e_{1}
and
e_{2}
. The readers should be convinced that the
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
-grading on the Laurent polynomial (and the skew Laurent polynomial) ring is defined by setting

L_{\mathsf{k}}(1,2)[t^{-1},t]_{n}:=\operatorname{span}_{\mathsf{k}}\{xt^{i}~|~x\in L_{\mathsf{k}}(1,2)^{h},i\in\mathbb{Z};~\iota_{2}(deg(x))+(i,0)=n\},

where
deg(x)
is the degree of
x
in
L_{\mathsf{k}}(1,2)
when we view it as the Leavitt path algebra
L_{\mathsf{k}}(R_{2})
with the usual
\mathbb{Z}
-grading and
\iota_{2}:\mathbb{Z}\longrightarrow\mathbb{Z}^{2}
is the standard injection sending
j
to
(0,j)
.

The above example shows that the factorization of paths, which may affect the algebraic structure of Kumjian-Pask algebra, is not well captured by the graded
K
-theory. In the
1
-graph case, factorization of paths into edges is always unique and hence the above problem does not appear. However, this significant difference between
1
-graphs and general
k
-graphs makes the investigation of graded
K
-theory (or, talented monoid) in line with the classification of Kumjian-Pask algebras more interesting. To be precise, we state the following problem.

Problem 7.2
.

Characterise the class of
k
-graphs for which the existence of a pointed
\mathbb{Z}^{k}
-isomorphism between talented monoids imply that the respective Kumjian-Pask algebras are graded isomorphic.

We remark that the above problem is very general. One may work in a particular dimension. For example, if
k=1
, solving the problem will settle the Graded Classification Conjecture; as it says that the path categories of all finite directed graphs form the required class. For
k=2
, it is interesting to know whether the relevant class of
2
-graphs can be characterised by means of bicoloured skeletons. Obviously, the bicoloured graph in Example
7.1
is a forbidden skeleton in such a plausible characterization.

In Theorem
6.12
, we obtain a sufficient condition (via bridging matrix) for lifting homomorphisms between graded
K
-theories of Kumjian-Pask algebras to graded homomorphisms between the Kumjian-Pask algebras. However, we have not yet explored whether the condition is necessary. If it is so then obviously not all pointed order-preserving homomorphisms between graded Grothendieck groups can be lifted (one can easily find such a homomorphism in view of Example
6.7
). On the level of Leavitt path algebras, this lifting can always be done as the functor
K_{0}^{\operatorname{gr}}
is full. One way of showing this, among others, is to realise Leavitt path algebras as
Bergman algebras
(see
[
31
]
for details). So, in order to have a more specific answer to the lifting question (Question
6.1
) for Kumjian-Pask algebras, it may be fruitful to execute further research in the following direction.

Problem 7.3
.

Describe the class of
k
-graphs whose Kumjian-Pask algebras can be realised as Bergman algebras.
